# Sūrah al-Isrāʾ (Chapter 17) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Journey That Named the Chapter**

Sūrah al-Isrāʾ takes its title from its very first word-cluster: the nocturnal journey (*al-isrāʾ*) of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ from the Sacred Mosque in Makkah to the Farthest Mosque in Jerusalem. The event is dated by most authorities to approximately the year 619 CE — the closing phase of the Makkan mission, in the sorrow-drenched period the biographers call *ʿĀm al-Ḥuzn*, the Year of Sorrow, when the Prophet ﷺ lost both Khadījah, his wife of twenty-five years, and Abū Ṭālib, the uncle whose protection had sheltered him from Quraysh. It is not a coincidence of chronology that the greatest honor ever conferred upon a human being came immediately after the greatest human loss. When every earthly support is withdrawn, God demonstrates that the support that matters was never the earthly one.

**Its Other Names and Why They Matter**

The sūrah carries at least three names in the classical sources. It is *al-Isrāʾ* after the journey. It is *Subḥān*, after the opening word, following the widespread Arab practice of naming a sūrah by its first distinctive term. And it is *Sūrat Banī Isrāʾīl* — the Sūrah of the Children of Israel — because verses 2–8 open with a compressed philosophy of Israelite history: two episodes of corruption, two devastations, and an open door of mercy thereafter. That triad of names is itself a map of the chapter’s contents: a *glorification* of God that begins with an act of transcendent power, a *historical case-study* in how communities rise and fall under divine law, and a *journey* that re-centers the whole prophetic tradition around one servant.

**Makkan Setting, Makkan Themes**

The overwhelming majority of commentators regard the whole sūrah as Makkan, with a minority assigning verses 73 and 76, and sometimes 60, 74–75, 80, and 107, to Madinah. The Makkan fingerprint is unmistakable throughout: God’s power displayed in the natural order (vv. 66–70), repeated warnings of punishment in this world and the next (vv. 13–18, 58–59, 71–72), the refutation of those who ascribe partners and daughters to God (vv. 39–41), the obstinacy of those who refuse the Qur’an (vv. 45–48), the demand for spectacular miracles as the price of belief (vv. 90–93), and the denial of resurrection (vv. 49–52, 97–99). Interwoven with all of this is a remarkable moral code (vv. 22–39) — the nearest thing in the Qur’an to a Decalogue — and an instruction in prayer (vv. 78–79).

**A Chapter the Prophet ﷺ Recited Every Night**

Al-Bukhārī records in his Ṣaḥīḥ (5014) from ʿĀʾishah that the Prophet ﷺ would not sleep until he recited Banī Isrāʾ (Sūrah al-Isrāʾ) and al-Zumar — that is, he recited this chapter nightly as part of his devotion before sleeping. ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd said of it, together with al-Kahf, Maryam, Ṭāhā and al-Anbiyāʾ:

> *“They are among the earliest chapters I learned, and they are from the muʿaffaqāt (or: the ʿitāq al-awwal, the ancient foremost ones) — and they are among what I would teach first.”* (al-Bukhārī 4994)

The Companions’ instinct was that this chapter contains the marrow of Makkan faith: God’s transcendence, the soul’s accountability, the discipline of the tongue and the hand, and the certainty of standing before one’s record.

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary below treats each of the 111 verses as an independent discourse in strict order. Where the source commentary groups verses — for example 2–3, 13–14, 45–46, 90–93, 107–109 — every verse is here separated and given its own complete treatment, as the workflow requires. Nothing has been shortened because the chapter is long. Each verse is quoted individually from its canonical text, then expanded through Arabic language and roots, cross-references with the actual Qur’anic wording, authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections named, the positions of al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAbbās and others where relevant, the logic of the teaching, and its bearing on ordinary human life today.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:1

> **Glory be to Him Who carried His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque, whose precincts We have blessed, that We might show him some of Our signs. Truly He is the Hearer, the Seer.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why the Verse Begins with Glorification and Not with Narrative**

The verse does not open with *wa* (“and”) or *idh* (“when”), nor with the name of the Prophet ﷺ. It opens with *subḥāna* — a verbal noun of *s-b-ḥ*, meaning to declare God free of every deficiency, exalted beyond every imperfection. The grammatical choice is deliberate and carries enormous theological weight. Before the Qur’an describes *what happened*, it establishes *how to receive it*: this is not a story to be evaluated by ordinary measures of distance, time and physical possibility. It is a declaration of God’s transcendence, and the miracle that follows is offered as *evidence* of that transcendence. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *subḥāna* is used at points where God’s power or majesty is displayed in a way that human expectation would find improbable — as in 19:35, *“Glory be to God that He should take a son,”* and 16:1, *“Glory be to Him who sent down the Criterion upon His servant.”* The word functions as a protective frame around the claim.

**The Honorific Choice: “His Servant”**

Of every title available — Muhammad, the Messenger, the Prophet, the Warner — the verse selects *bi-ʿabdihi*, “His servant.” This is the single most theologically significant word in the verse, and the commentators from Ibn ʿAbbās onward have drawn attention to it. At the moment of the greatest exaltation ever granted to a created being, the Qur’an fixes him at the rank of servitude, because servitude (*ʿubūdiyyah*) is the highest station a creature can occupy, not the lowest. It also forecloses the exact error the Christians made concerning Jesus: elevation is never a transfer of divinity. The one who rose to the Throne went as *ʿabd*, and returned as *ʿabd*. Ibn Taymiyyah makes this a general principle: the two things the Prophet ﷺ is praised for are perfect servitude and perfect conveyance of the message, and the opening of this verse establishes the first.

**Asrā bi-ʿabdihi laylan — The Doubling That Emphasizes the Impossible**

The verb *asrā* already means “he traveled by night”; the Arabic root *s-r-y* carries night-travel within it. Yet the Qur’an adds the accusative of time *laylan*, “by night” — a redundancy in ordinary speech that becomes emphatic here. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī both observe that the doubling signals that a journey which caravans accomplished in roughly a month, or in a substantial portion of one, was completed within a fraction of a single night. Some reports add the detail that the door through which he ﷺ left was still swinging on its hinge when he returned. The point is not the physics but the sign: distance, for God, is not a constraint.

**Two Mosques, One Sanctified Geography**

*Min al-masjid al-ḥarām ilā al-masjid al-aqṣā* — “from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque.” The literal reading, preferred by al-Zamakhsharī, is that the departure point was the Sacred Mosque itself; a minority (mentioned by al-Ṭabarī) took it as the sacred precinct of Makkah generally. A ḥadīth reported in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (162) from Anas ibn Mālik supports the literal reading, describing the Prophet ﷺ lying in the *ḥijr* — the semi-circular enclosure beside the Kaʿbah — between sleeping and waking when Gabriel came to him.

*Al-masjid al-aqṣā*, “the Farthest Mosque,” denotes the site of the ancient Temple in Jerusalem — at the time of revelation an empty, ruinous platform, since the Temple had been destroyed by the Romans in 70 CE and nothing stood there. That the Qur’an should name a *mosque* at a location where no mosque existed, and call it “the Farthest,” is itself remarkable: it establishes the site as a place of prayer by divine designation rather than by human construction. Al-Ṭabarī records the view that it was called “the Farthest” because it was the farthest place of worship the Prophet ﷺ reached in that journey — the outermost point of the sanctified geography of the Abrahamic tradition.

**“Whose Precincts We Have Blessed”**

The relative clause *alladhī bāraknā ḥawlahu* — “which We have blessed around it” — extends the blessing from the sanctuary outward to its surroundings: the land of Jerusalem and the wider land of Canaan. Compare 7:137, *“And We made the people who were oppressed to inherit the eastern and western parts of the land which We had blessed,”* and 21:71, *“And We delivered him and Lot unto the land which We had blessed for all peoples.”* Al-Ṭabarī reports two streams of interpretation: some read the blessing as agricultural and material — fertility, olive groves, fruit — while al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī insist it is both worldly and spiritual, because it was the land of so many prophets, the place where revelation descended and where worship was performed across centuries.

The logic of joining the two mosques in one sentence is not incidental. It announces, before a single legal verse is revealed, that Islam is not a tribal religion starting from scratch. It is the inheritor and guardian of a chain: the House built by Abraham at Makkah and the House consecrated by Solomon at Jerusalem belong to one Lord, and the servant traveled between them in one night to make that unity visible.

**“That We Might Show Him Some of Our Signs”**

The stated purpose is *li-nuriyahu min āyātinā* — “so that We might show him some of Our signs.” The preposition *min* (“some of”) is significant: what the Prophet ﷺ witnessed was a *portion* of the divine signs, not their totality. No created being can exhaust the signs of the Infinite. Ibn Kathīr, in his commentary, gathers the reports of what was shown: the meeting with the prophets in the heavens, the vision of the Lote Tree of the Utmost Boundary (*sidrat al-muntahā*), the Garden, the Fire, and the nearness described at 53:9, *“then he drew near and descended, till he was two bows’ length away, or nearer.”* The journey had two axes, and the classical commentators distinguish them carefully: this verse records the *horizontal* movement, Makkah to Jerusalem (*isrāʾ*), while 53:1–18 alludes to the *vertical* movement, the ascent through the heavens (*miʿrāj*).

**The Physical or the Spiritual? The Weight of the Debate**

The majority of the Companions and of mainstream Islamic tradition held the journey to be bodily and waking, not a dream. Ibn Kathīr is emphatic on this, arguing that if it had been a mere vision there would have been no miracle to report and no reason for the Quraysh to find it incredible — yet the historical reports show they *did* find it incredible, and that several who had traveled to Jerusalem tested him by asking him to describe the city, which he did accurately. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record the minority view — attributed in some reports to ʿĀʾishah and Muʿāwiyah — that the journey was of the spirit. Later Sufi writers, notably in the tradition of Ibn ʿArabī, valued precisely this dimension, holding that spiritual ascent is a possibility open to the seeker even if the bodily miracle was unique to the Prophet ﷺ. The mainstream position remains that the *isrāʾ* and *miʿrāj* were a bodily miracle granted to the Prophet ﷺ alone, while the *spiritual* ascent of the believer occurs through prayer — hence the well-known saying recorded in the Sufi and devotional literature:

> *“The prayer (ṣalāh) is the miʿrāj of the believer.”*

This is a devotional aphorism rather than a Prophetic ḥadīth with an authenticated chain, and scholars have said so; its value lies in its meaning, not in its attribution.

**What the Journey Established Permanently**

Two lasting institutions trace to this night. First, the sanctity of Jerusalem for Muslims: the city became the first *qiblah*, the direction of prayer, for the community’s earliest years, until 2:144 redirected prayer toward the Sacred Mosque in 2 AH / 624 CE. Al-Bukhārī (444) and Muslim (827) record the Prophet’s ﷺ statement:

> *“Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and the Farthest Mosque.”*

Second, the five daily prayers in their final form were prescribed during the ascent, as reported in the long ḥadīth of al-Bukhārī (349) and Muslim (162) from Anas ibn Mālik, in which the number was reduced from fifty to five at Moses’ repeated advice, and God’s words were preserved in the narration: *“They are five, and they are fifty — My word is not changed.”*

**Why the Verse Ends with “the Hearer, the Seer”**

The closing pair *innahu huwa al-samīʿu al-baṣīr* is not decorative. A journey performed at night, unseen by any human witness, could raise the objection: who saw it? who can verify it? The answer is that God hears all and sees all — the event needs no human corroboration because its Witness is the One who arranged it. Al-Rāzī adds a subtler point: the pairing of Hearing and Seeing after the mention of *signs* teaches that the purpose of a miracle is not spectacle but the correction of hearing and sight — the two faculties by which a human being receives revelation and reads the world. The one who is granted the vision is the one who must then speak what he heard and show what he saw.

**The Night Journey and the Ordinary Believer**

The practical resonance of this verse is easy to miss because of its grandeur. A man who had just been stoned and mocked at Ṭāʾif, who had lost his wife and his protector, who had been refused by every tribe he approached — that man was taken to the farthest sanctuary and brought before the Throne. The pattern is one every believer recognizes: the moment of maximum earthly abandonment is often the threshold of divine nearness. The verse is recited in the *taḥiyyat al-masjid* of hearts that feel stranded, and the reason it comforts is not that God promises to transport us bodily, but that He demonstrated, once, in the most extreme case imaginable, that no distance separates a sincere servant from his Lord.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:2

> **And We gave unto Moses the Book, and We made it a guidance for the Children of Israel—“Take no guardian apart from Me”—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why the Chapter Pivots to Moses Immediately**

Having established the journey and the honor of the servant, the sūrah turns at once to the community most intimately connected with the destination of that journey. The choice is not random. The Night Journey linked Makkah to Jerusalem; Jerusalem was the city of the Israelite prophets and of the Temple. The Qur’an therefore moves directly to the Torah and to the covenant with the Children of Israel — the historical precedent against which the new community will be measured. Al-Rāzī observes that the whole of verses 2–8 functions as a single argument: *here is what happened to a people given the Book; take the lesson before you are given yours.*

**Al-Kitāb: The Torah Named as “The Book”**

The definite article in *al-Kitāb* here is a specific reference, not the generic “book.” Al-Ṭabarī states plainly that it means the Torah given to Moses. The Qur’an uses this designation elsewhere: *“Say, ‘Who sent down the Book that Moses brought as a light and guidance for mankind?’”* (6:91). The word *hudan* — guidance — is placed as the direct purpose of the giving: the Book was not an object of veneration but an instrument of direction. Guidance in Qur’anic usage is not information; it is orientation toward an end. A map is not guidance until it is held in the direction you are walking.

**“Take No Guardian Apart from Me”**

The phrase *an lā tattakhidhū min dūnī wakīlan* contains the pivotal term *wakīl*. A *wakīl* is a trustee, an agent, one to whom affairs are entrusted and on whom one relies — closer to “disposer of affairs” than to “guardian” in the modern custodial sense. The command is not merely “do not worship another”; it is “do not entrust your affairs to any other.” Al-Qurṭubī draws out the difference: worship is a matter of the tongue and the limbs, but *tawakkul* — reliance — is a matter of the heart, and it is the heart’s reliance that idolatry most often corrupts. A person may profess one God while actually depending on his wealth, his connections, his lawyer, or his reputation.

The Qur’an repeats this theme relentlessly:

> *“Do you not know that to God belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth, and that apart from God you have no guardian (waliyy) or helper?”* (*2:107)

. And in the verse of the Throne: *“God is the Guardian (waliyy) of those who believe”* (2:257). The logic is simple and unbending: whoever can truly dispose of your affairs must have power over outcomes; no creature has power over outcomes; therefore no creature can be your *wakīl*.

**The Grammar of the Quotation**

The verse breaks off with a dash in most renderings because the quoted command is grammatically suspended — it continues in the next verse with the description of the addressees. This is one of the Qur’an’s characteristic devices: a quotation begins, then the speaker inserts a description of the audience before completing the thought. The effect is to slow the reader down and force attention on *who* is being addressed. The children of the carried ones are being told, in effect: *you are descendants of survivors; remember whose hand saved your ancestors.*

**Why the Command Comes as a Quotation, Not a Statement**

By embedding the command in direct speech — *“Take no guardian apart from Me”* — the Qur’an reproduces the actual address of the Torah to Israel, not a report about it. This has a rhetorical consequence: the first-person pronoun *Me* makes the demand personal and immediate. Idolatry is not primarily an error of theory; it is a misplacement of trust. Every human being entrusts something to something. The verse asks the question every person must answer: to whom, in the last resort, do you hand your affairs?

**The Modern Form of “Taking a Guardian Apart from God”**

The verse reads as ancient polemic against statues, but its actual target is far more current. Modern people rarely bow to carved wood; they bow to markets, to career trajectories, to the opinions of strangers on screens, to a diagnosis, to a bank balance. The *wakīl* of the modern heart is whatever one checks first when anxious. The verse’s criterion is diagnostic and easy to apply: whatever you turn to before you turn to God has already been appointed your *wakīl*, whether or not you named it so. The command “Take no guardian apart from Me” is therefore less a prohibition of a ritual act than a demand for the re-ordering of the heart’s dependencies — and the Children of Israel, the people of the Book, are the first to be reminded of it precisely because possessing the Book does not by itself secure the heart’s reliance.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:3

> **the progeny of those whom We carried with Noah. Truly he was a thankful servant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Genealogical Reminder With a Purpose**

*Dhurriyyata man ḥamalnā maʿa Nūḥ* — “the offspring of those We carried with Noah.” The Children of Israel are addressed not only as the recipients of the Torah but as descendants of the passengers of the Ark.

The reminder is designed to trigger a specific memory: your ancestors were saved when everyone else drowned, and they were saved by nothing they had earned. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the mention serves to make them recall the favor (*niʿmah*) and therefore to keep the covenant — a rhetorical pattern the Qur’an uses constantly with Israel, as in 2:47:

> *“O Children of Israel, remember My favor which I bestowed upon you, and that I favored you above all peoples.”*

**The Verb Ḥamalnā: Carried, Not Merely Saved**

The root *ḥ-m-l* means to carry, to bear, to transport. God does not say “We saved those with Noah” but “We *carried* them.” The image is of a burden-bearer. The saved party contributed nothing to the carrying; the passenger of a ship does not propel it. This is the theological core of the reminder: salvation at the moment of universal destruction was pure carriage. And the same root will reappear later in this very sūrah at 17:66 and 17:70 — *“Your Lord is He Who makes the ships sail upon the sea”* and *“We carry them over land and sea”* — binding the Noahic deliverance to the ordinary, daily experience of the reader. Every voyage you survive is a small rehearsal of the Ark.

**“Truly He Was a Thankful Servant”**

The verse closes with *innahu kāna ʿabdan shakūrā* — “Truly he was a thankful servant.” Two of the highest Qur’anic titles converge in four words: *ʿabd* (servant) and *shakūr* (grateful). The intensive form *shakūr* denotes not occasional thanks but a settled character of gratitude — one whose gratitude is abundant, repeated, and constitutive of who he is. Compare 34:13, where the family of David is told: *“Work, O family of David, in gratitude. And few of My servants are grateful (shakūr).”* The rarity is explicit there, which makes the description of Noah here a high honor.

Why is Noah’s gratitude mentioned at exactly this point? Because the sūrah has just told Israel to *“take no guardian apart from Me”* — and the practical opposite of gratitude is the arrogance of self-sufficiency. The one who remembers he was carried cannot claim he rowed. Ibn Kathīr notes the pairing: Noah was saved, and Noah was grateful; the two belong together. A favor received without gratitude begins the process of forgetting, and forgetting is the first stage of the corruption the coming verses describe.

**The Two Servants Framing the Passage**

There is a structural elegance the commentators have noted. Verse 1 calls Muhammad ﷺ *ʿabd* — “His servant.” Verse 3 calls Noah *ʿabd* — “a thankful servant.” The passage about the Children of Israel is thus bracketed by two servants, one at each end of the prophetic chain the journey connected. Between them stands the community being warned. The implication is plain: what distinguishes the prophets from the warned community is not knowledge — Israel had the Book — but servitude and gratitude.

**Gratitude as the Test Every Community Fails First**

The practical application is uncomfortably direct. Communities do not usually collapse from external attack first; they collapse from the internal erosion of gratitude, which turns blessing into entitlement. The survivor of the Ark who stops mentioning the flood begins, within a generation, to believe the dry land was always his. The Qur’an’s diagnosis of Israel’s two episodes of “corruption upon the earth” begins here, with a reminder about carriage — because the memory of being carried is the only durable protection against the arrogance that destroys.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:4

> **And We decreed for the Children of Israel in the Book, “Surely you will work corruption upon the earth twice, and you will ascend to great height.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“We Decreed for the Children of Israel in the Book”**

*Wa qaḍaynā ilā banī isrāʾīla fī al-kitāb* — the verb *qaḍā* here carries two readings that the classical commentators both preserve. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī agree the dominant sense is *“We informed them in the Book”* — that is, God communicated to Israel through the Torah that these events would occur. Al-Qurṭubī records the alternative: that the decree was recorded in the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*), of which the Qur’an speaks at 85:22, *“Nay, it is a glorious Qur’an, in a Preserved Tablet.”* The difference matters less than it appears, because both readings agree on the substance: this was foreknown and foretold, not improvised. And crucially, both readings locate the prediction *inside Israel’s own scripture* — a claim the Qur’an invites its audience to test.

**“Working Corruption Upon the Earth”: The Meaning of Fasād**

The phrase *la-tufsidunna fī al-arḍ* uses the intensive form of *f-s-d*, the root of *fasād* — corruption, spoilage, disorder.

Al-Qurṭubī, discussing *fasād* at 7:56, explains that it means the departure from the order God established, whether in belief, in social relations, or in the treatment of the earth itself. Corruption is not merely sin; it is sin with systemic reach — the kind that leaks from the individual into institutions, markets, courts, and the soil. The Qur’an’s own diagnosis of environmental and social breakdown is stated at 30:41:

> *“Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what the hands of men have earned, that He may let them taste some of what they have done, so that perhaps they will turn back.”*

The intensive doubling in *la-tufsidunna* (with the emphatic *lām* and *nūn*) signals certainty and magnitude. This is not occasional lapse; it is corruption as a sustained condition.

**“Twice”: Why the Number Matters**

*Maratayn* — twice. The double structure organizes everything that follows: two risings, two devastations, and between them a restoration (v. 6). The commentators read the two corruptions as two periods of serious departure from the Torah’s commands, and some link them to episodes the Qur’an mentions elsewhere, particularly the killing of the prophets: *“And [We condemned them] for their disbelief and their slander against Mary, and for their saying, ‘We killed the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, the messenger of God’”* (4:157), and *“They disbelieved in the signs of God and killed the prophets without right”* (3:112).

**The Historical Readings and the Commentators’ Honesty**

Two sets of identifications are commonly proposed. The first pairs the destruction of the Northern Kingdom of Israel (722 BCE, by Assyria) with the destruction of the Southern Kingdom of Judah (586 BCE, by Babylon). The second pairs the Babylonian destruction of the First Temple (586 BCE) with the Roman destruction of the Second Temple (70 CE). The classical commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī — record many names in connection with these verses: Goliath, Sennacherib, Nebuchadnezzar, and others, and they are candid that the reports conflict. Al-Rāzī’s methodological intervention is the most useful thing said on this passage in the entire exegetical tradition: the meaning of the verse does not depend on settling the historical identifications. The Qur’an is not delivering a chronicle; it is delivering a law.

That law is this: a community given revelation is not exempt from consequence — it is *more* exposed to it. The very fact that the prediction was written in their own Book means they could not plead surprise.

**“And You Will Ascend to Great Height”**

*Wa la-taʿlunna ʿuluwwan kabīrā* — literally, “you will rise with a great rising.” In isolation the phrase sounds like praise. In context, every commentator reads it as condemnation. Al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī all gloss it as tyranny, rebellion, and arrogance against God. The reason the wording is left positive-sounding is instructive: arrogance *feels* like elevation. The arrogant person experiences himself as rising. The Qur’an preserves the phenomenology of arrogance — how it is lived from the inside — before revealing that the same rising is the prelude to a fall. Compare 28:83: *“That abode of the Hereafter — We assign it to those who seek neither exaltation (ʿuluww) in the earth nor corruption.”* The pairing of *ʿuluww* and *fasād* there is precisely the pairing in this verse, confirming the negative sense.

**The Logical Principle: Foreknowledge Without Compulsion**

A question naturally arises: if the corruption was decreed and written, were the people forced into it? The classical answer, articulated by al-Rāzī and standard in Sunni theology, is that divine foreknowledge of an act does not cause the act. God’s knowing that a person will choose corruption does not remove the choice, any more than a teacher’s accurate prediction that a particular student will fail does not make the student fail. The prediction in the Torah was given *to* them — which is itself a warning, and warnings presuppose the capacity to avoid. The same principle runs through verse 8, where the door of mercy is explicitly left open: *“It may be that your Lord will have mercy upon you, but if you revert, We shall revert.”*

**Why This Matters to a Reader Who Is Not Israelite**

The passage is addressed to a specific community, but its grammar is universal. Every nation, every institution, every family, and every individual life contains the same sequence: given advantages, corrupted them, suffered consequences, was granted a second turn, corrupted again. The Qur’an’s insistence that the pattern was *written down in advance* is what makes it a law rather than an accident — and a law is something a reader can apply to himself.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:5

> **So when the promise of the first of these came to pass, We sent against you servants of Ours, possessed of great might, and they ravaged your dwellings, and it was a promise fulfilled.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“Servants of Ours” — The Devastators as Instruments**

*Baʿathnā ʿalaykum ʿibādan lanā ulī baʾsin shadīd* — “We sent against you servants of Ours, of great military might.” The word chosen is *ʿibād* — servants, the same plural of *ʿabd* used of the Prophet ﷺ in verse 1 and of Noah in verse 3. The irony is severe and intentional: the devastators are called God’s servants while the devastated are the people of the Book. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī both note that this does not make the invaders righteous; it makes them *instruments*. A flood is not virtuous; it is used. The theological point is that God employs the wicked against the wicked without approving of either, as He says of the punishment of Pharaoh: *“So each We seized for his sin”* (29:40).

The commentators differ on identity: Goliath, Sennacherib, and Nebuchadnezzar are all named in the reports collected by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī. But Ibn Kathīr records the more sober position that the *identity* is not the message. The message is that the devastation arrived at the appointed time, from a direction Israel did not choose, with a force Israel could not match.

**“Possessed of Great Might”: Baʾs**

*Baʾs* is a rich Qur’anic term meaning severe force, violent assault, the shock of battle. Compare 21:12, *“And when they felt Our might (baʾsanā), behold, they fled from it,”* and 6:65, *“Say, ‘He is able to send upon you a punishment from above you or from beneath your feet, or to confound you in factions and make you taste the might (baʾs) of one another.’”* That last verse is the Qur’an’s general law of communal self-destruction, and it explains this one: the *baʾs* that fell on Israel came through human hands, but the condition that permitted it was internal.

**“They Ravaged Your Dwellings”**

*Fa-jāsū khilāla al-diyār* — the verb *jāsa* means to pass through and back, to penetrate thoroughly, to move through a space searching it. It is used only here in the entire Qur’an, which is itself noteworthy: a unique word for a unique devastation. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī explain that it describes invaders moving through the streets and between the houses — not merely attacking from outside but occupying the interior. *Al-diyār* means dwellings, homes, quarters. The choice of *homes* rather than *cities* or *fortresses* is deliberate. What is destroyed is the domestic, the intimate, the place where a family sleeps. Devastation that reaches the bedroom is total.

The commentators add that this included the plunder of wealth, the desecration of the Temple, the burning of the Torah, and the killing of those trained to read it (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī), with the survivors taken into captivity. The three losses are ordered in the reports exactly as a civilization loses itself: first the sanctuary, then the book, then the readers of the book.

**“And It Was a Promise Fulfilled”**

*Wa kāna waʿdan mafʿūlā* — “and it was a promise enacted.” The passive participle *mafʿūl* means done, carried out, executed. Al-Rāzī explains that this phrase closes the verse with the assurance that the event was not a historical accident but the discharge of a commitment. The same phrase appears at the end of verse 7 in relation to the second devastation, and again at 17:16 in relation to the destruction of towns — binding all three into one doctrine: *what God announces, happens.*

There is a mercy hidden in this severity.

If divine warning were unreliable, warning would be worthless, and human beings would have no way to navigate. The reliability of the threat is precisely what makes the reliability of the promise credible — including the promise at 39:53:

> *“Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, despair not of the mercy of God; truly God forgives all sins.”*

**The Discipline of Reading History Theologically**

The verse teaches a method as much as a fact. When calamity strikes a community, the Qur’an does not permit two opposite errors: the purely materialist reading (this was simply geopolitics) and the purely fatalist reading (this was arbitrary divine cruelty). It insists on a third: the event had worldly causes *and* it fell within a moral order that had been announced in advance. A reader today can apply the method without claiming to know God’s specific intent in any given disaster — the Qur’an itself, in the person of al-Rāzī’s counsel, declines to build doctrine on uncertain historical identifications. What it asks is simpler and harder: when the *baʾs* comes, ask what the *fasād* was.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:6

> **Then We gave you a turn against them, and We aided you with wealth and children, and We made you greater in number.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn: Radda al-Kurrah**

*Thumma radadnā lakumu al-kurrah ʿalayhim* — “Then We returned the turn for you against them.” The noun *kurrah* means a return, a repetition, a second round — in military language, the counter-attack. The word appears in the Qur’an describing a return to worldly life in 23:99–100, where the dying disbeliever begs, *“My Lord, return me (irjiʿūn), that I may do good”* — and is refused. Here the same root is granted, and the contrast is the point: to Israel, the turn *was* given. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that this followed their repentance from the corruption and arrogance described in verse 4.

The sequence is theologically essential. Verse 4 describes corruption; verse 5, devastation; verse 6, restoration. Nothing in verse 6 says the restoration was unearned or arbitrary — al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī connect it explicitly to repentance. The Qur’an’s entire moral economy runs on this hinge: *fasād* leads to destruction; *tawbah* (return) leads to restoration. The same root *r-j-ʿ* underlies both the human turning and the divine turning — a symmetry the Qur’an depends on throughout.

**What the Restoration Consisted Of**

The commentators offer a range of identifications, all within the same horizon: the killing of Goliath, the defeat of Babylon, the liberation of the land, the return of the exiles to the region of Jerusalem, and the rising of new prophets among them (al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī).

The Qur’an does not narrate the return from the Babylonian exile in detail, which is characteristic: it selects the *pattern* and leaves the *chronicle*. The three components named — wealth, children, numbers — are the classical criteria of communal restoration in the Qur’anic worldview, appearing together again at 18:46:

> *“Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world.”*

**“We Aided You With Wealth and Children”**

*Wa amdadnākum bi-amwālin wa banīn* — the verb *amdada* means to supply, to extend, to reinforce, as one reinforces an army with fresh troops. It is the same verb used at 3:124, *“Your Lord will reinforce you with three thousand angels sent down,”* and at 8:9. The choice implies that wealth and children were given as *reinforcement* — as strategic support — which makes their later misuse all the more serious. Reinforcements given for defense that are converted into arrogance become the instrument of the second fall.

Notice what is *not* said. The verse does not say “We gave you the Book again” or “We returned the Temple to its glory.” The material restoration is named; the spiritual one is left conditional, and the condition arrives immediately in verse 7: *“If you are virtuous, you are virtuous for the sake of your own souls.”*

**“And We Made You Greater in Number”**

*Wa jaʿalnākum akthara nafīrā* — *nafīr* literally means those who go forth, a levy, a mobilized group; by extension, a population able to muster. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as increase in population and in fighting strength. The word carries a martial undertone: a *nafīr* is not merely a census but a body of men who can be called out. God gave them not just survival but capacity.

**Why the Verse Is Structured as Three Gifts**

Wealth, children, numbers. Each corresponds to a distinct human anxiety: poverty, extinction, insignificance. The Qur’an repeatedly names these three as the things human beings crave most — 3:14, *“Beautified for mankind is the love of desires — women, children, heaped-up heaps of gold and silver”* — and repeatedly warns that they are tests, not proofs. The verse’s placement is therefore a setup. The reader who has followed from verse 4 knows that these exact gifts preceded the “great rising” of arrogance the first time. God is about to give them again. Verse 7 will explain on what terms.

**The Practical Lesson of the Second Chance**

Every reader who has survived a collapse recognizes this verse. The restoration after a personal disaster — a restored marriage, a rebuilt business, a recovered health — carries an unspoken condition that the human heart is remarkably good at forgetting. The returned exile who forgets the captivity within one generation has already begun the second corruption. The verse is a warning disguised as a blessing: the turn has been given; what you do with the wealth and children determines whether it was the last.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:7

> **If you are virtuous, you are virtuous for the sake of your own souls, and if you commit evil, it is for them. So when the other promise comes to pass, they will make wretched your faces, and enter the Temple as they entered it the first time, and utterly ruin whatsoever they overtake.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Principle Stated Before the Threat**

Before describing the second devastation, the verse inserts a rule: *in aḥsantum aḥsantum li-anfusikum wa in asaʾtum fa-lahā* — “if you do good, you do good for your own souls; and if you do evil, it is [against] them.” The rule is stated first because it is the *reason* the threat exists. God does not need Israel’s obedience and is not damaged by its disobedience. The consequences are self-generated.

This is one of the Qur’an’s most frequently repeated doctrines. Compare 10:108, *“Say: O mankind, the truth has come to you from your Lord; so whoever is guided is guided only for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment.”* And 31:12, *“And whoever gives thanks gives thanks only for his own soul.”* And 39:41, *“Whoever is guided is guided for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment.”* And 45:15, *“Whoever does right, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it.”* The repetition across Makkan and Madinan revelation shows this is not incidental phrasing but the structural logic of the Qur’anic moral universe.

The rhetorical question at 55:60 says it in one line:

> *“Is the reward of goodness anything but goodness?”*

**Why “For Your Own Souls” Is Not Selfishness**

A modern reader may find “you do good for your own soul” cold. The classical commentators read it as the opposite of cold: it is the guarantee of justice. If virtue benefited God, God would be needy; if vice harmed God, God would be vulnerable. Neither is possible. Therefore the moral order is not a divine accounting system but a description of how souls actually work — closer to the statement “if you drink poison you poison yourself” than to “if you disobey you insult the ruler.” Al-Rāzī develops this at length: the phrase establishes that the beneficiary and the victim of every moral act is the actor’s own soul, which is why the soul is the one thing no one can delegate.

**“So When the Other Promise Comes to Pass”**

*Fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu al-ākhirah* — “when the promise of the latter [occasion] comes.” The commentators understand this as the second sending of a people against the Israelites (al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī). Some reports connect it to the execution of John the Baptist (Yahyā) by Herod — reported by al-Ṭabarī and al-Jaṣṣāṣ — which places it in the Roman period and connects it to the second destruction of the Temple. Others, anachronistically, associate it with Nebuchadnezzar again. Al-Rāzī explicitly flags the chronological problem and draws his methodological conclusion: understanding the verse does not require resolving the history.

**“They Will Make Wretched Your Faces”**

*Li-yasūʾū wujūhakum* — from *s-w-ʾ*, the root of evil and of that which disgraces. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī explain it as the grief and humiliation becoming visible on the face. The Qur’an treats the face as the outward index of the soul, in both directions. The face that submits is honored: *“Whoever submits his face to God and is a doer of good, he has grasped the firmest handle”* (31:22). The face that is disgraced in this world anticipates the Hereafter: *“On the Day when some faces will whiten and some faces will blacken”* (3:106), and *“Their faces will be covered by the Fire”* (14:50). To have one’s face made wretched is therefore not merely to be sad; it is to have the inner state published on the surface where all can read it.

**“And Enter the Temple as They Entered It the First Time”**

*Wa li-yadkhulū al-masjida kamā dakhalūhu awwala marrah* — “and to enter the *masjid* as they entered it the first time.” The word *masjid* — place of prostration — is used of the Temple, confirming that the Qur’an regards the Jerusalem sanctuary as a house of the same God to whom Muslims prostrate. The repetition *as they entered it the first time* is a formula of exact recurrence: the second devastation will mirror the first. Recurrence is the signature of law. An event that happens once may be chance; an event that happens twice in the same shape is a pattern, and a pattern can be predicted — which is precisely why verse 4 said it was written down.

**“And Utterly Ruin Whatsoever They Overtake”**

*Wa li-yutabbirū mā ʿalaw tatbīrā* — the verb *tabbara* means to shatter, to demolish completely, to reduce to fragments. The noun *tabār* means total ruin; the Qur’an uses it at 25:39, *“And each We destroyed with utter destruction (tabbarnāhu tatbīrā).”* The adverbial cognate *tatbīrā* intensifies: not damage, but annihilation. *Mā ʿalaw* — “whatever they rise over,” i.e., whatever they gain dominion over. The object is deliberately indefinite: nothing is exempt.

**The Balance Between Divine Justice and Open Mercy**

Read alone, the verse is bleak. Read with verse 8, it is a complete moral theology: the pattern is fixed, and the door is open. God announced the fall before it happened, sent the punishment when it was deserved, and then said *if you revert, We shall revert* (v. 8). The severity is not caprice; it is the working of a rule that also runs in the other direction. This is what al-Rāzī means when he insists that the reader’s task here is not historical speculation but the extraction of the law — a law that is severe in its certainty of consequence and generous in its certainty of return.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:8

> **It may be that your Lord will have mercy upon you, but if you revert, We shall revert. And We have made Hell a prison for the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“It May Be That Your Lord Will Have Mercy Upon You”**

*ʿAsā rabbukum an yarḥamakum* — the particle *ʿasā* expresses expectation. When used of human beings it indicates uncertainty; when used of God, al-Ṭabarī and others explain that it indicates near-certainty, because God’s “perhaps” is not a doubt but a withholding of guarantee — a way of keeping hope alive without licensing presumption. Some commentators go further and read *ʿasā* here as equivalent to certainty: He *will* have mercy if the condition is met. The mercy is conditional, not automatic; and the condition is repentance, as al-Rāzī states.

The verse thus closes the Israelite cycle on mercy rather than on ruin — a structurally important choice. A passage that ended with devastation would teach fear only. Ending with mercy teaches that the entire cycle of rise, corruption, fall and restoration is contained within an economy of mercy whose final word has not been spoken.

**“But If You Revert, We Shall Revert”**

*Wa in ʿudtum ʿudnā* — four words, and one of the most symmetrical statements in the Qur’an. The same verb is used for both parties: *if you return [to corruption], We return [to punishment].* The symmetry is the whole doctrine. God’s response is not arbitrary; it is proportionate and reactive to the human initiative. The initiative always comes first from the creature. Compare 13:11, *“Truly God does not change the condition of a people until they change what is in themselves”* — the same reactive structure stated as a general law.

Some commentators, reading this together with 7:167 — *“And [remember] when thy Lord proclaimed that He would surely send against them, until the Day of Resurrection, those who would inflict upon them a terrible punishment”* — conclude that the cycle extends forward indefinitely. The pattern is not closed in history; it is available as a warning to every generation that reads it.

**“And We Have Made Hell a Prison for the Disbelievers”**

*Wa jaʿalnā jahannama lil-kāfirīna ḥaṣīrā* — *ḥaṣīr* means a place of confinement, a prison, from the root *ḥ-ṣ-r* meaning to confine, to besiege, to restrict.

The word also means a mat or carpet — that which is spread out and on which one is confined. Both senses have been noted: Hell as a place from which there is no exit, and Hell as the “bedding” of its inhabitants, as at 7:41:

> *“They shall have a bed of Hell and over them coverings.”*

The verse shifts from Israel to a universal statement, and the shift is the point. The cycle of mercy and return is offered to Israel; but the final alternative for those who refuse belief altogether is a place described as a *prison* — the exact opposite of the freedom the arrogant sought in “ascending to great height” (v. 4). Those who refused to be servants will be confined as prisoners. The Qur’an’s moral symmetry is exact: the arrogance of unbounded self-elevation ends in the constraint of an unbreakable enclosure.

**Why the Verse Ends the Cycle Here**

Verses 2–8 form a self-contained unit: the Book given, the warning written, the corruption, the fall, the return, the mercy, the reopened warning, and the final alternative. Then verse 9 turns to the Qur’an itself: *“Truly this Qur’an guides toward that which is most upright.”* The placement is deliberate. The reader has just witnessed, in eight verses, the entire moral history of a community that received a Book. Now the reader is handed a Book. The transition is an argument: the previous people had the Torah and were governed by this law; you have the Qur’an, and the law has not changed — but the guidance now available is described as leading to *that which is most upright*, which is more than the previous people were told.

**The Practical Doctrine of “If You Revert, We Shall Revert”**

This is perhaps the most useful sentence in the passage for an ordinary believer. It removes two errors at once. The first error is despair: the person who has fallen concludes that God has finished with him. The verse says otherwise — mercy is standing, and it is *ʿasā*, God’s characteristic way of saying *come back*. The second error is presumption: the person who believes his status or lineage or community membership exempts him. The verse says otherwise — the same community that was carried with Noah and given the Book was devastated twice. Between the two errors stands the actual rule, which is neither hopeful nor harsh but simply true: the direction of your movement determines the direction of His response.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:9

> **Truly this Quran guides toward that which is most upright, and gives glad tidings to the believers who perform righteous deeds that theirs shall be a great reward,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Aqūm: The Superlative of Uprightness**

*Inna hādhā al-qurʾāna yahdī lillatī hiya aqwam* — “this Qur’an guides to that which is *aqwam*.” The word is the elative of *qāʾim* / *qayyim*, from *q-w-m*, the root of standing, rising, being established.

*Aqwam* therefore means “most upright, most established, most firm, most correct.” The Qur’an uses the same word of the religion of Abraham at 30:30: *“So set thy face toward the religion, upright (ḥanīfan)... the nature (fiṭrah) of God upon which He created mankind,”* and at 98:5:

> *“And they were commanded only to worship God, sincere to Him in religion, upright (ḥunafāʾ).”*

Al-Ṭabarī explains that the guidance is toward truth, or toward the religion itself. Al-Zamakhsharī makes a grammatical observation that sharpens the meaning: the pronoun in *lillatī* (“to that which”) is feminine, so the antecedent is likely a feminine noun — *ṭarīqah* (way), *millah* (creed/religious community), or *khallah* (quality/state). The guidance is not toward a list but toward a *path*, a *creed*, a *condition of being*. Guidance in the Qur’an is directional, and direction implies a single line rather than a menu.

**Why “Most Upright” Rather Than Simply “Right”**

The elative form is doing real work. Every moral system claims to be right; the Qur’an claims to be *most* upright — a comparative claim that invites comparison and does not fear it. It also implies that other things are upright to some degree. The Qur’an acknowledges this repeatedly: *“And We revealed unto thee the Book in truth, confirming what was before it of the Book and as a guardian over it”* (5:48). The claim is not that nothing else has truth but that this is the most established, the most comprehensive, the most resistant to collapse. A building may be upright; *aqwam* is the one that survives the earthquake.

**The Conditional Glad Tidings**

*Wa yubashshiru al-muʾminīna alladhīna yaʿmalūna al-ṣāliḥāt* — “and gives glad tidings to the believers *who perform righteous deeds*.” The relative clause is not decorative. Faith alone is not the object of the glad tidings; faith *that acts* is. This pairing — *īmān* plus *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* — occurs well over fifty times in the Qur’an, so frequently that the classical commentators treat it as a technical formula. Al-Qurṭubī explains the logic: belief without action is a claim without evidence, and the Qur’an consistently refuses to honor unverified claims. The tree is known by its fruit; the faith is known by what it produces in the marketplace, the home, and the treatment of the orphan.

**“That Theirs Shall Be a Great Reward”**

*Anna lahum ajran kabīrā* — *ajr* is wage, recompense, payment for labor. The Qur’an’s choice of a commercial word for spiritual reward is deliberate: it establishes that the relationship, while not a contract of equals, has the structure of *work followed by payment*. The worker is not begging; he is being paid. And the adjective *kabīr* — great, large — is left unspecified, which in Qur’anic idiom is a way of exceeding imagination. The Garden itself is meant, per al-Ṭabarī, and the Qur’an describes it elsewhere in terms deliberately beyond description: *“No soul knows what is kept hidden for them of the comfort of the eyes, as a reward for what they used to do”* (32:17).

**The Logical Necessity of the Reward**

If the Qur’an guides to what is most upright, and uprightness is costly — it requires refusing profitable lies, forgiving when revenge is available, giving when keeping is easier — then a purely this-worldly moral system faces a serious problem: the upright person often loses. The Qur’an solves the problem not by promising worldly success but by placing the settlement outside this world. Al-Rāzī makes this an argument for the Hereafter’s rational necessity: a moral order in which virtue is systematically unrewarded and vice systematically unpunished is not a moral order but a lottery. The *ajr kabīr* is what makes uprightness rational rather than merely noble.

**Why This Verse Follows the Israelite Cycle**

The placement transforms the preceding eight verses from history into instruction. Israel was given a Book and a law of consequence; now the reader is told what *this* Book does — it guides to what is most upright, and it guarantees payment for those who act on it. The reader is being moved from the position of spectator watching another people’s failure to the position of participant holding a better instrument. The next verse will complete the offer by stating its refusal clause.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:10

> **and that We have prepared a painful punishment for those who believe not in the Hereafter.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of a Single Announcement**

Grammatically this verse is dependent on the previous one: the Qur’an gives glad tidings of two things — that the believers have a great reward, *and* that the disbelievers have a painful punishment. Both are *contents of the good news*, which is a striking formulation. For the believer, the announcement of the punishment of the deniers is genuinely good tidings, because it means that injustice will not have the final word. Al-Rāzī notes this: glad tidings include the vindication of the wronged, and no vindication is complete without the settling of accounts.

**Aʿtadnā: Already Prepared**

*Wa anna li-lladhīna lā yuʾminūna bi-l-ākhirati aʿtadnā lahum ʿadhāban alīmā* — the verb *aʿtadnā* is from *ʿ-t-d*, meaning to prepare, to make ready, to have something in reserve.

It is used of the Garden at 3:133:

> *“And hasten toward forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden whose breadth is the heavens and the earth, prepared (uʿiddat) for the reverent.”*

The same verb applied to both destinations establishes that neither is improvised at the moment of judgment; both are prepared in advance. The Qur’an’s eschatology is not a reaction but a readiness.

**Why Unbelief in the Hereafter Specifically**

The verse does not say “those who disbelieve” generally but specifies disbelief *in the Hereafter*. The specification is precise and diagnostic. Denial of the Hereafter is not one error among many; it is the error that removes the constraint on all others. A person who believes he will answer for his treatment of the orphan has a reason to treat the orphan well even when no one is watching. A person who believes there is no accounting has only prudence, and prudence fails whenever the odds favor him. Al-Qurṭubī makes this point directly: *al-ākhirah* is the foundation on which moral obligation rests, and its removal collapses the entire structure.

This is why the Qur’an pairs belief in God and belief in the Last Day so persistently — 2:4, 2:62, 2:177, 4:59, 5:69 — as though they were one article rather than two. They are structurally inseparable: a just God without a day of settlement would be unjust in outcome.

**“A Painful Punishment”: ʿAdhāb Alīm**

*Alīm* is the intensive form of “painful,” from *a-l-m*, pain. The adjective is applied to the punishment rather than to the punished, which the grammarians note as a rhetorical intensification: the pain is so intrinsic to the punishment that it is described as itself agonized. The phrase appears throughout the Qur’an — 2:10, 3:21, 4:18, 9:79, 16:104 — always in contexts of warning.

The Qur’an never dwells on the physical detail of the Fire the way it dwells on the detail of the Garden; the descriptions of punishment are brief and general, while the descriptions of reward are lush and specific. This asymmetry is itself instructive about the character of the revelation: it is far more interested in making the reward desirable than in making the punishment terrifying. The mention of *ʿadhāb alīm* here is a boundary marker, not a spectacle.

**The Logic of the Pair**

Verses 9 and 10 form a complete moral address: a direction (*aqwam*), a reward for those who follow it, and a consequence for those who deny that any settlement exists. Nothing is missing from the structure. A reader who wants to know what the Qur’an requires of him has been told in two verses: believe that the accounting is real, and act accordingly. Everything else in the sūrah — the moral code of verses 22–39, the prayers of verses 78–79, the arguments of verses 90–99 — is the elaboration of these two lines.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:11

> **Man prays for evil as he prays for good, and man is ever hasty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About the Danger of Answered Prayers**

*Wa yadʿu al-insānu bi-l-sharri duʿāʾahu bi-l-khayr* — “and man calls for evil with the same calling with which he calls for good.” The construction is comparative: *duʿāʾahu bi-l-khayr* means “his supplication for good,” so the verse says man supplicates for evil *in the same manner and with the same earnestness* as he supplicates for good. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī all identify the phenomenon: people pray for what they believe is beneficial and what will in fact harm them, or they pray in anger for the destruction of themselves, their children, or their property.

The implication, drawn explicitly by al-Ṭabarī, is severe: if God answered every supplication as it is uttered, human beings would be destroyed by their own tongues. This is one of the Qur’an’s clearest arguments that divine mercy includes the *refusal* of requests. The unanswered prayer is not evidence of neglect; it is often evidence of protection.

**The Psychological Mechanism**

The verse identifies a specific cognitive failure: the human being’s inability to predict consequences. He sees the immediate object of desire — the wealth, the position, the revenge, the removal of an obstacle — and does not see the chain it sets in motion. The Qur’an states the same diagnosis at 2:216: *“It may be that you dislike a thing while it is good for you, and it may be that you love a thing while it is evil for you. And God knows, and you do not know.”* The closing clause is the operative one: the problem is epistemic, not moral. Man is not wicked for wanting; he is *ignorant* of what wanting produces.

**“And Man Is Ever Hasty”**

*Wa kāna al-insānu ʿajūlā* — *ʿajūl* is the intensive form of hasty, impatient, precipitate. The verse states it as a fixed trait, *kāna* indicating a permanent condition. The Qur’an repeats the diagnosis: *“Man was created of haste (min ʿajal). I will show you My signs, so do not ask Me to hasten”* (21:37). And: *“Mankind was created of haste”* is echoed in the reports of the commentators — al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both transmit the account that when the Spirit was breathed into Adam beginning at the head, Adam sought to rise before it reached his feet.

Whether or not one reads that report literally, its meaning is exact and universally recognizable: the human being cannot wait for the completion of a process that is already working in his favor. He interrupts the very thing that is saving him. Anyone who has abandoned a course of treatment, a discipline, or a relationship one step before it bore fruit has performed this gesture.

**Haste as the Root of the Preceding Prayer Problem**

The two clauses are causally linked. Why does man pray for evil? Because he is hasty. Haste compresses the horizon: it makes the immediate vivid and the distant dim. The angry father who curses his son, the trader who borrows beyond his means, the patient who abandons the regimen, the reformer who destroys the institution before he has built the alternative — all are enacting *ʿajal*. The Qur’an’s remedy for haste is not willpower but *delay*: the discipline of not acting on the first impulse, of praying and then waiting.

This is why the prophetic tradition attaches such weight to deliberation. The Prophet ﷺ said to Ashajj ʿAbd al-Qays:

> *“In you there are two qualities that God loves: forbearance (ḥilm) and deliberation (anāh).”* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 17)

The two words are the precise antonyms of *ʿajal*.

**The Prophetic Prohibition Against Hasty Supplication**

The verse explains a practice the Prophet ﷺ explicitly legislated. Al-Bukhārī (6340) and Muslim (2735) record:

> *“The supplication of one of you is answered so long as he does not hasten, saying: ‘I supplicated and was not answered.’”*

The ḥadīth and the verse meet exactly: man is hasty even in his waiting for the answer. He prays, does not see the result within his chosen timeframe, and concludes that prayer is ineffective — thereby abandoning the very instrument that would have served him. The verse is thus not merely descriptive; it is a diagnosis of why people stop praying.

**The Practical Rule the Verse Establishes**

Read as instruction rather than description, the verse yields a simple discipline: never pray for a specific outcome in a state of anger, and never treat the refusal of a request as the refusal of care. A believer who internalizes this makes his supplication differently — asking for what is good *if* it is good, and adding, as the Prophet ﷺ taught, the condition that God’s knowledge govern. The hasty man asks for the thing; the instructed man asks for the good, and leaves the identification of the good to the One who sees the whole chain.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:12

> **We made the night and the day two signs. Then We effaced the sign of the night, and made the sign of the day, giving sight, that you might seek bounty from your Lord, and that you might know the number of years and the reckoning. And We have expounded everything in detail.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Signs: The Cosmological Argument in One Sentence**

*Wa jaʿalnā al-layla wa-l-nahāra āyatayn* — “We made the night and the day two signs.” The dual form *āyatayn* is precise: not signs among many, but a matched pair. The alternation is the sign; neither element alone would signify. A perpetual day or a perpetual night would produce no calendar, no rhythm, no sense of order — only a single undifferentiated condition. Meaning arises from the contrast.

This is one of the Qur’an’s most frequently repeated appeals. Compare 2:164, *“Truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the alternation of night and day, are signs for people of understanding”*; 3:190; 6:96–97, *“He cleaves the dawn, and He made the night for repose and the sun and the moon for reckoning”*; 10:67, *“It is He who made the night for you that you might rest therein, and the day giving sight”*; 30:23, *“And of His signs are your sleep by night and day, and your seeking of His bounty.”* The recurrence across the revelation shows that the Qur’an treats the alternation not as background but as *evidence* — the primary datum available to every human being in every age, requiring no laboratory and no scholarship.

**“We Effaced the Sign of the Night”**

*Fa-maḥawnā āyata al-layl* — the verb *maḥā* means to efface, to obliterate, to wipe out. The commentators offer two readings. The first, favored by al-Ṭabarī and reported from Ibn ʿAbbās, is that the “sign of the night” is the moon and the “sign of the day” is the sun; God “effaced” the moon’s sign by making it dark in itself, deriving its light from another — the crescent waxing and waning, unlike the sun’s constant blaze. The second reading is that the effacement refers to the darkness of night itself, which “blots out” visibility.

Both readings carry the same theological freight: the moon’s light is *borrowed*. It shines without being a source. This is the Qur’an’s most elegant natural metaphor for the whole created order — everything luminous in the universe is reflecting a light that is not its own. The verse that follows the night/day pair elsewhere makes the point directly: *“God is the Light of the heavens and the earth”* (24:35).

**“And Made the Sign of the Day, Giving Sight”**

*Wa jaʿalnā āyata al-nahāri mubṣirah* — “and We made the sign of the day *mubṣirah*,” literally “seeing” or “giving sight.” The active participle applied to the day is a striking personification: the day is described as itself seeing, or as causing sight. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the idiom as the day’s being made such that one sees in it — the same construction as “a night sleepless” meaning a night in which one does not sleep. The function is stated plainly: light exists so that vision is possible, and vision exists so that work is possible.

**Two Purposes: Bounty and Reckoning**

The verse names two ends. The first, *li-tabtaghū faḍlan min rabbikum* — “that you might seek bounty from your Lord” — is economic. The word *faḍl* means surplus, grace, superabundance; not mere survival but increase. The day exists for the pursuit of livelihood, and the Qur’an never treats that pursuit as inferior to worship. The same phrase appears at 62:10, *“And when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the land and seek of the bounty of God.”* Work is not a distraction from religion; the day was made for it.

The second, *wa li-taʿlamū ʿadada al-sinīna wa-l-ḥisāb* — “that you might know the number of the years and the reckoning” — is calendrical. Compare 10:5, *“It is He who made the sun a shining glory and the moon a light, and determined for it stations, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning,”* and 2:189, *“They ask thee about the new moons. Say: they are appointed times for mankind and for the pilgrimage.”* Time is knowable only because it is *measured*, and it is measurable only because it is *cyclical*. A universe without recurrence would be a universe without history.

**Why the Calendar Is a Religious Fact, Not a Neutral One**

The verse’s insistence that reckoning is a *purpose* of creation is easily overlooked and profoundly important. Islamic worship is time-structured: the five prayers depend on the sun’s position, Ramadan on the moon’s sighting, the pilgrimage on a lunar month, zakāt on the completion of a year. A religion whose obligations are indexed to celestial motion requires a cosmos in which that motion is regular, knowable, and divinely intended. The verse therefore grounds the entire structure of Islamic practice in the physics of the sky. It also explains why Muslim civilization developed astronomy so intensively: the calendar was not a curiosity but an act of obedience.

**“And We Have Expounded Everything in Detail”**

*Wa kulla shayʾin faṣṣalnāhu tafṣīlā* — the verb *faṣṣala* means to set out in detail, to distinguish item from item, to articulate.

The cognate adverb intensifies: fully detailed, nothing left vague. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as God’s having explained things clearly in the Qur’an. The same description is applied to God’s signs at 6:97–98, 7:174, 11:1, and to the Book at 7:52:

> *“a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe.”*

The claim is audacious and the commentators defend it carefully. “Everything” does not mean every fact about the physical universe — the Qur’an explicitly disclaims that at 6:50, *“I do not say to you that I possess the treasuries of God, nor that I know the Unseen.”* It means everything a human being needs for guidance: the nature of God, the obligations of the soul, the structure of justice, the reality of the Hereafter. On those matters, the Book leaves nothing essential unexplained.

**The Verse’s Argumentative Function**

Coming immediately after verse 11’s diagnosis of human haste, the verse performs a specific task. The hasty man cannot wait; the ordered cosmos teaches waiting. Day follows night with a regularity that no human can accelerate. The one who internalizes the rhythm of the sky learns the discipline that haste destroys — and he learns, from the same sky, that the One who measures the years is also the One who measures his life.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:13

> **And [for] every man We have fastened his omen upon his neck, and We shall bring it forth for him on the Day of Resurrection as a book he will meet wide open.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṭāʾir: The Bird That Became Destiny**

*Wa kulla insānin alzamnāhu ṭāʾirahu fī ʿunuqihi* — “every man, We have fastened his *ṭāʾir* to his neck.” The word *ṭāʾir* derives from *ṭ-y-r*, to fly; it means a bird, and by extension an omen — because the pre-Islamic Arabs, like many ancient peoples, practiced augury, reading the flight and behavior of birds as portents of fortune. Al-Rāzī explains the semantic history: an Arab undertaking a journey would startle a bird; if it flew to the right he proceeded, if to the left he turned back. The bird became, in language, the thing it signified — one’s fortune, one’s lot.

The Qur’an takes the pagan instrument and converts it. There is no bird; there is no flight to read. What is fastened to your neck is your *own* record. The verse thus performs a piece of theological demolition and reconstruction in a single image: the omen you sought in the sky is in fact the deed you performed on the ground.

Compare the Qur’an’s other treatment of augury, when the people of Thamūd said to Ṣāliḥ, *“We augur evil (iṭṭayyarnā) from you and those with you”* — and he answered, *“Your augury (ṭāʾirukum) is with God”* (27:47). The same word, the same correction: your fortune is not in the bird; it is in God’s knowledge and your own action.

**“Fastened to His Neck”: The Anatomy of the Image**

The verb *alzamnā* means We have made it adhere, We have bound it, We have made it inseparable. And the location is the *neck*. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī explain why: the neck is where honor and shame are displayed. A necklace of status hangs there; a chain of servitude is fastened there. Whatever is on the neck is visible, cannot be set down, cannot be forgotten, and travels with the person wherever he goes.

The image is exactly right for a moral record. You cannot put it down to sleep. You cannot hand it to another. It does not grow heavier as you carry it — it *is* you carrying it. Modern readers recognize the phenomenon in a different vocabulary: the debt that follows the borrower, the criminal record that follows the applicant, the reputation that precedes the arrival. The Qur’an’s claim is that the moral version is more inescapable than any of these.

**Two Readings: Deeds or Decree**

The commentators divide. Many — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī — take the *ṭāʾir* as one’s deeds, which portend bliss or wretchedness exactly as the bird was thought to portend fortune. Others take it as the divine decree (*qadar*) apportioned to each individual, supported by the traditions that every human being is born with an apportioned lifespan, provision, offspring, and ultimate state. Al-Qurṭubī records a third reading: that the *ṭāʾir* is moral responsibility itself (*taklīf*) — the inescapable fact that every human being is under obligation, and cannot escape being answerable.

The three readings converge on one practical conclusion, and this is why the debate never troubled the exegetes as much as it might have: whether the record on your neck is what you did, what was apportioned to you, or the fact that you are answerable — in every case, you cannot remove it, and it will be opened.

**“As a Book He Will Meet Wide Open”**

*Wa nukhriju lahu yawma al-qiyāmati kitāban yalqāhu manshūrā* — *manshūr* means spread open, unrolled, published.

The participle implies that the book is already open when he encounters it; there is no unsealing, no examination, no delay. The same scene appears at 18:49:

> *“And the Book will be set down, and thou wilt see the guilty fearful of what is in it, and they will say: ‘Alas for us! What kind of Book is this that leaves out nothing small or great but has enumerated it!’”*

And at 69:19–25, where the one given his book in his right hand says *“Here, read my Book! I knew that I would meet my reckoning,”* while the one given it in his left hand says *“I wish my Book had not been given to me.”* And at 84:7–10, where the one given his book in his right hand receives an easy reckoning, while the one given it behind his back cries for destruction.

**Why the Book Is Wide Open to Its Owner**

The openness of the book is theologically significant. It is not merely that God sees the contents; it is that the *owner* sees them, completely, with no capacity for the self-deception that operated in this world. Al-Rāzī offers the most penetrating account of what the book actually is: every deed is like a drop of water falling on rock — individually small, but each leaves an impression, and repeated deeds carve channels. The soul is the rock. The book is not an external ledger brought from a heavenly archive; it is the soul itself, inscribed by its own choices, made legible at last.

Al-Qurṭubī preserves the formulation that captures this: *“Your tongue is its pen, your saliva is its ink, your limbs are its pages; you dictate it to your own memory.”* The author of the book and its reader are the same person.

**The Psychological Truth Beneath the Image**

The verse describes an eschatological scene, but its accuracy about ordinary psychology is what makes it land. Every human being already carries a private, largely unread record — of the promise broken, the kindness omitted, the sentence spoken in anger. Most of life consists of not reading it. Distraction, work, noise, and self-justification function as the closed cover. The Day of Resurrection, in this verse, is not the arrival of new information; it is the removal of the cover. *Manshūrā* — spread open. The terror of the scene is not that God will accuse you, but that you will finally agree with Him.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:14

> **“Read your book! On this Day, your soul suffices as a reckoner against you.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Read**

*Iqraʾ kitābaka* — “Read your book.” Two words, and they contain the entire moral theory of the Qur’an. The verb *iqraʾ* is the same verb with which the revelation began: *“Recite (iqraʾ) in the name of thy Lord who created”* (96:1). The first word revealed to the Prophet ﷺ is given here to every human being on the Day of Judgment. The one who refused to read the Book of God in this world will be commanded to read the book of himself in the next.

The command is addressed to the individual in the singular. There is no collective reading, no tribal record, no delegation. The Qur’an is emphatic that the reckoning is personal: *“And every one of them will come to Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone (fardan)”* (19:95).

**Why “Read” Rather Than “Be Read To”**

The choice of the active imperative is decisive. The individual is not a defendant hearing a prosecutor; he is a reader reading his own text. Al-Rāzī explains that in reading the book, the person renders judgment on himself — because the book consists solely of his own acts, and no one is better placed to assess them. The verdict, in a profound sense, is self-pronounced. This is the Qur’an’s answer to the complaint that divine judgment is arbitrary: it is not. The evidence is produced by the accused, in his own hand, from his own life.

**“Your Soul Suffices as a Reckoner Against You”**

*Kafā bi-nafsika al-yawma ʿalayka ḥasībā* — *ḥasīb* is the one who reckons, computes, audits; from the same root as *ḥisāb*, reckoning, and *ḥasab*, number. The construction *kafā bi-* means “suffices” — your soul is *enough* as an auditor against you. Not merely competent; sufficient. No external witness is required.

The Qur’an develops this idea in the testimony of the limbs, which is among its most vivid eschatological images. At 36:65: *“This Day We shall seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify to what they earned.”* At 41:20–22: *“And you were not hiding yourselves lest your hearing, your sight, or your skins testify against you; but you assumed that God does not know much of what you do... And they will say to their skins, ‘Why did you testify against us?’ They will say, ‘God, who gives speech to all things, gave us speech.’”* And at 24:24: *“On the Day when their tongues, their hands, and their feet will testify against them for what they used to do.”*

The logic is severe and exact: the instruments you used to commit the act will be the witnesses to it. The hand that took, the tongue that lied, the eye that lingered — each will be given speech. The person who believed his body was a neutral tool discovers it was a recording device.

**God Does Not Wrong: The Guarantee Beneath the Severity**

The self-reckoning is framed by a doctrine the Qur’an repeats so often it functions as a refrain: God does not wrong people; they wrong themselves. *“Truly God does not wrong mankind in anything, but mankind wrong themselves”* (10:44). *“And God did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves”* (16:33). *“Whoever is guided is guided for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment”* (17:15 — the very next verse). The sequence is deliberate: verse 14 establishes that the record is self-authored; verse 15 establishes that the consequence is self-incurred. Together they close every avenue of complaint.

**The Mercy Hidden in the Verse**

Read only as threat, the verse is unbearable. But the classical commentators note something else: if the soul suffices as a reckoner, then the soul that has repented has already begun to read. The act of reading one’s book is available *now*. This is the entire practice of *muḥāsabah* — self-reckoning — which the second Caliph ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb formulated in the sentence the tradition preserves:

> *“Reckon yourselves before you are reckoned, and weigh yourselves before you are weighed; for it is lighter for you to reckon yourselves today in preparation for the great Reckoning.”* (reported in the ascetic tradition; al-Tirmidhī 2459 in similar wording)

The practice is not an invention of the pious but an anticipation of the verse. The one who reads his book daily will not be surprised by it on the Day. This is why the great spiritual writers made nightly self-examination a discipline: the book is being written in real time, and the cover is only temporarily closed.

**The Verse as an Argument for Moral Seriousness**

Stripped of its eschatological setting, the verse makes a claim about the human condition that even a secular reader can test: you already know. Beneath the justifications, beneath the narratives you tell others and yourself, there is an accurate internal account of what you did and why. The energy spent constructing the alternative narrative is measurable — in the avoidance of silence, in the reflexive self-defense, in the anger at anyone who reads too closely. The Qur’an’s claim is simply that this internal knowledge is not a private opinion but the actual record, and that the Day of Resurrection is the day it is published. The command *iqraʾ kitābaka* is therefore not only future; it is the most useful sentence a person can obey today.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:15
