> **Whosoever is rightly guided is only rightly guided for the sake of his own soul, and whosoever is astray is only astray to its detriment. None shall bear the burden of another. And never do We punish till We have sent a messenger.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Doctrines in a Single Verse**

This verse compresses three of the Qur’an’s most fundamental principles into two sentences, and their order is not accidental. First: guidance and error are personally consequential. Second: no one carries another’s load. Third: punishment presupposes a messenger. The three together form the Qur’an’s complete theory of justice — responsibility is individual, consequence is proportionate, and liability requires notification.

**The Personal Return of Every Act**

*Man ihtadā fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsihi wa man ḍalla fa-innamā yaḍillu ʿalayhā* — the reflexive structure is emphatic. The one who is guided is guided *for his own soul*; the one who errs errs *against it*. The verse has already appeared in essence at 17:7 in the Israelite cycle, and it recurs at 10:108, 27:40, 31:12, 39:41, 40:28, and 45:15. Al-Ṭabarī explains the underlying point: God is not increased by obedience nor diminished by disobedience. The moral order is not a treasury from which God draws profit; it is a description of what happens to souls.

The practical consequence is immense and easily missed. Because the return is personal, no proxy arrangement works. A person cannot inherit another’s righteousness, cannot be carried on someone else’s piety, cannot outsource his standing before God to his family, his shaykh, his nation, or his party. This is the Qur’an’s most leveling doctrine: it dissolves every hierarchy of spiritual credit.

**“No Bearer of Burdens Shall Bear the Burden of Another”**

*Wa lā taziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā* — the verb *wazara* means to bear a heavy load; *wizr* is the load, and by extension the sin, and also the mountain-shelter (as in 75:11, *“No! There is no refuge (wazīr)”*). The phrase is repeated at 6:164, 35:18, 39:7, and 53:38 — always in the same wording, which indicates a settled formula. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as the abolition of a pre-Islamic Arab practice: the custom of holding a tribe or a family liable for the crime of one of its members, and of blood vengeance falling on whoever was available rather than on the perpetrator. The verse strikes at collective punishment as a principle.

The commentators raise an apparent tension: does not the one who *leads others astray* carry their burden? The resolution is careful and important. The misleader carries the burden of his own misleading, not the burden of the misled person’s act. At 16:25: *“That they may bear their burdens in full on the Day of Resurrection, and some of the burdens of those whom they misled without knowledge. Truly evil is what they bear.”* The additional burden is the burden of *causing*, which is his own deed — not a transfer of the victim’s guilt. At 37:32–33 the misled say to their misleaders, *“You used to come at us from the right hand”* — and are answered that they themselves did not believe. Both parties are answerable for their own act.

**“And Never Do We Punish Till We Have Sent a Messenger”**

*Wa mā kunnā muʿadhibīna ḥattā nabʿatha rasūlā* — and this is the verse’s theological crown.

It is one of the clearest statements in the Qur’an of the principle that divine punishment requires prior notification. Compare 6:131: *“That is because thy Lord would not destroy the towns for their wrongdoing while their people were heedless”* — that is, before a messenger had informed them. And 26:208–209: *“And no town did We destroy but that it had warners, as a reminder — and We are never unjust.”* And 28:59:

> *“And thy Lord would not destroy the towns till He had raised in their mother-city a messenger reciting unto them Our signs.”*

This is the Qur’an’s answer to the most serious objection to revealed religion: how can a just God condemn those who never heard? The answer is that He does not. Liability is not retroactive and it is not inherited from Adam. It arises only when the message arrives.

The classical theologians debated the status of those who lived in periods between prophets (*ahl al-fatra*) — those who received no messenger at all. The dominant Sunni position, drawing on exactly this verse, is that such people are not punished for what they could not have known. Al-Ghazālī devoted a section of his *Fayṣal al-Tafriqah* to the question and concluded that the vast majority of humankind in his own day — including those in distant lands who had received only distorted reports of Islam — fell outside the category of the condemned, because they had never received the message in a form that made its truth compelling. Ibn Taymiyyah took a similar line. The verse is the ground on which that generosity stands.

**Why the Three Belong Together**

The sequence answers a chain of objections. If consequences are personal, is that not harsh? No — because no one is burdened with another’s sin, and each carries only his own. If each carries his own, is that not unfair to those born into ignorance? No — because no one is punished before a messenger comes. Every avenue of complaint is closed in order, and the closing is not by assertion but by principle. The verse is, in effect, the Qur’an’s declaration that its God is not arbitrary.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:16

> **And when We desire to destroy a town, We command those who live a life of luxury within it; yet they commit iniquity therein. Thus the Word comes due against it and We annihilate it completely.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Elite Are Addressed First**

*Wa idhā aradnā an nuhlika qaryatan amarnā mutrafīhā* — “and when We intend to destroy a town, We command its *mutrafūn*.” The word *mutraf* is from *t-r-f*, meaning to be given ease and abundance; the *mutraf* is the one who lives in luxury, the pampered, the soft-living elite.

The Qur’an uses the term consistently as a moral category: *“And We sent no warner to a town but its mutrafūn said: ‘We disbelieve in that with which you were sent’”* (34:34). And at 23:33, the chiefs of Noah’s people; at 11:116, *“And thy Lord would not destroy the towns unjustly while their people were reformers”*; at 43:23:

> *“And likewise We sent no warner before thee into any town but its mutrafūn said: ‘We found our fathers upon a way, and we follow in their footsteps.’”*

The pattern is consistent enough to be a law: the first to reject the prophets are the comfortable. Not because luxury is inherently evil, but because the comfortable have the most to lose from a message that demands redistribution of wealth, status, and moral authority. The Qur’an’s sociology of disbelief is precise and unsentimental.

**The Difficulty of “We Command”**

*Amarnā* — “We commanded” — is one of the most discussed words in the sūrah. Three readings are preserved.

The first, held by al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī, al-Jaṣṣāṣ and most commentators, is that God commands the *mutrafūn* to belief and obedience, and they respond with iniquity. On this reading *amarnā* is the command of the Law, and *fa-fasaqū* — “and they transgressed” — is their refusal. The command and the transgression are in the correct moral order: command first, disobedience second.

The second, al-Zamakhsharī’s, is that the verse means God commands them to commit iniquity — but he immediately insists this must be read metaphorically, since the Qur’an states categorically at 7:28, *“Say: God does not command indecency.”* For al-Zamakhsharī the meaning is that by granting them the means, the power, and the impunity to transgress, it is *as if* He had commanded it.

The third, recorded by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī, is a variant vocalization: *ammarnā* — “We made them rulers” — from *imārah*, authority. On this reading the verse says: when We intend to destroy a town, We place its luxurious class in positions of authority, and they then commit iniquity, and the sentence falls.

The three readings converge on the same historical observation — that the ruin of communities begins at the top — and the exegetes’ refusal to read the verse as divine authorship of sin is itself a datum worth noting: the entire classical tradition treats 7:28 as an interpretive constraint on this verse.

**Al-Rāzī on the Timing of Destruction**

Al-Rāzī’s contribution is a distinction of real philosophical value. “When We desire to destroy a town” does not mean that God suddenly forms a new intention; it means that the time approaches for the destruction of a town whose fate is already known to Him. God’s knowledge is eternal; the *timing* of the decree is what unfolds. The command to the *mutrafūn* at that moment is therefore genuine — they are truly commanded, and they truly disobey — even though the outcome is foreknown. This is the same reconciliation of foreknowledge and freedom the commentators applied to verse 4, and it is the standard Sunni position: foreknowledge is not causation.

**“Thus the Word Comes Due Against It”**

*Fa-ḥaqqa ʿalayhā al-qawl* — the verb *ḥaqqa* means to become true, to be established, to fall due. The “word” (*qawl*, elsewhere in the Qur’an *kalimah*) is the threatened sentence. The same formula appears at 10:96, 28:63, 32:13, 36:7, 37:3, 39:19, 40:6, 41:25, 46:18. Its recurrence establishes that divine punishment operates on a schedule with a fixed endpoint: respite is real, but it expires.

The verse therefore teaches a doctrine of delay that is neither mercy nor indifference. The town is not destroyed immediately; it is given time, warned, commanded, and observed. When the word falls due, it is because the period of opportunity has been consumed, not because God lost patience.

**“And We Annihilate It Completely”**

*Fa-dammarnāhā tadmīrā* — *dammara* means to destroy utterly; the cognate adverb *tadmīrā* intensifies it to total demolition. Al-Rāzī explains the word as uprooting from the foundations. The same verb describes the fate of Pharaoh’s people at 7:137 and the destroyed towns at 25:39. The Qur’an’s use of total-destruction vocabulary is not decorative: it corresponds to the archaeological reality of the ruined cities the Arabs passed on their caravan routes — Ḥijr, Sodom, Maʾrib, Petra — which the Qur’an repeatedly invites them to walk through and look at. *“And how many a town have We destroyed whose people were wrongdoers, so it is fallen in upon its roofs!”* (22:45).

**The Sociological Lesson**

The verse’s practical force is that it locates the origin of communal ruin precisely where ordinary analysis does not look. When a society decays, the visible symptoms are usually at the bottom — crime, disorder, poverty. The Qur’an places the cause at the top — the *mutrafūn*, those who benefit from the existing order and therefore resist the reformer. Any reader who has watched an institution fail — a company, a family, a state — recognizes the mechanism: the people with the most to lose from correction are the people with the most power to prevent it, and they use it. The destruction that follows is not a punishment of the weak for the sins of the strong; it is the collapse of a structure whose leaders refused to repair it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:17

> **How many a generation have We destroyed after Noah! Thy Lord suffices as One Aware of the sins of His servants, Seeing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Noah as the Watershed of Sacred History**

*Wa kam ahlaknā min al-qurūni min baʿdi Nūḥ* — “and how many generations have We destroyed after Noah.” Noah occupies a specific structural position in Qur’anic history: he is the first messenger to a people who were destroyed for rejecting him. Compare 7:59–64, the account of Noah and the flood, and 11:25–49. The verse’s phrase *min baʿdi Nūḥ* therefore means something precise: from that first destruction onward, the pattern has repeated — ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, Midian, the towns of the prophets, Pharaoh’s court. The Qur’an returns to these ruins constantly: 6:6, *“Have they not seen how many generations We destroyed before them?”*; 7:4, *“And how many a town have We destroyed!”*; 19:74; 20:128; 21:11; 22:45.

The function of the destroyed generations in Qur’anic rhetoric is evidential. They are not stories; they are *precedents*. A legal argument cites precedent to establish that a rule has been applied before; the Qur’an cites destroyed cities to establish that the rule it announces has been enforced before.

**Kam: The Indefinite Number as Rhetoric**

*Kam* — “how many” — is used here in its exclamatory (*khabariyyah*) sense, indicating multiplicity beyond counting.

The indefinite quantity is more effective than a number would be. A specific figure invites the reader to catalogue and dismiss; an unbounded figure places him inside a pattern he cannot totalize. The same device appears at 2:243:

> *“Didst thou not consider those who went forth from their homes, being thousands, out of fear of death?”*

And at 22:45, *“How many a town have We destroyed!”*

**“Thy Lord Suffices as One Aware, Seeing”**

*Wa kafā bi-rabbika bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihi khabīran baṣīrā* — *khabīr* is the one who has intimate, detailed knowledge, from *kh-b-r*, meaning to test and thereby to know something by direct acquaintance; *baṣīr* is the one who sees, from *b-ṣ-r*, denoting not merely sight but insight. The pairing is one of the Qur’an’s characteristic divine name-pairs and appears at 4:58, 25:58, 67:19.

The verse’s closing is a reassurance directed at the Prophet ﷺ and at every believer who has watched wrongdoing go apparently unpunished. The generations were destroyed; the record was kept; nothing was missed. *Kafā* — “it suffices” — is the same verb used in verse 14: *“your soul suffices as a reckoner against you.”* The sufficiency of divine awareness answers the insufficiency of human justice.

**Why This Verse Follows Verse 16**

Verse 16 states the law of communal destruction; verse 17 documents its enforcement history. The sequence is legal reasoning: principle, then precedent, then the assurance that the judge has the file. The reader who doubts whether the law will actually be applied is pointed to the archaeological record — the ruined towns the Quraysh walked past on the caravan route to Syria, the very route the Prophet ﷺ had traveled as a merchant.

**The Comfort and the Warning in One Sentence**

The verse cuts both ways, and the classical commentators read it in both directions. For the oppressed, “thy Lord suffices as One Aware of the sins of His servants” is comfort: the tyrant’s crimes are recorded by a witness who cannot be bribed, intimidated, or deceived. For the oppressor, the same sentence is a warning of unusual precision: *bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihi* — “of the sins of His servants” — not of some servants but of all, not of the public ones but of the concealed ones. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said that the believer’s fear of God is proportional to his knowledge of God, and this verse is the ground of that fear: the accounting is complete.

The practical application for an ordinary life is narrower but just as real. Every person carries private wrongs that no human court will ever see — the advantage quietly taken, the reputation quietly damaged, the promise quietly broken. The verse asserts that these are not outside the record. That assertion is either terrifying or consoling, depending entirely on which side of the transaction one stands.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:18

> **Whosoever would desire the ephemeral, We hasten for him therein whatsoever We will for whomsoever We desire. Then We appointed Hell for him, wherein he shall burn, blameworthy, banished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-ʿĀjilah: The Hurried Thing**

*Man kāna yurīdu al-ʿājilah* — “whoever desires *al-ʿājilah*.” The word is the active participle of *ʿ-j-l*, haste — the same root as *ʿajūl* in verse 11. *Al-ʿājilah* literally means “the hastening one,” and it is the Qur’an’s standing name for this world: the world of immediacy, of what arrives quickly. The pairing is fixed throughout the revelation: *al-ʿājilah* and *al-ākhirah*, the hasty and the final. See 75:20–21, *“Nay, but you love the hastening (al-ʿājilah) and neglect the Hereafter (al-ākhirah).”* And 76:27, *“Truly these love the hastening and leave behind a heavy Day.”*

The naming is a diagnosis, not a description. The world is not called “the near” (*al-dunyā* also carries the sense of nearness, from *d-n-w*); it is called “the hasty” — the thing that comes fast. Its defining characteristic is speed of delivery, which is precisely what makes it attractive and precisely what makes it deceptive.

**The Connection to Verse 11**

The link between verse 11 and verse 18 is the hinge of this section. Man is *ʿajūl* — hasty by nature — and therefore prays for evil as he prays for good. Here, whoever desires *al-ʿājilah* is the same hasty creature, now acting on his hastiness at the level of a whole life-orientation rather than a single request. The psychological root is identical: the vividness of the immediate overwhelms the reality of the distant.

**“We Hasten for Him Therein Whatsoever We Will for Whomsoever We Desire”**

*Nuʿajjil lahu fīhā mā nashāʾ li-man nurīd* — the verse’s threefold qualification is deliberate and devastating. God hastens for the seeker of the immediate: (1) what He wills — not what the seeker asked for; (2) for whom He wills — not for every seeker; (3) in the measure He wills. The one who has oriented his life toward getting does not thereby get. Al-Rāzī draws out the irony: the very thing desired is withheld from most who desire it, and given to some who do not. The worldly reward is not a wage; it is a dispensation.

This dismantles the most common error in religious reasoning — the assumption that material success indicates divine approval. The Qur’an is explicit that it does not: *“Let not man consider that when We provide for him and honor him, We are honoring him. Nay, but We are testing him”* (89:15–16). And: *“Do they think that what We provide them of wealth and sons — We hasten unto them good things? Nay, but they do not perceive”* (23:55–56).

**“Then We Appointed Hell for Him”**

*Thumma jaʿalnā lahu jahannama yaṣlāhā madhmūman madḥūrā* — the verb *jaʿalnā* (We made, We appointed) is in the past tense, though the event is future. The past tense for future divine acts is a standard Qur’anic device indicating certainty: what God has decreed is as good as done. *Yaṣlāhā* — he will roast in it, from *ṣ-l-y*, to be exposed to fire. And the two adjectives complete the humiliation: *madhmūm* — blamed, condemned, the opposite of praiseworthy; *madḥūr* — banished, driven away, expelled from mercy.

The pairing of blame and banishment is exact. The seeker of the immediate sought status and pleasure; he receives blame and expulsion. The one who lived for reputation dies disgraced; the one who lived for proximity to the powerful ends driven from all proximity.

**Who Counts as a Seeker of the Ephemeral**

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī extend the category beyond obvious sinners to include those who perform apparently pious acts for worldly ends — the one who gives charity for reputation, who learns knowledge for a position, who fasts to be seen. Their deeds look like the deeds of the seekers of the Hereafter; their orientation is the opposite. This is the doctrine of *riyāʾ*, ostentation, which the Prophet ﷺ called “the lesser shirk.” Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record from Jundub:

> *“Whoever acts to be heard, God will make him heard [i.e., expose him]; and whoever acts to be seen, God will make him seen.”* (al-Bukhārī 6499; Muslim 2986)

The verse therefore does not distinguish between the open sinner and the hidden hypocrite by their acts but by their orientation — which is why verse 19 will make intention one of the three conditions of reward.

**The Logic of the Verse**

The verse is not saying that worldly success is a punishment; it says that worldly success obtained by an orientation wholly directed at this world is a *completion of the request and nothing more*. You asked for the immediate; you received the immediate; the transaction is closed. There is no further credit. The severity lies in the closure — the door through which the reward would have come has been locked from the inside.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:19

> **And whosoever desires the Hereafter, and endeavors for it earnestly, and is a believer, it is they whose efforts shall be appreciated.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Conditions, Stated in Order**

*Wa man arāda al-ākhirata wa saʿā lahā saʿyahā wa huwa muʾmin* — the verse sets three conditions for the acceptance of effort, and al-Rāzī enumerates them precisely.

The first is *intention*: “whoever desires the Hereafter.” The orientation must be toward the final, not the immediate. The second is *earnest striving*: “and strives for it its striving” — *saʿā lahā saʿyahā* uses the cognate accusative, an intensifying construction meaning he strives for it *the striving it deserves*, with full effort proportionate to the goal. The third is *faith*: “while he is a believer.”

Al-Rāzī observes a crucial feature: the verse does not require *success* or *completion*. It requires desire, earnest endeavor, and belief. A person who intends good, strives genuinely, and is prevented by death or incapacity has fulfilled the verse’s conditions. This is one of the Qur’an’s clearest bases for the doctrine that deeds are judged by intentions — a doctrine al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both cite here.

**The Ḥadīth of Intentions**

The Prophetic formulation is the most frequently cited sentence in Islamic literature, opening al-Bukhārī’s Ṣaḥīḥ as its very first entry:

> *“Deeds are only according to intentions, and every man shall have only what he intended. Whoever’s emigration was for God and His Messenger, his emigration is for God and His Messenger; and whoever’s emigration was for a worldly gain he might obtain, or a woman he might marry, his emigration is for that to which he emigrated.”* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

Al-Zamakhsharī cites this ḥadīth directly in connection with this verse. The two Hijrah cases in the ḥadīth are outwardly identical — the same journey, the same hardship, the same destination — and they differ only in the invisible orientation. This is the exact structure of verses 18 and 19: two people performing similar acts, receiving categorically different outcomes, distinguished by nothing observable.

**“It Is They Whose Effort Shall Be Appreciated”**

*Fa-ulāʾika kāna saʿyuhum mashkūrā* — *mashkūr* means thanked, appreciated, acknowledged. The word is remarkable: God *thanks* the servant. The Qur’an uses the same language at 4:147, *“What would God do with your punishment if you are grateful and believe? And God is Ever Thankful (shākir), Knowing,”* and at 2:158, *“Truly God is Thankful (shākiran), Knowing.”* That the Infinite should describe Himself as thanking the finite is one of the most striking features of Qur’anic theology. Al-Ṭabarī explains that God’s “thanks” is His reward — the acknowledgment expressed as recompense.

The word also recalls verse 3, where Noah is called *ʿabdan shakūrā*, a thankful servant. The root *sh-k-r* binds the passage together: the grateful servant thanks God, and God thanks the striving servant. The relationship is one of reciprocal acknowledgment, not of cold transaction.

**Why Faith Is the Third Condition and Not the First**

The order — desire, striving, belief — is unusual; one might expect belief first. Al-Rāzī’s explanation is instructive: belief is stated last as the *condition of validity* that must be present alongside the other two, not as a prior stage. It functions like the qualification at the end of a legal formula: whatever the striving, and whatever the intention, the whole is accepted *if* the person is a believer. The placement emphasizes that intention and effort without faith are incomplete, exactly as faith without intention and effort are incomplete.

This is why the Qur’an’s formula *āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — “those who believe and do righteous deeds” — appears so persistently. Neither half suffices alone.

**The Practical Effect of the Verse**

The verse is unusually generous in a way that has real psychological consequence. A person who strives and fails is often paralyzed by the sense that his effort was wasted — the abandoned project, the uncompleted study, the reform that did not take. The verse states that the accounting is of the *striving*, not the *outcome*. This is not a license for half-effort; the verse demands *saʿyahā*, the striving the goal deserves. But it does mean that the sincere worker who dies mid-task has not lost his wage.

The pairing with verse 18 produces the complete doctrine: orientation determines the value of the act. Two people give the same coin; one seeks the immediate and gets it, one seeks the final and is thanked. The coin is identical. The Qur’an’s moral arithmetic is conducted entirely in the invisible.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:20

> **Each do We aid—both these and those—with the Gift of thy Lord; and the Gift of thy Lord is not confined.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Refuses the Easy Moral Story**

After drawing the sharpest possible contrast between the seeker of the immediate and the seeker of the final, the Qur’an immediately complicates it: *kullan numiddu hāʾulāʾi wa hāʾulāʾi min ʿaṭāʾi rabbik* — “each do We supply — these and those — from the gift of thy Lord.” *Kullan* is an accusative of totality: *all* of them, without exception. Both groups receive. The *ʿaṭāʾ* — the gift, the grant, the bestowed provision — is not distributed according to spiritual merit.

This is one of the Qur’an’s most honest statements about the world as it actually appears. The wicked are often prosperous; the pious are often poor. Any religious system that denies this is falsified by a single observation. The Qur’an confronts it directly and explains it rather than explaining it away.

**“And the Gift of Thy Lord Is Not Confined”**

*Wa mā kāna ʿaṭāʾu rabbika maḥẓūrā* — *maḥẓūr* means withheld, restricted, fenced off, prohibited; from *ḥ-ẓ-r*, to enclose, to forbid. The gift of God is not restricted to a category of people. Compare 38:9, *“Or do they possess the treasuries of the mercy of thy Lord, the Almighty, the Giver?”* and 52:37, *“Or do they possess the treasuries of thy Lord, or are they the ones in charge?”* The rhetorical point is that no human being holds the keys; the distribution is God’s prerogative, and human beings are not entitled to audit it in advance.

The verse simultaneously dismantles two errors: the prosperity gospel (wealth proves God’s favor) and its mirror (poverty proves God’s favor). Neither is true. Wealth is a gift and a test; poverty is a condition and a test. Compare 89:15–17, which rebukes both inferences in a single breath.

**Why the Address Shifts to “Thy Lord”**

The verse shifts from third person to the second-person address to the Prophet ﷺ — *ʿaṭāʾi rabbik*, “the gift of thy Lord.” Al-Rāzī notes that this personalization is characteristic of passages where the Prophet ﷺ is being consoled. He had watched the wealthy leaders of Quraysh — Abū Jahl, al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, Umayyah ibn Khalaf — flourish while his followers starved. The verse tells him: their provision comes from the same source as yours, and its quantity tells you nothing about their standing.

The same consolation appears at 15:3, *“Leave them to eat and enjoy, and let hope divert them; for they will come to know,”* and at 20:131:

> *“And do not extend thine eyes toward that wherewith We have provided some classes of them — the splendor of the life of this world — that We may test them therein. The provision of thy Lord is better and more lasting.”*

**The Theological Function of Undiscriminating Provision**

If provision tracked righteousness, faith would be compelled: everyone would believe, because belief would visibly pay. The Qur’an is explicit that faith under compulsion is worthless — *“There is no compulsion in religion”* (2:256), and *“If thy Lord had willed, all who are on earth would have believed together. Wouldst thou then compel mankind until they become believers?”* (10:99). For belief to be a free act, its worldly consequences must be indeterminate. The indiscriminate gift is therefore a *condition of the possibility of genuine faith* — a point of considerable philosophical subtlety that al-Rāzī develops.

**The Practical Lesson**

The verse frees the believer from two exhausting preoccupations. The first is the compulsion to interpret other people’s success as a verdict on their character — a habit that produces both envy and self-righteousness. The second is the compulsion to interpret one’s own lack as a verdict on one’s standing. Both interpretations are refused. Provision is a gift, distributed by God, on terms He has not published; it is material for stewardship and testing, not a report card. The believer’s task is not to read the distribution but to use what he has been given.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:21

> **Observe how We have favored some of them over others, and surely the Hereafter is greater in ranking and greater in favor.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Look**

*Unẓur kayfa faḍḍalnā baʿḍahum ʿalā baʿḍ* — “See how We have preferred some of them over others.” The imperative *unẓur* is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to every reader: this is not a proposition to be assented to but a fact to be *observed*. The Qur’an repeatedly appeals to sight in this way — *“Say: Travel in the land and see”* (6:11) — because the inequality of provision is so plainly visible that it requires no argument, only attention.

**The Fact of Gradation**

The verse states as divine action what sociologists describe as a universal: human beings are distributed unequally in wealth, capacity, health, and circumstance. The Qur’an does not treat this as an accident or an injustice to be explained away; it treats it as *faḍl* — a bestowal by God, and therefore as a matter of design. Compare 4:32, *“And covet not that whereby God has favored some of you above others”*; 6:165, *“And He it is who appointed you vicegerents on the earth and raised some of you above others in degrees, that He might try you in what He gave you”*; and 43:32, which al-Rāzī cites here:

> *“Is it they who apportion the mercy of thy Lord? We have apportioned among them their livelihood in the life of this world, and raised some of them above others in rank, that some of them may take others into service. And the mercy of thy Lord is better than what they amass.”*

The last clause of 43:32 is the interpretive key to this verse: the gradation of this world is real but *inferior in kind* to the mercy of God.

**Why Inequality Exists: The Two Qur’anic Answers**

The Qur’an gives two reasons, and both are functional rather than punitive. The first, from 43:32, is mutual dependence: *“that some may take others into service”* — a society in which everyone had identical means would have no division of labor and no occasion for the exchange of services. The second, from 6:165, is testing: *“that He might try you in what He gave you.”* The one with more is tested by abundance; the one with less by want. Neither condition is a verdict; both are examinations with different question papers.

**“The Hereafter Is Greater in Ranking and Greater in Favor”**

*Wa la-l-ākhiratu akbaru darajātin wa akbaru tafḍīlā* — the two comparatives repeat the structure of the verse’s first half and reverse its significance. In this world there are degrees and preferences; in the Hereafter there are *greater* degrees and *greater* preferences. Al-Rāzī explains that the separation there will be absolute — the people of the Garden and the people of the Fire — and the gradations within each will be far more pronounced than anything this world produces.

The Qur’an describes the gradations of the Garden in terms that indicate real distinction: 6:132, *“And for all are degrees according to what they did”*; 20:75, *“And whoever comes to Him as a believer having done righteous deeds — for those are the highest ranks (al-darajāt al-ʿulā)”*; 57:10, *“Not equal among you are those who spent and fought before the conquest. Those are greater in rank than those who spent and fought afterward.”*

**The Consolation and the Warning**

The verse is addressed to those who feel the inequality of this world as a wound — and it is a genuine wound, not a fiction. The Qur’an does not minimize it; it *outbids* it. Whatever the disparity here, it will be exceeded there, and the direction of the disparity will not be the same. The poor believer in this world may occupy a station in the next that no king of this world reaches.

The same verse warns the prosperous: the ranking you enjoy is the smaller one. It will be superseded, and the criteria of the greater ranking are different — not wealth but what you did with it. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (6490) and Muslim (2964):

> *“The people of the Garden will look up at those in the elevated chambers above them as you look at a brilliant star on the horizon — and Abū Bakr and ʿUmar are among them, and how excellent they are.”*

The image is of permanent, visible gradation — and it is presented as a motivation, not a cause of envy.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:22

> **Do not set up another god along with God, lest you sit blameworthy, forsaken.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening of the Moral Code**

Here begins the section the commentators often call the Qur’anic Decalogue — verses 22 through 39, a sequence of commands and prohibitions that Ibn Masʿūd reportedly described as containing the most comprehensive moral instruction in the whole Qur’an. Al-Bukhārī (4626) records that ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd said:

> *“Whoever wishes to look at the will (waṣiyyah) of Muhammad ﷺ upon which is the seal of his ring, let him read these verses of Sūrah Banī Isrāʾīl: ‘Do not set up another god along with God...’ through ’...lest thou be cast into Hell, condemned, banished.’”*

The description “the will with the seal” indicates that Ibn Masʿūd regarded this passage as the Prophet’s ﷺ final testament in condensed form. That it opens with the prohibition of shirk is not arbitrary: in Islamic theology, shirk is the only sin God does not forgive without repentance — *“Truly God forgives not that partners be set up with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills”* (4:48). The moral code is built on a foundation; if the foundation fails, nothing above it holds.

**“Do Not Set Up”**

*Lā tajʿal maʿa Allāhi ilāhan ākhar* — the verb *jaʿala* here means to appoint, to establish, to posit. The prohibition is not against the *existence* of other gods — the Qur’an denies that categorically — but against the human act of *establishing* something as a god. Shirk is not primarily a metaphysical error about how many gods there are; it is a practical act of enthronement. Whatever a person appoints to the position God occupies — as the object of ultimate reliance, ultimate fear, or ultimate love — has been made *ilāh*, whatever he calls it.

The verse appears twice in this sūrah, here and at 39, framing the entire moral code with the same prohibition. The repetition is structural: the code begins and ends with tawḥīd, so that every command in between is understood as an expression of it rather than an independent rule.

**“Lest You Sit Blameworthy, Forsaken”**

*Fa-taqʿuda madhmūman makhdhūlā* — the verb *qaʿada* literally means to sit; idiomatically in Arabic it denotes the state one is left in as a result of something, as in “he ended up sitting disgraced.” *Madhmūm* — blamed, condemned. *Makhdhūl* — abandoned, forsaken, left without support; from *kh-dh-l*, the root of *khidhlān*, the withholding of help.

The two adjectives describe the exact consequences of shirk in this life as well as the next. The one who appoints a created thing to God’s position will find, sooner or later, that it cannot hold him. Wealth fails at bankruptcy; reputation fails at scandal; the powerful patron fails at his own fall. The idolater ends *makhdhūl* — let down by the very thing he trusted. And *madhmūm*: blamed, because having been warned, he cannot plead ignorance.

Note that the same pair of adjectives — *madhmūman madḥūrā*, “blameworthy, banished” — closed verse 18. The seeker of the immediate and the setter-up of partners end in the same state, because they have committed the same act: they have enthroned something created.

**Why Shirk Is Placed First in a Moral Code**

A modern reader may find it strange that a list beginning with prohibitions against murder, adultery, and fraud starts with a theological statement. The classical answer is that shirk is not one item on the list but the condition that makes the list intelligible. Moral commands require a Lawgiver whose authority is ultimate. If ultimate authority is shared, divided, or transferred to a created thing, the commands become negotiable — because the one who has appointed his own god can also unappoint it when inconvenient. Tawḥīd is what makes obedience non-optional.

The practical version of this is visible everywhere. The person whose ultimate concern is his career will violate moral commands when they conflict with it. The person whose ultimate concern is tribal loyalty will violate them for the tribe. The person whose ultimate concern is his own desire will violate them for the desire. Each has an *ilāh ākhar*, and each finds the code negotiable exactly at the point where his god is threatened. The prohibition is first because it is the only one that protects all the others.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:23

> **Thy Lord decrees that you worship none but Him, and be virtuous to parents. Whether one or both of them reaches old age, say not to them “Uff!” nor chide them, but speak unto them a noble word.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Thing God Joins to Himself**

*Wa qaḍā rabbuka allā taʿbudū illā iyyāhu wa bi-l-wālidayni iḥsānā* — “And thy Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him, and that you show *iḥsān* to parents.” The conjunction *wa* places the duty to parents in the same sentence as the duty to God, with no intervening matter. This pairing occurs at 2:83, 4:36, 6:151, 29:8, 31:14, and 46:15 — so consistently that the classical commentators treat it as a fixed Qur’anic formula. Al-Qurṭubī states the reasoning: God is the one who created you and provided for you in the absolute sense; parents are the means through which that creation and provision reached you. To be ungrateful to the means is to be ungrateful to the Source.

**The Meaning of Qaḍā**

*Qaḍā* carries both “decreed” and “commanded.” Al-Ṭabarī takes it as commanded; al-Zamakhsharī notes that it may also carry the sense that in the ultimate order, nothing is truly worshipped but God, as at 13:15, *“And unto God prostrates whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly.”* Both readings agree that the obligation is not advisory. The word implies a settled determination — the same verb used at 17:4 of the decree written in the Book.

**Iḥsān: Beyond Duty**

*Iḥsān* is from *ḥ-s-n*, goodness, beauty. Its verbal form *aḥsana* means to do good, but the Qur’anic usage carries a specific excess: *iḥsān* is goodness beyond what is required. The Qur’an defines it at 16:90: *“Truly God commands justice (ʿadl) and excellence (iḥsān), and giving to kin.”* Justice is giving what is owed; *iḥsān* is giving more than is owed, and giving it beautifully. Applied to parents, it means that fulfilling the minimum — feeding them, housing them, not harming them — is not the standard. The standard is generous, beautiful, willing service.

**“Whether One or Both of Them Reaches Old Age”**

*Immā yablughanna ʿindaka al-kibara aḥaduhumā aw kilāhumā* — the specification of old age is not incidental. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī all note that the verse addresses the precise moment when the relationship inverts: the parent becomes dependent, and the child becomes the provider. The phrase *ʿindaka* — “with you,” “in your care” — makes the situation concrete. This is not about parents in general; it is about *your* parent, aged, *in your house*.

The verse singles out old age because it is when the duty becomes hardest. A child’s love for a parent is easy while the parent is strong and providing. It becomes tested when the parent is weak, repetitive, forgetful, inconvenient, and no longer able to give anything back. The Qur’an locates the command exactly at the point of maximum difficulty, which is where moral commands matter.

**“Say Not to Them ‘Uff!’”**

*Fa-lā taqul lahumā uff* — the word *uff* is an interjection of irritation, a sigh, an “ugh.” Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note its root meaning: something filthy, dirt, the residue under the fingernails. Some commentators therefore read the verse as addressing specifically the disgust a child may feel when cleaning an elderly parent — the most intimate and most testing form of care. Others take it generally: any expression of annoyance, however small.

The exegetes draw a powerful inference from the specific prohibition. Al-Rāzī formulates it as an argument *a fortiori* (*qiyās al-awlā*): if the smallest possible expression of irritation — a sigh, a monosyllable — is forbidden, then raising the voice, insulting, striking, and abandoning are forbidden *all the more*. The Qur’an prohibits the least so that the greater is understood to be included. This method of reasoning is standard in Islamic jurisprudence and the commentators apply it here explicitly.

The converse inference is equally important: the smallest kindness counts. If an “ugh” is registered as a violation, a gentle tone is registered as a virtue. The verse establishes a moral sensitivity at the level of the sigh.

**“Nor Chide Them”**

*Wa lā tanharhumā* — the verb *nahara* means to rebuff, to drive away with a harsh word, to speak sharply. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as turning away in anger or speaking dismissively. The prohibition covers not only what is said but the *manner* of saying it. A child may technically comply with every instruction while delivering compliance in a tone that wounds; the verse closes that loophole.

**“But Speak Unto Them a Noble Word”**

*Wa qul lahumā qawlan karīmā* — *karīm* means noble, generous, honorable; it is one of the Qur’an’s most positive adjectives, used of God Himself (*al-Karīm*), of the Qur’an (56:77), of provision (26:58), of reward (8:4). A *qawl karīm* is speech that honors the person addressed. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as speech combining respect, gentleness, and deference — the way one speaks to someone whose dignity one is anxious to protect.

The verse moves from prohibition to command: do not sigh, do not rebuff, and *do* speak nobly. The negative alone would produce sullen compliance; the positive command produces the intended state of heart.

**The Ḥadīth Literature on Parents**

The Prophetic tradition develops this verse with unusual intensity. Al-Bukhārī (5976) and Muslim (85) record from Abū Hurayrah:

> *“A man came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and said: ‘O Messenger of God, who is most deserving of my good companionship?’ He said: ‘Your mother.’ He said: ‘Then who?’ He said: ‘Your mother.’ He said: ‘Then who?’ He said: ‘Your mother.’ He said: ‘Then who?’ He said: ‘Your father.’”*

Al-Tirmidhī (1898) records:

> *“The pleasure of the Lord is in the pleasure of the parent, and the anger of the Lord is in the anger of the parent.”* (graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ; al-Zamakhsharī cites it at this verse)

And al-Bukhārī (5971) records the ranking of deeds:

> *“I asked the Prophet ﷺ: ‘Which deed is most beloved to God?’ He said: ‘The prayer at its time.’ I said: ‘Then which?’ He said: ‘Then goodness to parents.’ I said: ‘Then which?’ He said: ‘Then striving in the path of God.’”*

That goodness to parents is placed above jihad — the highest physical sacrifice in the tradition — indicates the weight the revelation assigns to it.

**Why Parents and Not Children**

A reader may notice that the Qur’an legislates the child’s duty to the parent far more extensively than the parent’s duty to the child, and the reason is structural rather than arbitrary. Parental love is largely instinctive; filial gratitude is not. A mother’s attachment to her infant requires no legislation — it is carried by biology and by the memory of carrying. A grown child’s patience with a demanding elderly parent requires a command, because the natural current of life runs the other way. The Qur’an legislates where nature is weakest.

**The Modern Relevance**

The verse addresses a situation that has become structurally rare and morally acute. In societies where the aged are placed in institutions, the specific test the verse describes — an aging parent *ʿindaka*, in your care, in your house — is largely outsourced. The outsourcing is not itself forbidden; the Qur’an’s concern is with the state of the heart and the tone of the voice, which persist at whatever distance. The person who visits weekly and sighs has violated *uff*; the person who arranges excellent care and speaks nobly has not. The verse legislates a manner, not a living arrangement.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:24

> **Lower unto them the wing of humility out of mercy and say, “My Lord! Have mercy upon them, as they raised me when I was small.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“The Wing of Humility”: One of the Qur’an’s Great Images**

*Wa akhfiḍ lahumā janāḥa al-dhulli min al-raḥmah* — “and lower to them the wing of humility, out of mercy.” The image is drawn from the bird: a bird lowers its wing over its young to shelter them, and lowers its wing to another bird as a gesture of submission and non-aggression. The Qur’an transfers the gesture to the child before the parent — the reversal is the whole point. The one who was sheltered under a wing now lowers his own.

*Janāḥ* means wing; *dhull* means humility, submissiveness, the state of being lowly without being degraded. The commentators distinguish *dhull* (willing lowness before one worthy of honor) from *dhull* in the sense of humiliation imposed by another. The former is a virtue; the latter is an injury. The Qur’an uses the same word of the believers toward one another at 5:54: *“humble (adhillah) toward the believers, mighty toward the disbelievers.”*

Al-Qurṭubī notes the extremity of the command: the child is instructed to lower himself before his parent as the bird lowers its wing — a posture of total non-assertion. There is no room in this verse for the assertion of the adult child’s superiority in education, income, or worldly success, which is precisely the temptation old age creates.

**“Out of Mercy”**

*Min al-raḥmah* — the qualifier is critical. The lowering of the wing must proceed from mercy, not from obligation, fear, or social expectation. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that mercy here is the child’s tenderness toward the parent’s weakness, and specifically the *memory* of the parent’s tenderness toward his own weakness. The verse establishes an emotional logic: you were once as helpless as they now are, and they did not sigh.

The Qur’an’s ethics rarely rest on rule alone; they rest on recollected experience. The command is made bearable by the memory that makes it obvious.

**The Prescribed Prayer**

*Wa qul rabbi rḥamhumā kamā rabbayānī ṣaghīrā* — “and say: My Lord, have mercy on them as they raised me when I was small.” The verse does not merely command kindness; it supplies the words. *Rabbayānī* is from *r-b-y*, to raise, nurture, cause to grow — the same root as *rabb*, Lord, and *tarbiyah*, upbringing. The child is instructed to acknowledge, in prayer, that his very formation was their work.

The comparative *kamā* — “as” — is remarkable. The child asks God to have mercy on his parents *in the measure of* their nurturing of him. Al-Rāzī observes that this is not a request for equivalence — God’s mercy exceeds any human act — but a request that the mercy be *patterned on* their care: as they were tender with your smallness, be tender with their weakness.

**Even If They Are Disbelievers**

Al-Zamakhsharī records that the duty extends to non-Muslim parents: the child should still pray for their guidance and treat them with kindness. The Qur’anic basis is 31:15: *“And if they strive with thee to make thee associate with Me that of which thou hast no knowledge, then obey them not; but keep company with them in this world kindly (maʿrūfan).”* The theological limit is precise: obedience in shirk is refused; kindness in this world is commanded. And 60:8: *“God forbids you not, with regard to those who fought you not for religion and drove you not from your homes, that you should be kind to them and deal justly with them. Truly God loves the just.”*

The case of Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr is the classical precedent: her mother, a polytheist, came to visit her in Madinah, and Asmāʾ asked the Prophet ﷺ whether she should receive her. He said, “Yes, keep good ties with your mother” (al-Bukhārī 2620; Muslim 1003).

**The Prayer for the Dead Parent**

The verse’s wording is in the present tense and does not restrict itself to the parents’ lifetime, which the commentators use as the basis for the well-established practice of praying for deceased parents. Muslim (2553) records:

> *“When a man dies, his deeds cease except for three: a continuing charity, a knowledge from which benefit is gained, or a righteous child who prays for him.”*

The verse is the scriptural ground of that third category: the child’s prayer continues to benefit the parent after death, which means the duty outlives the person to whom it is owed. This is one of the few obligations in Islamic law that the death of the obligee does not extinguish.

**Why the Verse Pairs Action With Prayer**

Kindness alone would be a behavioral command; prayer alone would be a verbal one. The verse requires both, and the pairing is psychologically astute. Behavior can be performed without feeling, and feeling can be cultivated without effect. The child who lowers the wing but resents it, and the child who prays but neglects, have each failed half the verse. Prayer does something behavior cannot: it places the parent before God, which relocates the relationship out of the economy of exchange. You are no longer repaying a debt to a person who can no longer repay you; you are asking the One who can.

**The Practical Shape of the Verse**

Read as instruction, the verse prescribes three things in a specific order: a posture (lowered, non-assertive), an emotion (mercy arising from memory), and a speech-act (a specific prayer). Anyone caring for an aging parent recognizes how often the difficulty is not the labor but the posture — the resistance to being *lower* than someone you now outrank in every worldly measure. The verse addresses exactly that resistance, and it does so not by command alone but by remembrance: *kamā rabbayānī ṣaghīrā* — as they raised you when you were small. The memory, honestly held, resolves the resistance.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:25

> **Your Lord knows best that which is in your souls. If you are righteous, then verily He is Forgiving toward the penitent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why This Verse Follows the Command to Parents**

The placement is deliberate and the commentators have noted its necessity. Verses 23–24 have just commanded the most demanding form of familial devotion: the lowering of the wing, the noble word, the suppression of the sigh. Any reader who has cared for an aging parent knows what the command provokes — not only effort but *inadequacy*, and sometimes irritation that is felt and immediately regretted. Verse 25 answers before the reader despairs: your Lord knows what is in your soul.

Al-Rāzī explains the connection precisely: God knows that perfect, uninterrupted gentleness toward a demanding parent is beyond most human beings, and He does not condemn the struggle. The verse converts an impossible standard into a livable one — not by lowering the standard but by revealing that the standard includes the repentance.

**“That Which Is in Your Souls”**

*Mā fī nufūsikum* — not merely your actions, but the interior: the flash of annoyance, the unspoken complaint, the resentment that surfaces and is pushed down. The Qur’an asserts God’s knowledge of this interior repeatedly: 2:234, *“And God knows what is in your souls, so beware of Him”*; 2:284, *“Whether you show what is in your souls or conceal it, God will call you to account for it”*; and of the breasts, 3:154, *“And God knows well what is in the breasts”*; and of the heart, 4:63, *“God knows what is in their hearts.”*

The commentators distinguish two things here, and the distinction is theologically important. God knows what is in the soul — including passing thoughts and involuntary feelings. But the Qur’an does not punish the involuntary. Al-Bukhārī (5269) and Muslim (127) record:

> *“God has overlooked for my community what their souls whisper to them, so long as they do not act upon it or speak it.”*

The knowledge in verse 25 is therefore not a threat of punishment for every passing irritation; it is the assurance that God sees the *effort*, the regret, and the intention, none of which are visible in the outward act.

**“If You Are Righteous, He Is Forgiving to the Penitent”**

*In takūnū ṣāliḥīna fa-innahu kāna li-l-awwābīna ghafūrā* — *awwāb* is the intensive form of one who returns; the *awwābūn* are those who turn back repeatedly. The form indicates frequency: not the one who repents once, but the one whose characteristic is returning. The verse is thus addressed specifically to the person who fails, regrets, and tries again — which is the actual condition of nearly every moral life.

Al-Qurṭubī explains *ghafūr* as the one who covers; the root *gh-f-r* denotes covering and protection, as a helmet covers the head. God’s forgiveness is not only the cancellation of the penalty but the covering of the fault, so that it does not disgrace the sinner before others.

**The Theological Balance**

The verse holds two things together that moral systems usually separate: an exacting standard and a wide forgiveness. It does not say “do your best and that is enough,” nor does it say “the standard is perfect and failure is fatal.” It says: the standard is what it is, God knows your interior completely, and if you are among those who return, He is forgiving. The combination is what makes the demanding ethics of verses 22–39 actually livable.

Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said of the *awwāb*: he is the one who does not persist. The definition is not about never falling but about not staying down. This is the practical doctrine of the verse, and it applies far beyond the parent-child relationship — to every obligation in the code that follows.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:26

> **Give unto the kinsman his right, and unto the indigent and the traveler, but do not squander wastefully.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Claimants Named**

*Wa āti dhā al-qurbā ḥaqqahu wa-l-miskīna wa-bna al-sabīl* — “and give the kinsman his right, and the indigent, and the wayfarer.” Three categories, and the order is meaningful. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both note that charity in the Qur’an begins with those nearest and moves outward — the same principle stated at 2:215: *“They ask thee what they should spend. Say: whatever you spend of good is for parents and kinsmen, orphans, the indigent, and the wayfarer.”* And 2:177 includes the same list in the definition of righteousness itself.

The word *ḥaqq* — right — is decisive. The kinsman does not receive charity as a favor; he receives what is *owed*. This transforms the transaction from benevolence to justice, and it removes the giver’s discretion. A favor may be withheld; a right may not.

**The Kinsman: Ṣilat al-Raḥim**

*Al-qurbā* is from *q-r-b*, nearness; *dhū al-qurbā* is the one possessed of nearness — the relative. The duty of maintaining kinship (*ṣilat al-raḥim*) is one of the most heavily emphasized obligations in Islamic ethics. Al-Bukhārī (5988) and Muslim (2556) record:

> *“Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him maintain his ties of kinship.”*

And al-Bukhārī (5989) records the warning attached to it:

> *“No one who severs his ties of kinship will enter the Garden.”*

The word *raḥim* (womb) itself supplies the theology: the tie is named after the organ from which both parties emerged. Severing it is not a social disagreement but a denial of a biological and therefore divinely-established fact. Al-Bukhārī (4830) records the ḥadīth qudsī in which God says:

> *“I am God, and I am al-Raḥmān; I created the raḥim and derived its name from My name. Whoever maintains it, I maintain him; and whoever severs it, I sever him.”*

Some commentators record the view of ʿAlī Zayn al-ʿĀbidīn that *dhā al-qurbā* refers specifically to the kin of the Prophet ﷺ, who were entitled to a share of the spoils and forbidden from receiving ordinary charity. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī report this. The majority reading, however, is general: every relative has a right.

**The Indigent: Al-Miskīn**

The *miskīn* is the one who lacks, from *s-k-n*, stillness — the one whose circumstances have brought him to a standstill. The Qur’an distinguishes *faqīr* and *miskīn* at 9:60, listing both among the recipients of zakāt. The commentators explain the difference variously, but the practical sense is clear: the *miskīn* is the one whose need has become chronic rather than temporary.

**The Wayfarer: Ibn al-Sabīl**

*Ibn al-sabīl* — literally “son of the road” — is the traveler who has run out of means, even if he is wealthy at home. Al-Ṭabarī explains the duty as providing food and lodging. The category is remarkable in a pre-modern context: a legal entitlement for the stranger, based on nothing but his having arrived. In a world without hotels, banks, or embassies, this verse established a system of hospitality as religious obligation — the reason the Muslim world developed the *khān*, the *ribāṭ*, and the *waqf* for travelers.

**“But Do Not Squander Wastefully”**

*Wa lā tubadhdhir tabdhīrā* — *badhdhara* means to scatter, to disperse recklessly. The cognate adverb intensifies. The prohibition is placed inside the command to give, which is unusual and instructive: the Qur’an legislates the *manner* of generosity, not only its existence.

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify the forms this takes: giving excessively beyond one’s means; giving for reputation rather than need; giving to those without a claim while those with a claim go unfed. The third is the most subtle and the most common — the person who gives lavishly to strangers for the appearance of generosity while his own relatives struggle.

The Qur’an’s balance here is stated at 25:67: *“And those who, when they spend, are neither wasteful nor miserly, but stand between that, uprightly.”* The middle is not a compromise between two vices but a distinct virtue with its own name: *qawām*, uprightness.

**Why Generosity Requires Regulation**

An unrestricted command to give would produce three predictable failures: the depletion of the giver’s household, the misdirection of resources to the unworthy, and the corruption of the act by the desire for praise. The verse prevents all three by naming the rightful recipients in order and prohibiting excess. Generosity without discipline is not virtue; it is impulse, and impulse is as likely to harm as to help. The person who gives away the family’s rent to a stranger has not performed charity; he has performed injustice wearing charity’s clothes.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:27

> **Truly the wasteful are the brethren of satans, and Satan is ungrateful to his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“The Brethren of Satans”**

*Inna al-mubadhdhirīna kānū ikhwāna al-shayāṭīn* — the plural *shayāṭīn* is significant: not “the brethren of Satan” but “of the satans.” The Qur’an uses the plural for both jinn and human agents of corruption — 6:112:

> *“And thus We made for every prophet an enemy — satans from among mankind and jinn, inspiring one another with flowery speech in delusion.”*

The wasteful are thus brothered to a class, not to an individual.

*Ikhwān* — brethren — indicates resemblance in character and companionship in action. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as their being friends and supporters of the satans. The word implies more than similarity; it implies affiliation. The one who squanders has joined a party.

**Why Waste Is Brotherhood With Evil**

The connection is not obvious and the commentators work to establish it. Al-Qurṭubī gives the reasoning: waste is the misuse of a trust, and every trust misused is an act of ingratitude; ingratitude is Satan’s defining characteristic; therefore the waster is Satan’s brother in the quality that defines him. The chain is short and each link is Qur’anic.

There is a second, economic link. Resources squandered are resources not available to the *miskīn* and the *ibn al-sabīl* named in the previous verse. Waste is therefore not a private extravagance but a deprivation of the rightful claimant. The one who throws away what another needs has taken it from him.

**“And Satan Is Ungrateful to His Lord”**

*Wa kāna al-shayṭānu li-rabbihi kafūrā* — *kafūr* is the intensive form of *kāfir*, one who covers, conceals, denies; in moral usage, one profoundly ungrateful. The word is chosen with precision. Satan’s original sin, in the Qur’an’s account, was not atheism — he knows God, addresses Him, and asks Him for respite. His sin was *kufr al-niʿmah*: the refusal to acknowledge a favor received. He was created, honored, and admitted to the assembly of angels, and he responded to the command to prostrate by saying: *“I am better than him; Thou createdst me of fire and createdst him of clay”* (7:12).

The verse therefore identifies waste as a species of ingratitude, and ingratitude as the satanic quality. The parallel with verse 3 is exact: Noah was *ʿabdan shakūrā*, a thankful servant; Satan is *li-rabbihi kafūrā*, ungrateful to his Lord. The entire sūrah is framed by this contrast, and every human being is positioned somewhere between the two adjectives.

**The Logic of Gratitude as the Root of Moral Order**

The verse makes a claim of considerable depth: that the moral life rests on the recognition of favor. If a person believes his wealth is entirely self-generated, spending it is his business and no one has a claim on it. If he believes it is a gift held in trust, spending it becomes an accountable act. The entire legislation of zakāt, of the kinsman’s right, of the wayfarer’s entitlement depends on the second belief. Waste is the practical expression of the first.

This is why the Qur’an pairs *shukr* and *kufr* so persistently — 14:7, *“If you are grateful, I will surely increase you; and if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe”*; 76:3, *“We guided him to the way, be he grateful or ungrateful.”* Gratitude is not a sentiment but an epistemological position about the source of what one has, and the practical consequences follow necessarily.

**The Modern Application**

The verse’s target is more visible today than in any previous century. Consumer economies are structurally built on *tabdhīr* — the deliberate production of waste through overpackaging, planned obsolescence, the disposal of edible food, the purchase of what will never be used. The Qur’an’s category is precise enough to diagnose it: waste is not the amount spent but the *proportion to need and to the claims of others*. A wealthy person spending proportionately is not *mubadhdhir*; a poor person spending beyond his means to appear wealthy is. The criterion is internal and relational, not absolute — which is why no threshold can be legislated and why the verse appeals to the heart rather than to a figure.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:28

> **But if thou turnest away from them, seeking a mercy from thy Lord, for which thou dost hope, then speak unto them a gentle word.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About Refusing Without Wounding**

*Wa immā tuʿriḍanna ʿanhumu btighāʾa raḥmatin min rabbika tarjūhā fa-qul lahum qawlan maysūrā* — “and if thou turnest away from them, seeking a mercy from thy Lord that thou hopest for, then say to them a word that is easy.” The verse legislates the situation every generous person eventually faces: the request arrives, the means are not there, and something must be said.

The three elements are ordered with care. First, the turning away — *tuʿriḍ*, a physical gesture of withdrawal. Second, the interior state — *ibtighāʾ raḥmah*, the seeking of a mercy, which is to say that the refusal is not a dismissal but a postponement in hope. Third, the speech — *qawlan maysūrā*, an easy word.

**“Seeking a Mercy From Thy Lord”: Poverty Re-described**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both note the remarkable euphemism. The verse does not say “because you have nothing.” It says “seeking a mercy from thy Lord for which thou hopest.” Poverty is thereby re-framed as a state of expectation rather than of lack. Al-Zamakhsharī draws out the two implications: the condition is *temporary* (one who hopes for something does not regard himself as permanently without it), and the person remains *connected* to the source of provision even while lacking it.

The verse thus gives the poor man a vocabulary that preserves his dignity. He is not a man who cannot give; he is a man awaiting the means to give. This distinction matters enormously in practice, because the shame of inability is what most often produces the harshness the verse prohibits.

**The Prophet’s ﷺ Silence and the Correction of It**

Al-Qurṭubī records that when the Prophet ﷺ was asked for something he did not have, he would remain silent rather than refuse — waiting for provision to arrive rather than saying no. The verse instructs him to replace silence with speech. The correction is instructive: silence, though well-intentioned, leaves the petitioner in uncertainty, and uncertainty is its own injury. The one who has asked needs to know where he stands.

**“A Word That Is Easy”**

*Qawlan maysūrā* — from *y-s-r*, ease; the same root as *yusrun* in the Prophetic maxim *“the religion is easy”* and as *yassirnā* in 54:17:

> *“And We have made the Qur’an easy to remember.”*

An *easy* word is one that does not burden the hearer: it does not lecture, does not moralize, does not make him feel that his asking was an imposition.

Al-Qurṭubī records a second reading of the command: that the Prophet ﷺ should speak *for* them easy words — that is, supplicate on their behalf. On this reading, the one who cannot give materially gives spiritually. The two readings are not in conflict; together they describe the full duty: explain gently, and pray for them.

**The Alternative Occasion of Revelation**

Al-Qurṭubī records a different context: that the verse was revealed after the Prophet ﷺ declined a request from people he knew would use the funds for corruption, so that his refusal was itself a seeking of mercy — the mercy of preventing harm. On this reading the verse covers the refusal that is *not* due to lack of means but to judgment about use. Both situations produce the same command: even the justified refusal requires an easy word.

**Why the Verse Belongs in the Code**

Placed between the prohibition of waste (vv. 26–27) and the prohibition of both miserliness and excess (v. 29), the verse handles the case the surrounding verses create. A person told not to squander will sometimes have to say no. The Qur’an anticipates the psychological danger: the one who learns to refuse may learn to refuse *coldly*. The verse closes that gap. Generosity is not only about what leaves the hand; it is about what enters the other person’s heart.

**Refusing Without Wounding Today**

The situation is constant and the failure is familiar. The colleague who asks for help you cannot give; the relative who asks to borrow; the stranger who asks on the street. The instinctive responses are either a flat refusal that humiliates, or a vague evasion that leaves hope suspended indefinitely. The verse prescribes a third: state the truth, state it easily, and keep the door’s status clear. And where the inability is genuine, pray for the one refused — which costs nothing and is, in the verse’s own logic, a form of giving.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:29

> **And let not thine hand be shackled to thy neck; nor let it be entirely open, lest thou shouldst sit condemned, destitute.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Bodily Images of Economic Vice**

*Wa lā tajʿal yadaka maghlūlatan ilā ʿunuqika wa lā tabsuṭhā kulla al-basṭ* — the verse draws two pictures. The first: a hand bound to the neck — the posture of the miser, unable to reach out even if he wished. The second: a hand stretched out to its full extension — the posture of the one who gives without limit. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr all read the first as the metaphor of miserliness, which the Qur’an condemns elsewhere: *“And whosoever is miserly is only miserly toward himself”* (47:38); and 3:180, *“Let not those who are miserly with what God has given them of His Bounty think that it is good for them. Nay, it is evil for them”*; and 57:24, *“those who are miserly and enjoin miserliness upon mankind.”*

The images are not decorative; they carry the argument. A bound hand is *useless* — the miser’s wealth does nothing, for him or anyone else. An over-extended hand is *unrecoverable* — once fully open, it cannot be closed to protect what remains.

**Why Excess Is Prohibited Alongside Miserliness**

Most moral systems prohibit stinginess and praise generosity without qualification. The Qur’an prohibits both extremes, and the prohibition of excess is stated in the same breath as the prohibition of stinginess. The reason is practical: unmeasured generosity destroys the giver’s capacity to give. The person who has given away his household’s means has not performed charity; he has created two claimants where there was one.

This is why the Qur’an’s ideal is not maximum giving but *qawām* — a standing, a measured middle: *“And those who, when they spend, are neither wasteful nor miserly, but stand between that, uprightly (qawāmā)”* (25:67). The same root gives *qāʾim*, upright, and connects to *aqwam* in verse 9 — the most upright way. Economic virtue is a species of the uprightness the whole sūrah is about.

**“Lest Thou Shouldst Sit Condemned, Destitute”**

*Fa-taqʿuda malūman maḥsūrā* — *malūm* means blamed, reproached; *maḥsūr* means cut off, exhausted, depleted, prevented from moving — from *ḥ-ṣ-r*, to confine or besiege. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī all gloss *maḥsūr* as exhausted or depleted.

Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī distinguish the two failure modes by the source of the blame. The miser is blamed *by those he refused* — the relative, the needy, the petitioner who went away empty. The prodigal is blamed *by himself and by others* once the depletion becomes visible: he is reproached for having ruined his own household through foolishness. Both end *sitting* — a posture of immobility, unable to act, which is precisely what a hand is for.

The image of *sitting* recalls verse 22’s *fa-taqʿuda madhmūman makhdhūlā* and verse 18’s *madhmūman madḥūrā*. The Qur’an uses this verb consistently for the terminal state of moral failure: the person who acted wrongly ends up sitting in the consequence.

**The Islamic Economic Principle Behind the Verse**

The verse is the scriptural basis for a substantial body of jurisprudence. Islamic law prohibits giving away more than one can bear when doing so harms dependents — the rights of the wife, the children, and the parents take precedence over voluntary charity. The Prophet ﷺ asked Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, who wished to bequeath all his wealth:

> *“Two-thirds?” “Too much.” “Half?” “Too much.” “A third?” “A third, and a third is much. Truly, leaving thy heirs wealthy is better than leaving them poor, begging from people.”* (al-Bukhārī 1295; Muslim 1628)

The ḥadīth applies the verse’s logic to a different domain: even posthumous generosity is limited by the claims of the household. The same principle governs the ruling that a person may not give away wealth he owes, nor charity that leaves his family destitute.

**The Psychological Insight**

Both extremes arise from the same defect: an inability to relate to wealth as a *measured trust*. The miser over-values it; the spendthrift under-values it. Neither has achieved the relationship the verse presupposes, in which wealth is a resource with a purpose, distributed according to a hierarchy of claims. The middle is not a tepid compromise but the most difficult position, because it requires constant judgment — each request assessed against the actual state of the purse and the actual hierarchy of need.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:30

> **Truly thy Lord outspreads and straitens provision for whomsoever He will. Verily of His servants He is Aware, Seeing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Control of Provision**

*Inna rabbaka yabsuṭu al-rizqa li-man yashāʾu wa yaqdir* — “thy Lord outspreads provision for whomever He wills, and straitens [it].” The pair of verbs describes the full range: *yabsuṭu*, from *b-s-ṭ*, to spread out, to extend, to make ample; *yaqdir*, from *q-d-r*, to measure, to apportion, to restrict. The same pairing appears at 13:26, 28:82, 29:62, 30:37, 34:36 and 39, 39:52, and 42:12 — a fixed formula asserting that provision is under divine control.

The verb *yabsuṭu* shares its root with the divine Name *al-Bāsiṭ*, the Expander, the Open-Handed — a Name attested in the ḥadīth lists of the ninety-nine though not occurring in the Qur’an as such. The quality it names is expressed in the Qur’an at 5:64:

> *“The Jews say, ‘God’s Hand is shackled.’ Shackled are their hands, and cursed are they for what they said. Nay, but His two Hands are outspread (mabsūṭatān); He spends as He wills.”*

The contrast with verse 29 is deliberate and almost punning. The verse before prohibits the human hand from being *maghlūlah* — shackled to the neck. This verse declares that God’s Hands are *mabsūṭatān* — outspread. Human miserliness is measured against divine generosity and found to be its opposite.

**Why This Verse Follows the Prohibition of the Two Extremes**

The connection is not incidental. Having forbidden both miserliness and excess, the Qur’an grounds the prohibition in theology: the distribution of provision is God’s act, not yours. Al-Rāzī draws out the two consequences. If God expands, you need not hoard — hoarding will not add to what has been apportioned. If God straitens, you cannot force it open — spending beyond your means will not produce provision. Both extremes are therefore not only morally wrong but *futile*, which is a stronger argument than moral prohibition alone.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the underlying principle in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (3339) and Muslim (2643) from Ibn Masʿūd:

> *“Then the angel is sent to him and he breathes the spirit into him, and he is commanded with four words: to write his provision, his life span, his deeds, and whether he is wretched or happy.”*

Provision (*rizq*) is among the decreed matters. This does not license passivity — the same tradition insists on action — but it removes provision from the category of things one can secure by anxiety or by grasping.

**“Verily of His Servants He Is Aware, Seeing”**

*Innahu kāna bi-ʿibādihi khabīran baṣīrā* — the same pair that closed verse 17. The placement here gives it a specific force: God’s awareness of His servants is the *reason* the distribution is what it is. It is not random, not a matter of luck, not the outcome of a purely mechanical competition. It proceeds from knowledge of each servant’s condition.

The classical commentators draw the pastoral conclusion: a person whose provision is straitened should not read it as abandonment, because the One who straitened it knows him. And a person whose provision is expanded should not read it as endorsement, for the same reason. Both readings of one’s own circumstances are forbidden by the same attribute.

**The Verse as Answer to the Complaint of Inequality**

Verse 21 commanded the reader to *look* at the fact of gradation. Verse 30 explains its governance. Together they answer the perennial complaint: the inequality is real, it is not deserved in any simple sense, and it is administered by One who sees. The Qur’an does not resolve the complaint by denying the inequality; it resolves it by locating the inequality within knowledge rather than chance — and by promising, in verse 21, that the greater gradation lies elsewhere.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:31

> **And slay not your children for fear of poverty. We shall provide for them and for you. Surely their slaying is a great sin.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Practice the Verse Addresses**

*Wa lā taqtulū awlādakum khashyata imlāq* — “and do not kill your children for fear of poverty.” The occasion is specific and well documented. Among certain tribes of pre-Islamic Arabia, infanticide was practiced — the burying alive of infant daughters, driven by fear of poverty and of the shame associated with female children. The Qur’an returns to the practice repeatedly, and its eschatological indictment is among the most severe in the Book: 81:8–9, *“And when the buried girl (al-mawʾūdah) is asked: for what sin was she slain?”*

The parallel verse at 6:151 places the same prohibition in a similar list: *“And slay not your children out of poverty — We provide for you and for them.”* The two verses differ in a meaningful detail, which the commentators noticed. In 6:151 the wording is *min imlāq* — “out of poverty,” i.e., from poverty already present — and the assurance is *“We provide for you and for them,”* placing the parents first. Here the wording is *khashyata imlāq* — “for fear of poverty,” i.e., poverty anticipated but not yet present — and the assurance is *“We provide for them and for you,”* placing the children first.

Al-Rāzī and others draw the inference: when poverty is already present, the parents’ need is the pressing concern, so they are mentioned first; when poverty is merely feared, the child’s provision is the concern, so the child is mentioned first. The variation is a small masterpiece of Qur’anic precision.

**The Theological Correction**

The verse does not merely prohibit; it *refutes*. The fear of poverty rests on a false belief about who provides. If you believe the child will consume your provision, you will kill the child. If you understand that the child arrives with provision apportioned by God, the fear dissolves. *Naḥnu narzuquhum wa iyyākum* — “We provide for them and for you.” The subject is God, and the order of provision is His.

This is why the prohibition of infanticide is inseparable from the doctrine of *rizq*. Every act of killing one’s own child for economic reasons presupposes that the parent is the source of sustenance; the verse denies the premise. The Prophet ﷺ named the sin among the gravest. Al-Bukhārī (6871) and Muslim (86) record:

> *“I asked: ‘O Messenger of God, which sin is greatest with God?’ He said: ‘That thou make a rival for God while He created thee.’ I said: ‘Then which?’ He said: ‘That thou slay thy child for fear that he will eat with thee.’”*

The two sins are joined — shirk and infanticide — because the second flows from the first: to believe that provision comes from anywhere but God is already to have set up a rival.
