**“Surely Their Slaying Is a Great Sin”**

*Inna qatlahum kāna khiṭʾan kabīrā* — *khiṭʾ* means a sin, a wrong, an error of grave kind; *kabīr* means great. The verse closes with a flat declaration, unqualified by any mitigating circumstance. The Qur’an uses *kabīr* for sin in only a few places, most notably 2:219 on intoxicants and gambling — *“in them is great sin (ithmun kabīr) and some benefit for men, but their sin is greater than their benefit.”* The classification places infanticide among the *kabāʾir*, the major sins, in the technical vocabulary of Islamic law.

**The Extension of the Principle**

The classical jurists extended the verse’s principle well beyond the Arabian practice. It is the primary textual basis for the prohibition of abortion, debated in the schools with respect to timing and to cases of genuine necessity — a discussion the verse itself does not settle, but which the schools conduct within its frame. It is also invoked against the modern forms of the same logic: the refusal to have children for economic reasons is not, in Islamic law, the same as killing them, but the verse’s diagnosis — fear of poverty as a motive for refusing life — is what the commentators apply.

The deeper application is to every situation in which a human being is treated as an economic liability. The aged parent whose care is costly, the disabled child whose upbringing is expensive, the dependent relative who consumes resources — the logic the verse refutes is not confined to infants. Its premise is that a person’s value is measured by his consumption, and its answer is that provision comes from God, not from the household budget.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:32

> **And approach not adultery; verily it is indecency and an evil way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“Approach Not”: The Grammar of Prevention**

*Wa lā taqrabū al-zinā* — “and do not *approach* unlawful sexual intercourse.” The verb is not “do not commit” (*lā tafʿalū*) but “do not come near” (*lā taqrabū*). The choice is one of the Qur’an’s most consequential legal-linguistic decisions, and the commentators treat it as deliberate. Al-Qurṭubī explains that prohibiting the approach prohibits the means as well as the end: the glance, the seclusion, the conversation, the arrangement of circumstances. A prohibition on the act alone could be observed while every road to it remained open; a prohibition on approach closes the roads.

The same construction appears at 2:187 regarding the fast, at 2:229 regarding transgressing limits, and at 6:151 in the parallel list. It reflects the Islamic legal maxim *sadd al-dharāʾiʿ* — blocking the means — which holds that what leads to the forbidden is itself forbidden.

**Zinā: The Scope of the Term**

*Zinā* designates sexual intercourse outside a valid marriage. The Qur’an legislates it at 24:2–3 with corporal penalties, and the Prophetic tradition extends the concept to the lesser forms. Al-Bukhārī (6243) and Muslim (2657) record:

> *“The eyes commit zinā, and their zinā is the look; the ears commit zinā, and their zinā is listening; the tongue commits zinā, and its zinā is speech; the hand commits zinā, and its zinā is the touch; the foot commits zinā, and its zinā is the step; and the heart desires and wishes — and the private part confirms that or denies it.”*

The ḥadīth does not equate these with the legal sin; it explains the *mechanism* by which the legal sin is approached — which is precisely what the verse’s verb prohibits.

**“Verily It Is an Indecency and an Evil Way”**

*Innahu kāna fāḥishatan wa sāʾa sabīlā* — *fāḥishah* is from *f-ḥ-sh*, exceeding measure; it denotes that which is grossly excessive, shameful, obscene. The Qur’an applies the term to zinā here, to the sin of Lot’s people at 7:80, and to stinginess at 2:268 — *“Satan threatens you with poverty and commands you to indecency (bi-l-faḥshāʾ).”* The word indicates a transgression that exceeds not only law but the natural measure of things.

*Wa sāʾa sabīlā* — “and evil is it as a way.” The noun *sabīl* means path, road. The Qur’an characteristically describes moral states as roads: the straight path (*al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*), the way of error, the way of those who earned wrath. Calling zinā an *evil way* rather than an evil act makes a claim about trajectory: it is not an isolated event but a road with a direction and a destination. The one who enters it finds that it goes somewhere, and that the somewhere is worse than the beginning.

**Why the Verse Is Placed Between the Prohibitions of Killing**

The sequence is striking: verse 31 forbids killing one’s children; verse 32 forbids zinā; verse 33 forbids killing the inviolable soul. The commentators note the internal logic. Zinā destroys lineage (*nasab*), which is one of the five things Islamic law exists to protect — religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property. A child born of uncertain paternity is, in the pre-modern world, without inheritance, without protection, without standing. The destruction of lineage is a form of social killing, and it is placed between two literal killings for that reason.

Al-Ghazālī and the jurists after him organized the whole of Islamic law around these five protected interests, and verses 31–34 of this sūrah cover three of them in order: life (31), lineage (32), life again (33), and property (34). The sequence is not arbitrary; it is a compressed legal theory.

**The Social Logic of the Prohibition**

The Qur’an does not argue for sexual restraint on the basis of sentiment but on the basis of consequence. Zinā is *fāḥishah* — an excess beyond measure — because it separates the sexual act from the structure of responsibility that makes its consequences bearable. The child produced has claims; the act that produced it created no one obligated to meet them. The prohibition is therefore not primarily about the body but about the *unmet claims* the act generates.

The modern reader who finds the prohibition harsh should note what the verse does *not* say. It does not describe desire as evil, does not describe the body as unclean, and does not treat the sinner as beyond recovery — the Qur’an’s account of repentance is open to all, and 25:68–70 lists zinā among the sins of those who, upon repenting and believing, *“God will change their evil deeds into good.”* The prohibition is on the *way*, not on the person, and the way back is explicitly available.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:33

> **And slay not the soul that God has made inviolable, save by right. And whosoever is slain unjustly, We have appointed authority unto his heir. Then let him not be excessive in slaying. Verily he shall be helped.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inviolable Soul**

*Wa lā taqtulū al-nafsa allatī ḥarrama Allāhu illā bi-l-ḥaqq* — “and do not kill the soul that God has made *ḥarām* except by right.” The verb *ḥarrama* means to make forbidden, sacred, inviolable; the same root gives *ḥaram*, the sacred precinct, and *iḥrām*, the pilgrim’s consecrated state. To call a soul *muḥarramah* is to place it in the category of the sacred — not because of anything it has done but because God has so designated it.

The parallel is 6:151, and the general principle is stated at 5:32 in the verse that has become the Qur’an’s most quoted statement on the value of life:

> *“Whoever slays a soul, not for a soul or for corruption in the land, it is as if he had slain all mankind; and whoever saves a life, it is as if he had saved all mankind.”*

**“Save by Right”: The Exception and Its Limits**

*Illā bi-l-ḥaqq* — the exception is narrow and legally specified. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr identify the cases: capital punishment for murder after due process, and the punishments prescribed for waging war against God and His Messenger (5:33). The exception is defined by the Law, not by private judgment — a limitation of enormous importance in a society in which blood vengeance was customary.

The Prophet ﷺ specified the categories in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (6878) and Muslim (1676):

> *“The blood of a Muslim who testifies that there is no god but God and that I am the Messenger of God is not lawful except in one of three: a life for a life, a married person who commits adultery, and the one who abandons his religion and separates from the community.”*

**“We Have Appointed Authority Unto His Heir”**

*Fa-qad jaʿalnā li-waliyyihi sulṭānā* — *walī* means the near relative, the protector, the one with standing; *sulṭān* means authority, warrant, power. Al-Zamakhsharī explains *walī* as the relative close enough to the deceased to claim retribution. The authority granted has three possible exercises, as Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī all state: to demand execution of the murderer in retaliation (*qiṣāṣ*), to pardon, or to accept the blood-money (*diyah*).

The granting of *choice* is the crucial feature. Pre-Islamic Arabia operated on mandatory vengeance, typically extended to the tribe. The verse transfers the decision to a specific individual and makes it discretionary. Al-Bukhārī (2434) records the Prophet’s ﷺ encouragement of the merciful option:

> *“Whoever has a right of retaliation and he pardons, it is an expiation for him.”*

And the Qur’an itself recommends pardon at 2:178: *“But whosoever is pardoned somewhat by his brother, let the following be honorable and the payment be with kindness”* — and at 5:45, *“And whosoever judges by what God has revealed, those are the righteous,”* after stating *“a life for a life... and whosoever forgoes it in charity, it is an expiation for him.”*

The historical use of the verse is recorded by Ibn Kathīr: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ invoked it at the arbitration following Ṣiffīn (37/658) to argue that Muʿāwiyah, as *walī* of the slain Caliph ʿUthmān, possessed *sulṭān* — political authority. The exegetes record the argument and generally decline to endorse the extension of *sulṭān* from the right of retribution to the caliphate.

**“Let Him Not Be Excessive in Slaying”**

*Fa-lā yusrif fī al-qatl* — the prohibition of *isrāf*, excess, is the verse’s most socially transformative clause. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as the requirement that retaliation fall only on the actual perpetrator and not extend to his family or tribe. Al-Qurṭubī adds the further prohibition: the condemned must not be tortured, maimed, or mutilated before execution.

This clause abolished the mechanism by which a single homicide produced decades of tribal war. In the pre-Islamic practice, the killing of one man could justify the killing of his kinsman of equal or greater status, escalating indefinitely. The verse terminates the chain at the individual. Compare 2:194, *“And transgress not; truly God loves not the transgressors,”* and 5:45, which sets the retaliation as an equal exchange.

**“Verily He Shall Be Helped”**

*Innahu kāna manṣūrā* — the pronoun is read by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī as referring to the heir: he is supported by the Law, by the community, and by God in his claim. Others read it as the slain person, whose cause is not lost. Either reading makes the same point: the victim is not abandoned. The verse’s assurance is directed at the family of the murdered — the people most likely to take the law into their own hands precisely because they feel no one else will act.

**The Modern Significance**

The verse establishes three principles that remain contested everywhere: that life is inviolable by default, that the exception must be legal rather than private, and that even the legal exception must not exceed the perpetrator. Any system in which punishment falls on family members, in which retaliation is unlimited, or in which killing is decided by the aggrieved party rather than by law has departed from all three. The verse is the Qur’an’s compact theory of criminal justice, and its central achievement is the substitution of proportion for escalation.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:34

> **And approach not the orphan’s property, save in the most virtuous manner, till he reaches maturity. And fulfill the pact; surely the pact is called to account.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Again, “Approach Not”**

*Wa lā taqrabū māla al-yatīm* — the same verb used of zinā in verse 32. The prohibition is on *approach*, not merely on consumption, and the commentators apply it accordingly: the guardian may not place himself in a position where the orphan’s wealth is within his casual reach. Al-Qurṭubī explains that this forbids not only theft but commingling, speculative investment without safeguards, and any arrangement in which the guardian’s interest could conflict with the orphan’s.

The Qur’an’s concern for orphans is among its most persistent themes. It appears in 2:83, 177, 215, 220; 4:2–10; 6:152; 8:41; 89:17; 93:9; and 107:2, where the one who *“repulses the orphan”* is numbered among those who deny the religion. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (5304) and Muslim (2983):

> *“I and the one who supports the orphan will be in the Garden like this” — and he indicated with his index and middle fingers, separating them slightly.*

And al-Bukhārī (6011) records the pairing of the sin:

> *“Avoid the seven destructive sins... associating partners with God, sorcery, slaying the soul God has forbidden except by right, consuming usury, consuming the orphan’s property, fleeing on the day of battle, and slandering chaste, heedless, believing women.”*

**“Save in the Most Virtuous Manner”**

*Illā bi-llatī hiya aḥsan* — the exception is not a permission to use but a standard for handling. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī specify the permitted cases: where the property is land or animals, the guardian may use it in a way that maintains rather than depletes it; an indigent guardian may take what is strictly necessary; and the guardian may trade the property in order to increase it. The governing principle is that every act must be *aḥsan* — better than the alternative.

The Qur’an legislates the same at 6:152, *“And approach not the orphan’s property save in the most virtuous manner, till he reaches his strength,”* and at 4:6, *“And test the orphans until they reach marriageable age; then if you perceive in them sound judgment, deliver to them their property, and consume it not wastefully and hastily, [anticipating] that they will grow up.”* That last clause — *badāran an yakbarū* — captures the specific temptation the verse addresses: the guardian who spends the orphan’s wealth quickly, *before the orphan grows up and asks for it*.

**“Till He Reaches Maturity”**

*Ḥattā yablugha ashuddah* — *ashudd* denotes the fullness of strength, physical and mental maturity. The Qur’an makes the transfer of property conditional on demonstrated capacity, not on age alone, which is why 4:6 requires *testing*. This is a genuinely advanced piece of legislation: the orphan’s wealth is protected until the orphan is *competent*, not merely adult.

**“And Fulfill the Pact”**

*Wa awfū bi-l-ʿahd* — the command shifts abruptly from orphans to covenants, and the shift is meaningful: guardianship *is* a covenant. The guardian undertook a trust, and the verse treats the undertaking as binding. The Qur’an commands the fulfillment of pacts throughout: 2:40, *“O Children of Israel, remember My favor... and fulfill My covenant, that I may fulfill your covenant”*; 5:1, *“O you who believe, fulfill the contracts (al-ʿuqūd)”*; 16:91, *“And fulfill the covenant of God when you have made a covenant, and break not oaths after confirming them”*; 8:58; 9:4; 13:20; 23:8; 33:15; 70:32.

**“Surely the Pact Is Called to Account”**

*Inna al-ʿahda kāna masʾūlā* — the construction is grammatically striking: the *pact* is described as the thing questioned, not the person. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī explain it as a rhetorical intensification — the covenant itself will be brought forward and asked, as though it were a witness. The device personalizes the obligation: the promise you made is not a passing speech-act but an entity that survives you and will testify.

Compare 24:24, where the tongue and hands testify, and 41:20–22, where the skin testifies. The Qur’an consistently imagines the instruments of human action becoming witnesses. The promise is added to that list — which means the casual commitment, the verbal assurance, the contract signed without reading, all acquire an eschatological weight that ordinary life does not assign them.

**The Two Commands as One**

Orphan’s property and covenants seem unrelated until one sees the common thread: both are *trusts held against a future claim*. The orphan’s wealth is held against the day he matures; the covenant is held against the day it is called for. In both cases the holder is tempted by the fact that no one is currently watching and no one can currently enforce. The verse addresses precisely that temptation, and its answer is the same in both cases: the accounting is coming, and the covenant itself will be asked.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:35

> **And give full measure when you measure, and weigh with the straight balance. That is better and more virtuous in the end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Marketplace as a Site of Revelation**

*Wa awfū al-kayla idhā kiltum wa zinū bi-l-qisṭāsi al-mustaqīm* — “and give full measure when you measure, and weigh with the straight balance.” The verse places commercial honesty inside a moral code that begins with tawḥīd and ends with the prohibition of shirk. The placement is a claim: the marketplace is not a morally neutral zone governed by its own rules. What a man does with his scales is of the same order as what he does with his prayer.

The Qur’an returns to this command with remarkable persistence. Sūrah al-Muṭaffifīn — “The Defrauders” — is named for it and opens with a woe:

> *“Woe to the defrauders, who, when they take measure from mankind, take it in full; but when they measure or weigh for them, they diminish. Do they not think that they will be resurrected for a tremendous Day?”* (83:1–5)

And Shuʿayb’s mission to Midian was substantially economic: *“O my people, give full measure and weight with justice, and withhold not from mankind their goods, and do not act corruptly in the land, making mischief”* (11:85). And again at 26:181–183. And 55:8–9 makes it a cosmic principle: *“And the heaven He raised, and He set the balance, that you not transgress in the balance — so establish the weight with justice and diminish not the balance.”*

**Al-Qisṭās al-Mustaqīm**

*Qisṭās* is a loanword, likely from Greek, meaning balance or scale; *mustaqīm* is the straight, the upright — the same root as *ṣirāṭ mustaqīm*, the straight path of the Fātiḥah, and connected to *aqwam* in verse 9. The pairing of the *straight* balance with the *straight* path is not accidental in a sūrah that opened by describing the Qur’an as guiding to what is *most upright*. Justice in the marketplace and uprightness in the soul are the same quality expressed in different media.

**Two Forms of the Same Fraud**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain that the command covers both directions: giving short measure when selling, and demanding excess when buying. The Muṭaffifīn passage makes the asymmetry explicit — the defrauder takes in full and gives short. This is the exact structure of most economic dishonesty: the party with more information or more power adjusts the measure in his favor, and the other party cannot verify.

The verse’s generalization is important: it applies not only to physical scales but to every exchange in which what is given and what is received are meant to correspond. Al-Qurṭubī and later commentators extend it to wages, to contracts, to the description of goods, to the concealment of defects. The modern forms — misrepresentation in advertising, hidden fees, the undisclosed conflict of interest, the wage paid late or short — fall under the same command because they are the same act: a manipulated measure.

**“That Is Better and More Virtuous in the End”**

*dhālika khayrun wa aḥsanu taʾwīlā* — *taʾwīl* means outcome, final interpretation, where the matter eventually arrives; from *a-w-l*, to return. The phrase means: this is better, and better in its *outcome*. The verse makes a claim about the long-run consequences of honesty — that the fair dealer’s position is ultimately better even in worldly terms, and certainly in the accounting.

The claim is counterintuitive enough to require the argument. In the short run, the defrauder profits. The Qur’an does not deny this; it disputes its *duration*. Compare 3:185, *“And what is the life of this world but the enjoyment of delusion,”* and 30:39, *“And whatever you give of usury that it may increase in the wealth of mankind, it increases not with God.”* The economics of the Qur’an run on a longer horizon than the quarter.

**Why the Verse Belongs in This Code**

Verses 31–34 covered life, lineage, and property. Verse 35 covers the mechanism by which property changes hands. A society that protects life and property but permits fraudulent exchange has protected nothing, because the fraudulent exchange accomplishes by stealth what theft accomplishes by force. The verse is thus not a commercial appendix to the moral code but its economic enforcement mechanism.

**The Practical Weight**

The command is unusually testable. A person can deceive himself about almost any virtue; he cannot easily deceive himself about whether he gave short measure. This is why the ḥadīth tradition attaches such weight to the marketplace. Al-Bukhārī (2079) records:

> *“The truthful, trustworthy merchant is with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs.”* (al-Tirmidhī 1209, graded ḥasan)

The rank is extraordinary — the honest trader placed with the prophets — and it exists because commercial honesty is performed daily, under continuous temptation, in private, and with immediate material cost. It is therefore among the most reliable indicators of character available.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:36

> **And pursue not that whereof you have no knowledge. Truly hearing, and sight, and the heart—all of these will be called to account.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Governs Speech, Inquiry, and Belief**

*Wa lā taqfu mā laysa laka bihi ʿilm* — “and do not follow what you have no knowledge of.” The verb *taqfū* is from *q-f-w*, to follow on the heels, to track, to pursue — the same root as *qāfah*, a tracker who reads footprints. The image is of someone following a trail without knowing where it leads or whether it is a trail at all.

Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify three applications, all of which the verb supports.

The first is *speech*: do not say what you do not know. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as the prohibition of false testimony and of claiming knowledge one does not possess. The Qur’an makes the same point with devastating force in the slander of ʿĀʾishah: *“When you received it with your tongues and said with your mouths that of which you had no knowledge, and thought it a small matter — but it is great in the sight of God”* (24:15).

The second is *inquiry*: do not investigate private matters in order to expose the faults of others. This connects to 49:12, *“And spy not (wa lā tajassasū), nor let some of you slander others.”* The tracker’s instinct — to follow another’s trail to find what he has hidden — is prohibited.

The third is *following*: do not follow a path or a leader without knowing whether he will guide or mislead. Al-Zamakhsharī reads the verse as a rebuke of *taqlīd*, unthinking imitation in matters of belief. The commentators note the careful distinction: in law, the non-specialist necessarily follows the rulings of the qualified; in creed, blind following is not permitted, because belief requires knowledge.

**The Debate Over Speculation**

Al-Rāzī records an argument among the jurists about whether this verse prohibits reasoning by analogy (*qiyās*) in law, since analogy involves a degree of inference beyond certain knowledge. The majority responded that many necessary determinations — the direction of the qiblah from a distant location, the issuance of a non-binding legal opinion — inherently involve estimation, and that the verse prohibits following *without* knowledge, not reasoning *toward* knowledge. The distinction is between conjecture that has been examined and conjecture that has not.

**The Three Faculties**

*Inna al-samʿa wa-l-baṣara wa-l-fuʾāda kullu ulāʾika kāna ʿanhu masʾūlā* — “truly hearing, and sight, and the heart — each of those will be questioned about it.” The Qur’an names these three as the instruments of knowledge repeatedly: 16:78, *“And God brought you forth from the wombs of your mothers knowing nothing, and gave you hearing and sight and hearts, that perhaps you would be grateful”*; 23:78; 32:9; 67:23:

> *“Say: He is Who created you and made for you hearing and sight and hearts. Little thanks you give.”*

The three are gifts for which gratitude is owed, and they can be withdrawn: 2:7, *“God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and upon their sight is a covering”*; 6:46; 45:23, *“God has sealed his heart and his hearing, and set a covering over his sight.”* The faculties are not automatically reliable; they are accountable instruments that can be corrupted by misuse.

**“Each of Those Will Be Questioned”**

The pronoun in *ʿanhu masʾūlā* admits two readings, and both are recorded. The first: the person will be questioned about each faculty — how did you use your hearing, your sight, your heart? The second: the faculties themselves will be questioned, as witnesses. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī both preserve the second, connecting it to the testimony of the limbs at 24:24 and 41:20–22.

The second reading is the more unsettling and the more precise for the verse’s purpose. If the faculties testify, then the ear that listened to slander, the eye that sought what it should not see, and the heart that entertained what it should not entertain will each give an account. The instruments are not neutral channels; they are participants.

**Why This Verse Is in the Moral Code**

Placed between the command of honest weights (v. 35) and the prohibition of arrogance (v. 37), the verse addresses the epistemic vices that sit between commercial and personal ones. False testimony destroys justice as false measure destroys trade; arrogant bearing proceeds from a heart that claims to know more than it does. The sequence treats the mind’s honesty as a moral obligation of the same rank as the hand’s honesty.

**The Contemporary Urgency**

No verse in this sūrah is more directly addressed to the modern condition. An information environment in which assertion is costless, in which a claim travels faster than its verification, and in which the sharing of the unverified is a single tap, is a mass implementation of *qafw* — following what one has no knowledge of. The verse’s prohibition is not against ignorance; it is against *acting as though one knows*. The person who says “I don’t know” has complied; the person who forwards, asserts, or accuses on the basis of a headline has not.

The verse supplies both the rule and its enforcement mechanism. The rule: do not pursue what you do not know. The mechanism: your hearing, your sight, and your heart will be asked. The one who scrolls, watches, and inwardly entertains is not performing a neutral act; he is using accountable instruments, and the accounting will include how they were used.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:37

> **And walk not exultantly upon the earth; surely thou shalt not penetrate the earth, nor reach the mountains in height.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prohibition of the Arrogant Gait**

*Wa lā tamshi fī al-arḍi maraḥā* — “and do not walk upon the earth *maraḥan*.” The noun *maraḥ* denotes exultant, self-conceited walking — the gait of one pleased with himself. The parallel is Luqmān’s counsel to his son at 31:18: *“And turn not thy cheek from mankind in disdain, nor walk upon the earth exultantly (maraḥā). Truly God loves not any arrogant boaster.”* And the contrasting description of the servants of the Compassionate at 25:63: *“And the servants of the Compassionate are those who walk upon the earth gently (hawnā).”*

The Qur’an legislates a *gait* — not a feeling but a manner of moving the body through public space. The choice is characteristically concrete: arrogance is most legible in how a person walks, and the command is aimed at the visible sign because the visible sign shapes the interior.

**Why God Prohibits a Way of Walking**

A reader may wonder why a revealed book concerns itself with posture. The commentators’ answer is that the body is not separate from the soul; it trains it. Al-Rāzī notes that the Qur’an addresses both directions of the relationship — the interior producing the exterior (arrogance producing the arrogant walk) and the exterior reinforcing the interior (the arrogant walk deepening the arrogance). Prohibiting the gait is a practical means of prohibiting the state.

This is why the Qur’an’s ethics include so much physical detail: the lowering of the gaze, the moderation of the voice (31:19, *“And lower thy voice; truly the most repulsive of voices is the voice of the donkey”*), the humble walk. Each is a small, repeatable, publicly observable act whose repetition forms character.

**“Thou Shalt Not Penetrate the Earth”**

*Innaka lan takhriqa al-arḍ* — the verb *kharaqa* means to pierce, to cut through, to traverse. Al-Rāzī explains the sense: however forcefully you strike the ground with your feet, you will not break through it. The earth is not impressed by your walking. The image is comic and deflating in exactly the right measure — the arrogant walker is, from the earth’s perspective, irrelevant.

**“Nor Reach the Mountains in Height”**

*Wa lan tablugha al-jibāla ṭūlā* — and you will not attain the mountains in stature. The comparison is with the fixed, massive, immovable features of the landscape. The one who walks as though he were enormous is, next to a mountain, nothing. The verse’s rhetorical method is *scale*: it does not argue against arrogance, it simply places the arrogant person in a landscape where his size is obvious.

This is a recurring Qur’anic technique. 40:57, *“The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most mankind know not.”* And 79:27–29, *“Are you more difficult to create, or the heaven? He built it, raised its height, and ordered it.”* The cure for self-importance offered by the Qur’an is not a lecture on humility but a look out of the window.

**The Prophetic Example**

Al-Ṭabarī preserves in his *Taʾrīkh* the description composed by ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib of the Prophet’s ﷺ manner of walking: he walked energetically, leaning forward as though descending a slope, but never proudly. The description is important because it distinguishes two things a casual observer conflates: *vigor* and *arrogance*. The prohibition is not on confident, purposeful movement but on movement that performs superiority. The Prophet ﷺ walked with urgency — the urgency of a man with work to do — and without display.

Al-Tirmidhī (2458) records the sharpest Prophetic statement on the subject:

> *“Whoever has the weight of a mustard seed of pride (kibr) in his heart will not enter the Garden.”* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 91)

And Muslim (91) records the definition: *“Pride is rejecting the truth and despising people.”* The definition is behavioral, not emotional — it identifies arrogance by what it *does*, which is why the verse can legislate a walk.

**The Verse’s Place in the Code**

The code ends with arrogance because arrogance is the condition that makes all the other commandments negotiable. The one who does not regard himself as bound will not honor the orphan’s property, will not give full measure, will not refrain from following what he does not know. The sequence descends from the theological root (v. 22) through the family (vv. 23–25), through wealth (vv. 26–30), through life and lineage (vv. 31–33), through trust and exchange (vv. 34–35), through the mind (v. 36), and arrives at the character trait that governs them all. Verse 38 will then pronounce the whole set loathsome, and verse 39 will name it wisdom and return to tawḥīd.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:38

> **The evil of all this is loathsome unto thy Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Verdict Over Eighteen Verses**

*Kullu dhālika kāna sayyiʾuhu ʿinda rabbika makrūhā* — “all of that — its evil — is, with thy Lord, hateful.” The demonstrative *dhālika* gathers the entire preceding sequence. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī specify the referent as the sins warned against in verses 31–37: infanticide, adultery, murder, misappropriation of the orphan’s wealth, fraudulent measure, speaking without knowledge, and arrogant bearing. Some extend it to the whole code from verse 22.

The pronoun *sayyiʾuhu* — “its evil” — is grammatically notable. Al-Rāzī explains that the phrase means: all of that, insofar as it is evil, is hateful to God. The construction avoids implying that everything mentioned is evil in every respect; the *evil* of it is what is hateful.

**Makrūh: The Language of Divine Dislike**

*Makrūh* is the passive participle of *kariha*, to dislike, to find hateful. The verse attributes *dislike* to God — an affective term, and one the classical theologians handled with care. The standard Sunni reading, articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah and others, is that the divine attributes are real and not reducible to created affect: God’s dislike is a true attribute whose *reality* is not like ours, and whose *consequence* for the creature is what matters. The verse is therefore not merely a statement of legal prohibition; it declares a divine disposition. The prohibited acts are not arbitrary taboos but things that God finds repugnant.

The Qur’an uses the same vocabulary of divine love and dislike extensively: *“God loves not the transgressors”* (2:190), *“God loves not the arrogant boaster”* (4:36), *“God loves not any ungrateful sinner”* (2:276), against *“God loves the patient”* (3:146), *“God loves those who purify themselves”* (9:108). The moral universe of the Qur’an is not indifferent; it is structured by divine love and divine aversion.

The Qur’an states the pairing of divine love and divine aversion in a single verse, closing a comprehensive list of commands with the disclaimer:

> *“Truly God loves not one who is a vainglorious boaster.”* (4:36)

**Why a Summary Verdict Is Needed**

The code of verses 22–37 has moved through diverse domains — theology, family, wealth, life, commerce, speech, bearing. Without a unifying verdict, a reader might conclude that these are unrelated rules of varying weight. Verse 38 unifies them: they are all *makrūh* to the same Lord, in the same way. Infanticide and short measure stand under one description. This is a claim of considerable moral seriousness — it refuses to rank economic dishonesty far below violence.

**The Address to “Thy Lord”**

The shift to the second person — *ʿinda rabbika*, “with thy Lord” — is directed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to the reader. Al-Rāzī notes that this personal form is used in passages of consolation and reassurance. The one who keeps this code will find himself often at odds with the surrounding culture — which practiced infanticide, defrauded in trade, and prized the arrogant gait. The verse assures him that the divergence is not a defect: what his society considers normal, his Lord considers loathsome.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:39

> **That is from the wisdom thy Lord has revealed unto thee. Do not set up another god along with God, lest thou be cast into Hell, condemned, banished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Naming the Whole: Ḥikmah**

*Dhālika mimmā awḥā ilayka rabbuka min al-ḥikmah* — “that is of what thy Lord has revealed to thee of wisdom.” The demonstrative now covers the entire code of verses 22–39, and it names it *ḥikmah* — wisdom. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī both note the significance of the designation: these are not merely commands but *wisdom*, meaning truths accessible to the intellect as well as to revelation.

Al-Rāzī develops the point at length: the inherent rightness of these injunctions is evident to reason, which is why they are found in every religion and every creed, and why they are never abrogated. Unlike ritual details, which vary between revelations, the moral core does not change. A report preserved by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī states that all of these injunctions were found in the tablets given to Moses — the Qur’an is claiming continuity with the Torah at exactly the point where it has been discussing the Children of Israel.

This is the basis for the classical doctrine that the moral law is both revealed and rational — the position of the Māturīdīs and of many Muʿtazilīs, and substantially shared by the Ashʿarīs with respect to the *content* if not the *obligation*. Ibn Taymiyyah argued the same conclusion by a different route: revelation and sound reason never conflict, because both issue from the same source.

**The Frame: Tawḥīd at Both Ends**

Al-Zamakhsharī’s observation is the key to the section’s architecture. Verse 22 opened with *“Do not set up another god along with God”*; verse 39 closes with the identical words. The code is enclosed by tawḥīd, because — in al-Zamakhsharī’s phrase — knowledge of God’s Oneness is the beginning and foundation of all wisdom. Everything between the two verses is an elaboration of what it means to have one God.

The logic is not merely rhetorical. Each command in the code fails without tawḥīd for a specific reason. Kindness to parents fails when the parent’s approval becomes the ultimate standard. Honest measure fails when wealth is the ultimate good. Chastity fails when desire is the ultimate authority. Truthfulness fails when reputation is the ultimate concern. Humility fails when the self is the ultimate reference. In each case, the commandment is broken precisely at the point where something created has been enthroned — which is what shirk is.

The gravity of the code’s foundation is stated in the Qur’an’s most cited formulation of what is not forgiven:

> *“Truly God forgives not that partners be set up with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills.”* (4:48)

**“Lest Thou Be Cast Into Hell, Condemned, Banished”**

*Fa-tulqā fī jahannama malūman madḥūrā* — *tulqā* is the passive of *alqā*, to be thrown, cast, hurled. The verb conveys violence and indifference: the one who commits shirk is not escorted but thrown. *Malūm* — blamed. *Madḥūr* — banished, driven away from mercy.

The pair echoes verse 18’s *madhmūman madḥūrā* and verse 29’s *malūman maḥsūrā*. The sūrah uses these terminal adjectives as a refrain for the state of the one who has transgressed the boundary, and the repetition is cumulative: by the time the reader reaches verse 39, the words carry the weight of everything before them.

**Why the Section Closes With a Warning Rather Than a Promise**

The code has been largely imperative and prohibitive; it closes with the consequence of violating its foundation. The choice is deliberate. A moral code that ends with encouragement may be read as advisory. A code that ends with the alternative establishes that the matter is grave. And the gravity is located precisely: not in the details but in the root. The one who keeps every rule while enthroning something created has kept nothing; the one who has tawḥīd and stumbles has a foundation to return to — which is why verse 25 already assured the *awwābūn* of forgiveness.

**The Code as a Whole**

Read as a unit, verses 22–39 constitute one of the most compact moral statements in the Qur’an: God alone; parents honored; wealth given, not squandered, and not hoarded; children not killed for economic fear; sexuality guarded; life inviolable except by law; the orphan’s property held in trust; covenants kept; measures honest; speech limited to what is known; bearing humble. Ibn Masʿūd called it the Prophet’s ﷺ sealed testament, and the description fits: it is the minimum below which a Muslim life does not fall, and it is presented not as a burden but as *ḥikmah* — wisdom, the thing the human intellect recognizes as sound when it sees it clearly.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:40

> **Did your Lord favor you with sons, while He took females from among the angels [for Himself]? Surely you speak a monstrous word!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From Their Own Prejudice**

*Afa-aṣfākum rabbukum bi-l-banīna wa-ttakhadha mina al-malāʾikati ināthā* — “Has thy Lord chosen you for sons, and taken for Himself females from among the angels?” The verb *aṣfā* means to select for oneself, to give the purest and best part; the Qur’an uses it at 16:57 in the same polemical context. The argument is not a metaphysical refutation but a *reductio* aimed at the Arabs’ own values, and its force depends entirely on a fact the Qur’an states elsewhere with unusual bluntness.

At 16:57–59:

> *“And they ascribe unto God daughters — glory be to Him! — and for themselves what they desire. And when one of them is given the tidings of a female, his face darkens and he chokes with grief. He hides himself from the people because of the evil of what he has been told: shall he keep her in humiliation, or bury her in the dust? Surely evil is what they judge.”*

The Arabs who claimed the angels were God’s daughters regarded the birth of a daughter as a calamity — so much so that infanticide was practiced, which verse 31 of this very sūrah prohibits. The verse’s argument is therefore devastating in its simplicity: you attribute to God the very thing you regard as a misfortune for yourselves, and you keep for yourselves the thing you regard as a blessing.

**The Qur’an’s Method of Refutation**

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both note that the Qur’an frequently argues against shirk not by abstract demonstration alone but by exposing the internal incoherence of the belief. See 43:16–19, *“Or has He taken daughters from what He creates, and favored you with sons? And when one of them is given tidings of the like of what he ascribes to the Compassionate, his face darkens and he is full of grief”*; and 52:39, *“Has He daughters, and you sons?”*; and 53:21–22, *“What! For you the male, and for Him the female? That indeed were an unfair division.”*

The phrase at 53:22 — *tilka idhan qismatun ḍīzā*, “that indeed is an unjust division” — states the logical form explicitly. The argument is not that God cannot have children; it is that the *specific* attribution is self-contradictory given the attributors’ own premises. A belief that its holders would not accept if applied to themselves is not a belief but a convenience.

**“Surely You Speak a Monstrous Word”**

*Innakum la-taqūlūna qawlan ʿaẓīmā* — *ʿaẓīm* means great, enormous, tremendous. In a negative context it carries the sense of monstrous, appalling. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the enormity lies in what the attribution implies about God: to ascribe to Him a supposed deficiency, and to do so while regarding that same thing as a disgrace for oneself, reveals a total absence of reverence.

The word *qawl* — a saying, a word — is important. The condemnation falls on *speech*: not merely on a private belief but on a statement made publicly, defended, and built into a religious system. The Qur’an treats speech about God as a matter of grave consequence, which is why it prohibits saying of God what one does not know (7:33, 10:68).

**Why the Qur’an Argues This Way**

The polemical strategy is instructive for anyone who must argue about God. The Qur’an does not begin with the attributes of the divine nature in the abstract; it begins with the inconsistency in the opponent’s position, using the opponent’s own standards. This is a form of argument that remains available and effective: the person who says one thing about God and the opposite about himself has not stated a theology but a preference. The exposure of the preference is often more persuasive than any proof, because it is undeniable.

**The Modern Form of the Same Error**

The specific belief is gone, but the structure persists. Every generation attributes to God whatever its own culture finds acceptable, and refuses to attribute to Him whatever its own culture finds distasteful — while insisting that the resulting picture is the truth about God rather than a mirror of the age. The verse’s diagnostic remains useful: if your conception of God differs from your neighbor’s in exactly the places where your culture differs from his, the conception may be doing more work for you than for the truth.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:41

> **And We have indeed varied [Our signs] in this Quran, that they might reflect, though it increased them in naught but aversion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Variety as Design**

*Wa laqad ṣarrafnā fī hādhā al-qurʾāni li-yadhdhakkarū* — “and We have varied [it] in this Qur’an, that they might remember.” The verb *ṣarrafa* is the intensive form of *ṣarafa*, to turn, to divert, to direct in different directions; the intensive indicates repeated, deliberate turning. The Qur’an applies the same verb to its own composition at 6:46, 6:65, 6:105, 7:58, and 46:27, and to its parables at 18:54.

Al-Rāzī and al-Bayḍāwī explain the variety: the Qur’an presents its truths sometimes as rational demonstration, sometimes in an awesome register that inspires fear, sometimes in a gentle register that attracts. The same truth appears as argument, as warning, as promise, as narrative, as image, as legal ruling. The variation is not redundancy; it is pedagogy — the recognition that different minds are reached by different approaches.

**“That They Might Remember”**

*Li-yadhdhakkarū* — from *dh-k-r*, to remember, to recall, to be reminded. The Qur’an’s characteristic description of its own function is *dhikr*, remembrance: 15:9, *“We have sent down the Reminder (al-dhikr) and We are its Guardian”*; 21:50, *“And this is a blessed Reminder We have sent down.”* The verb implies that the content is not wholly new to the human being — that something in him already knows it and has forgotten. Revelation reminds rather than informs, which is why the response to it feels like recognition.

**“It Increased Them in Naught but Aversion”**

*Wa mā yazīduhum illā nufūrā* — *nufūr* is from *n-f-r*, to flee in alarm, to shy away as a startled animal does. The word is stronger than mere dislike; it denotes the reflexive recoil of a frightened creature. The verse states that the same variation which was meant to produce reflection produced flight in some.

The Qur’an documents this divergence repeatedly and without embarrassment.

At 2:26: *“He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby, and He misleads none but the transgressors.”* At 9:124–25: *“And whenever a sūrah is revealed, some of them say: ‘Which of you has it increased in faith?’ As for those who believe, it increases them in faith... But as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth.”* And at 17:82 in this very sūrah:

> *“We send down of the Qur’an that which is a cure and a mercy for the believers, and it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss.”*

**The Theological Debate the Verse Provoked**

Al-Rāzī records that this verse became a battleground between the Muʿtazilah and the Ashāʿirah. The Muʿtazilite argument: God varied the signs precisely *so that* different people could reflect — which indicates a universal divine will for human understanding. The Ashʿarite counter-argument: God varied them knowing that some would be increased only in aversion — which indicates that the divine will encompasses the turning away of some.

The debate is genuine and unresolved within the tradition, and the verse supports both readings grammatically. The Sunni synthesis, articulated by al-Rāzī and others, distinguishes the *universal will* (what God commands and desires for all) from the *particular will* (what God brings about) — and holds that human beings are responsible for the response they make to a guidance that is genuinely offered. The verse’s own wording supports this: the variation is *for the sake of* reflection; the aversion is the hearers’ achievement, attributed to them in the verb’s subject position (*mā yazīduhum* — “it increases *them*,” with the increase happening *in them* by their own recoil).

**The Practical Observation**

Anyone who has taught, preached, or simply argued knows the phenomenon: the same explanation that convinces one person repels another, and often the more careful the explanation, the stronger the recoil. The verse names this and does not treat it as a failure of the message. The messenger’s task is to vary the approach; the hearer’s response is his own. This is a considerable liberation for anyone responsible for conveying truth — the outcome is not entirely in the conveyor’s hands, and its failure is not proof that the conveying was defective.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:42

> **Say, “If there were gods with Him, as they say, they would surely seek a way unto the Possessor of the Throne.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From Order**

*Qul law kāna maʿahu ālihatun kamā yaqūlūna idhan labtaghaw ilā dhī al-ʿarshi sabīlā* — the verse presents a conditional: if there were other gods alongside Him, as they claim, they would seek a way to the Possessor of the Throne. Two readings are preserved by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī, and both are defensible.

The first, favored by al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī, is *competitive*: the rival gods would seek a path to *dethrone* Him and struggle with Him for dominance. On this reading the verse is a version of the argument from cosmic order — the universe displays a single coherent governance, which would be impossible under competing sovereignties.

The second, recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī, is *submissive*: the supposed gods would themselves seek nearness to the One God, recognizing His dominance, and would therefore be worshippers rather than objects of worship. On this reading the verse answers the specific Quraysh claim recorded at 39:3: *“We worship them only that they may bring us near to God.”* The response: if these were gods, would they not seek nearness for themselves rather than serving as intermediaries for you?

**The Parallel Argument at 21:22**

The Qur’an states the same reasoning more explicitly elsewhere:

> *“Were there gods other than God in them, they would surely have been corrupted. So glory be to God, Lord of the Throne, above that which they ascribe.”* (21:22)

And at 23:91: *“God has taken no son, nor is there any god along with Him; otherwise each god would have taken away what he created, and some of them would have risen over others. Glory be to God above what they describe.”*

The argument’s logical form is what later Islamic theology called *dalīl al-tamānuʿ* — the argument from mutual prevention. If there were two necessary beings with will, each would will something; where their wills differed, one would be prevented; the prevented one would not be omnipotent; therefore there is one. The observable fact that the cosmos is *not* in chaos is the empirical premise.

**“The Possessor of the Throne”**

*Dhī al-ʿarsh* — the Throne is the Qur’an’s designation for the summit of created existence, the boundary beyond which no creature passes. Its use here is deliberate: the argument is set at the highest point of the hierarchy. The one who possesses the Throne possesses everything below it. Any supposed god must either be below the Throne — and therefore a creature, dependent, and not divine — or contend with its Possessor — in which case the cosmos would show the marks of the contention. It shows none.

**Why the Argument Works Against Intermediary Worship**

The Quraysh did not deny God’s existence; they acknowledged Him as creator and placed idols between themselves and Him. The verse’s argument is aimed precisely at that arrangement. Whatever the intermediary is, it is either created — in which case it needs a way to God as much as the worshipper does, and cannot confer what it lacks — or uncreated — in which case it is a rival, and the observable order of the world refutes the supposition. There is no third option, which is why the verse can state the conditional so confidently.

The argument remains available in modern form. Whatever a person treats as the necessary intermediary between himself and ultimate reality — an institution, a lineage, a leader, a ritual system — must either be dependent or independent. If dependent, it cannot be the source of access; if independent, the coherence of reality is unexplained. The verse’s logic is not time-bound.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:43

> **Glory be to Him! Exalted is He above whatsoever they say.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tanzīh as the Necessary Response**

*Subḥānahu wa taʿālā ʿammā yaqūlūna ʿuluwwan kabīrā* — “Glory be to Him, and exalted is He above what they say, greatly exalted.” The two terms form the vocabulary of *tanzīh* — declaring God beyond every deficiency. *Subḥānahu* declares Him free; *taʿālā* declares Him high; and *ʿuluwwan kabīrā* — a cognate accusative of intensification — declares the elevation to be great.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note that this formula appears consistently after the mention of false deities or of children attributed to God: 6:100, 10:18, 16:1, 21:22, 23:91, 28:68, 30:40, 37:159, 39:67, 43:82, 52:43, 59:23. It functions as a liturgical correction — the revelation pausing to cleanse the record after reporting an attribution.

**Why the Qur’an Pauses to Glorify**

The practice is not merely rhetorical. Al-Rāzī observes that the repeated interjection trains the reader’s response: upon encountering a false claim about God, the correct reaction is not analysis first but glorification. The mind that habitually responds to a misdescription of God with *subḥānahu* develops a reflex of reverence that protects it from casual theological error.

This is why *tasbīḥ* occupies such a place in Muslim devotion. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (6682) and Muslim (2694):

> *“Two words are light on the tongue, heavy in the scale, and beloved to the Compassionate: Subḥāna Allāhi wa bi-ḥamdih, subḥāna Allāhi al-ʿaẓīm.”*

The verse is the scriptural model of the practice, and the practice is the verse’s intended effect.

**The Theological Content of the Formula**

*ʿUluwwan kabīrā* — a great elevation — carries a doctrinal claim that Ibn Taymiyyah treated as foundational: that God’s transcendence is not a mere abstraction but includes His being above His creation in a manner befitting His majesty, without resemblance to created elevation. The Salaf’s formula on such matters was the one Mālik ibn Anas gave when asked about the verse of the Throne: the elevation is known, the *how* is unknowable, belief in it is obligatory, and questioning about the *how* is innovation. The verse’s phrase — *greatly* exalted — is the Qur’an’s own indication that whatever elevation is meant, it exceeds the category.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:44

> **The seven heavens, and the earth, and whosoever is in them glorify Him. And there is no thing, save that it hymns His praise, though you do not understand their praise. Truly He is Clement, Forgiving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Universal Worship as a Fact, Not a Metaphor**

*Tusabbiḥu lahu al-samāwātu al-sabʿu wa-l-arḍu wa man fīhinna wa in min shayʾin illā yusabbiḥu bi-ḥamdih* — “the seven heavens, and the earth, and whoever is in them glorify Him; and there is not a thing but that it glorifies Him with His praise.” The scope is total: *min shayʾin* — any thing whatsoever. The negation *in min* with *illā* is the strongest form of universal statement in Arabic.

The Qur’an states the same doctrine repeatedly. 13:13: *“And the thunder hymns His praise, as do the angels, in awe of Him.”* 22:18: *“Hast thou not considered that unto God prostrates whoever is in the heavens and whoever is on the earth — the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, and the beasts, and many among mankind?”* 24:41: *“Hast thou not considered that God is glorified by whosoever is in the heavens and the earth, and by the birds with wings outspread? Each knows its prayer and its glorification.”* 16:49–50; 59:1; 62:1.

**Is the Praise Literal?**

The commentators divide, and the division is instructive. One position, held by many of the Salaf and by Ibn Kathīr, is that the praise is real, in a manner appropriate to each creature, known to God and not to us — which is exactly what the verse says: *wa lākin lā tafqahūna tasbīḥahum*, “but you do not understand their glorification.” Al-Ṭabarī records the opinion that *subḥāna Allāh* is the universal prayer of all creatures.

The other position, associated with the rationalist exegetes, is that the praise is the “speech of the case” (*lisān al-ḥāl*): every creature, by its existence, its order, and its dependence, testifies to its Maker, as a well-made object testifies to its maker without speaking.

The verse’s own wording — *you do not understand their praise* — favors the first reading, since the second would be comprehensible: the testimony of order is precisely what reflection discerns. If the praise were merely the evident fact of design, the verse would not need to say that it is beyond our understanding. The exegetes who note this argue that the praise is real but of a mode we cannot apprehend.

**The Ethical Consequence Al-Ṭabarī Draws**

Al-Ṭabarī records a practical inference of unusual tenderness: since every creature possesses spirit and glorifies God, one should never show disrespect to any animal, or indeed to any creature. The doctrine of universal praise is not a metaphysical curiosity; it re-orders the human being’s relation to the natural world. If the bird in the tree is engaged in worship you cannot hear, your treatment of the bird is a treatment of a worshipper.

This is the theological ground of the extensive Islamic legislation on animal welfare: the Prophet’s ﷺ prohibition of shooting at animals as targets, his command to sharpen the blade before slaughtering so as to minimize suffering, and his statement recorded by Abū Dāwūd (2550):

> *“God has written excellence (iḥsān) upon everything. So when you kill, kill well; and when you slaughter, slaughter well. Let one of you sharpen his blade and spare suffering to the animal he slaughters.”*

**The Argument Against Idolatry Completes**

The verse’s placement after verses 40–43 is argumentative. Al-Bayḍāwī notes that it continues the reasoning of verse 42: everything in the heavens and the earth is subordinate, dependent, and turned toward the One God. To worship any of them — even as an intermediary — is to be ignorant of what they are. Compare 41:37: *“Among His signs are the night and the day, the sun and the moon. Prostrate not unto the sun nor unto the moon, but prostrate unto God Who created them.”*

The contingency of the whole cosmos is the proof of the Necessary One. Everything that could have not existed, and does, points beyond itself.

**“Truly He Is Clement, Forgiving”**

*Innahu kāna ḥalīman ghafūrā* — *ḥalīm* is the one who withholds punishment despite the capacity to inflict it; al-Bayḍāwī explains it as God’s not hastening punishment upon wrongdoers. *Ghafūr* is the one who covers and forgives. The pairing appears at 2:225, 2:235, 3:155, 5:101, and 35:41.

The closing is remarkable in context. The entire universe is praising God; the human idolater alone is refusing. The response is not immediate destruction but forbearance and the offer of forgiveness. The verse thus ends the polemic not with a threat but with the two Names most associated with patience toward human failure — which is itself an argument about the character of the One being described.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:45

> **And when thou recitest the Quran, We place a hidden veil between thee and those who believe not in the Hereafter.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Veil Placed by God**

*Wa idhā qaraʾta al-qurʾāna jaʿalnā baynaka wa bayna alladhīna lā yuʾminūna bi-l-ākhirati ḥijāban mastūrā* — “and when thou recitest the Qur’an, We place between thee and those who do not believe in the Hereafter a veil *mastūr*.” The veil is God’s act, not the hearer’s — a point of considerable theological weight, which the commentators handle carefully.

*Ḥijāb mastūr* — the participle *mastūr* means covered, concealed, hidden. Al-Rāzī explains the doubling: a veil that is itself veiled, so that the one behind it does not know he is behind it. This is the most unsettling feature of the image. The disbeliever does not experience the barrier as a barrier; he experiences only incomprehension, or boredom, or irritation. He cannot locate the obstacle because the obstacle is invisible from his side.

**Two Readings of the Veil’s Function**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Jaṣṣāṣ record two interpretations. The first: the veil prevents the disbelievers from comprehending the recitation and deriving benefit from it — a reading consistent with verse 46’s coverings over the hearts. The second, favored by al-Rāzī: the veil protects the Prophet ﷺ from their harm, so that their hostility cannot reach him while he recites.

The two are not mutually exclusive, and the exegetes often hold both.

The same device appears at 41:5, where the disbelievers themselves describe it:

> *“Our hearts are under coverings from that to which thou callest us, and in our ears there is deafness, and between us and thee is a veil. So act; truly we are acting.”*

**Why the Verse Specifies Disbelief in the Hereafter**

The veil is placed specifically against *“those who do not believe in the Hereafter”* — the same specification as verse 10. The reason is the same: denial of the Hereafter is not one doctrinal error among many but the removal of the disposition that makes reception possible. A person who does not believe he will be questioned has no reason to attend to a message whose entire content is about the questioning. The veil is therefore not an arbitrary obstruction but the natural form taken by an antecedent refusal.

**The Consolation for the One Who Recites**

The verse is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and, through him, to everyone who conveys the message. Its practical function is to explain failure. The Prophet ﷺ recited to assemblies that produced nothing — not because the recitation was deficient but because a veil had been placed. This is one of the Qur’an’s most important pastoral teachings: the conveyor is responsible for the conveying, not for the reception.

The same reassurance appears at 27:80–81: *“Truly thou canst not make the dead hear, nor make the deaf hear the call when they have turned their backs. Nor canst thou guide the blind out of their error. Thou canst make none hear save those who believe in Our signs and are submissive.”* And 28:56: *“Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guides whom He wills.”*

**The Veil in Modern Conversation**

Anyone who has attempted to discuss faith with a person committed to disbelief recognizes the phenomenon the verse describes: the words are heard but not received; the argument is answered but not engaged; the conversation moves without ever arriving. The verse names it and locates it — not in the inadequacy of the presentation but in a condition the presenter cannot see and cannot remove. The practical conclusion is not to stop speaking but to stop measuring success by reception, and to recognize that the removal of the veil is not in the speaker’s hands.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:46

> **And We have placed coverings over their hearts, such that they understand it not, and in their ears a deafness. And whenever thou dost mention thy Lord alone in the Quran, they turn their backs in aversion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Aknah and Waqr: The Anatomy of Non-Reception**

*Wa jaʿalnā ʿalā qulūbihim akinnatan an yafqahūhu wa fī ādhānihim waqrā* — “and We have placed *akinnah* over their hearts, lest they understand it, and in their ears a *waqr*.” Two precise terms.

*Akkinnah* is the plural of *kinān*, a covering — the same word used for a quiver that holds arrows, or a sheath. It is not a light film but an enclosure. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as a barrier that prevents *fiqh* — the deep comprehension that goes beyond hearing words to grasping their meaning. The verse distinguishes the two levels explicitly: they may hear the sounds and not attain the understanding.

*Waqr* is a heaviness in the ear, a partial deafness — not total, since they do hear the recitation, but sufficient to prevent it from entering. The same pair appears at 6:25: *“And among them are some who listen to thee, but We have placed coverings over their hearts lest they understand it, and in their ears a deafness,”* and at 18:57. At 41:5 the disbelievers describe their own condition in the same terms, which the commentators note as an acknowledgment: they know they are not receiving, and they prefer it that way.

**The Theological Question and the Classical Answer**

That God *places* the coverings raises the question of human responsibility.

The classical answer, standard from al-Ṭabarī through al-Rāzī, rests on sequence: the coverings are placed *after* the refusal, as a consequence of it, not as its cause. Compare 2:7, *“God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing”* — which follows verses describing those who *chose* deception; and 61:5, *“And when they turned aside, God turned aside their hearts.”* And 6:110:

> *“And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time.”*

The principle is that the initial turning away is the human act; the sealing is the divine confirmation of a chosen state. The person who repeatedly refuses to look eventually loses the capacity to see, and the loss is real, and it is his — though it is God who enacts it. This is the Qur’an’s doctrine of the hardening of the heart, and it is one of its most serious warnings: refusal has cumulative consequences that are not fully reversible.

**“Whenever Thou Dost Mention Thy Lord Alone”**

*Wa idhā dhakarta rabbaka fī al-qurʾāni waḥdahu wallaw ʿalā adbārihim nufūrā* — al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that this refers to the recitation of verses affirming God’s Oneness and refuting partners. The reaction is not indifference but *nufūr* — the same word as in verse 41, the startled recoil of an animal.

The specificity is diagnostic. It was not the Qur’an’s moral teaching, its narratives, or its descriptions of the Hereafter that produced the recoil; it was the *exclusive* claim. Tawḥīd is the intolerable element, because it demands the removal of every other object of devotion — including the ancestral gods that structured Quraysh’s social and economic life. The verse identifies the exact point at which the message becomes unacceptable: not where it is difficult to believe, but where it is costly to accept.

**The Diagnostic Value of the Verse**

The verse offers a test available to any reader: observe what in the message you cannot bear. Not what you find hard to understand — that is an intellectual matter — but what makes you want to leave the room. That point is where the message is touching something you have enthroned. The disbelievers of Makkah could listen to the Qur’an’s poetry with admiration; al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah famously said of it that it was not the speech of mankind. What they could not tolerate was *waḥdahu* — “alone.” The verse suggests that the location of a person’s aversion is a reliable map of his idols.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:47

> **We know best that which they listen for when they listen to thee, and when they converse in secret, when the wrongdoers say, “You follow naught but a man bewitched!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Knows the Purpose of the Listening**

*Naḥnu aʿlamu bimā yastamiʿūna bihi idh yastamiʿūna ilayka* — “We know best what they listen *for* when they listen to thee.” The construction with *bi-mā* indicates the object or purpose of the listening: not what they hear, but what they are seeking in the hearing. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that they listened not for guidance but for material to mock.

The distinction is one of the Qur’an’s most penetrating observations about the psychology of hostile listening. Two people can hear identical words; one listens for truth, the other for a flaw. The second is not engaged in inquiry but in ammunition-gathering, and the verse asserts that God’s knowledge extends to that interior posture — which is invisible to the speaker and often to the listener himself.

**The Secret Converse**

*Wa idh hum najwā* — “and when they are in secret conference.” The noun *najwā* denotes private, whispered consultation.

The Qur’an treats secret conference with consistent suspicion, because it is usually where opposition is organized. 4:114: *“There is no good in most of their secret converse, save for him who enjoins charity or kindness or reconciliation between men.”* And Sūrah al-Mujādilah (58:7–10) is largely devoted to it: *“Hast thou not considered those forbidden secret converse, then returning to that which they were forbidden, and conversing in sin and enmity and disobedience to the Messenger?”* — and:

> *“Their secret converse is only from Satan, that he may grieve those who believe.”*

The verse’s assertion — *We know best* — is directed at the Prophet ﷺ as consolation. The conspiracies were hidden from him; they were not hidden from God. This is the recurring function of divine-knowledge statements in the Qur’an: not to inform but to reassure.

**“A Man Bewitched”**

*Idh yāqūlu al-ẓālimūna in tattabiʿūna illā rajulan masḥūrā* — *masḥūr* is the passive participle of *saḥara*, to enchant; it means one who has been bewitched, or one whose mind has been taken. The accusation is that the Prophet ﷺ is not a liar but a *victim* — deceived by sorcery, speaking under an influence he does not control.

The commentators note that this is a milder form of the more common accusation, that the prophets are *sorcerers* — active practitioners. The Qur’an records the accusation against many prophets: 5:110, 6:7, 10:76 and 81, 11:7, 20:57, 21:3, 26:35, 34:43, 37:15, 43:30, 51:52, 54:2, 61:6. Ṣāliḥ was called bewitched at 26:153, Shuʿayb at 26:185, and Moses himself in this very sūrah at 17:101. And 51:52 records the pattern across all of them: *“Likewise no messenger came to those before them but they said: ‘A sorcerer, or a madman!’”*

The recurrence is the point. The accusation is not a considered evaluation but a reflex — the standard vocabulary by which a society defends itself against a messenger it cannot refute. Al-Rāzī observes that the variety of the accusations (poet, sorcerer, madman, taught by a man, bewitched) is itself evidence against them: a man cannot be all of these at once, and the shifting charges indicate that none is held sincerely.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:48

> **Look how they set forth descriptions of thee. Thus they go astray and cannot find a way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Invitation to Observe**

*Unẓur kayfa ḍarabū laka al-amthāl* — “See how they have struck examples for thee.” The verb *ḍaraba al-mathal* — to strike a likeness, to coin a description — is the standard Qur’anic phrase for setting forth a comparison or characterization. The imperative *unẓur* invites the Prophet ﷺ and the reader to examine the accusations as objects: look at them, see what they are.

Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī identify the descriptions: bewitched (v. 47), poet (21:5, 37:36, 52:30), possessed (15:6, 23:70, 34:8, 44:14, 68:51), taught by a human being (16:103), a liar (38:4). The list is a catalogue of incompatible characterizations, and the verse asks the reader to notice the incompatibility.

**The Argument in the Invitation**

The command to look is itself the refutation. A man who is a poet is not a madman; a man who is mad is not composing verse of acknowledged excellence; a man bewitched is not a deliberate deceiver. The Quraysh tried each description in turn, which is the behavior of people searching for a charge rather than stating a finding. Al-Rāzī makes the argument explicit: the multiplicity and mutual exclusion of the descriptions demonstrate that none of them rests on observation.

The Qur’an describes the same closure elsewhere, and locates its origin in the first refusal:

> *“And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time.”* (6:110)

**“Thus They Go Astray and Cannot Find a Way”**

*Fa-ḍallū fa-lā yastaṭīʿūna sabīlā* — “so they have gone astray and cannot find a way.” The verb *ḍalla* means to lose the way, to err; *sabīl* is a path. The image is of people who have lost the road and cannot locate another. Al-Qurṭubī explains that their very act of describing him wrongly is the mechanism of their straying: by fixing on a false characterization, they closed the path to recognizing the truth.

This is a subtle and important claim about error. Error is not merely a wrong belief; it is a *closure*. Once the mind has settled on an explanation — even a bad one — it stops investigating. The Quraysh’s descriptions were not conclusions reached by inquiry but substitutes for inquiry, and having adopted them, they could not find a way out. The verse describes the trap of premature explanation, and it applies to every person who has ever decided what something is in order to avoid having to consider it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:49

> **They say, “What! When we are bones and dust, shall we indeed be resurrected as a new creation?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Skeptic’s Own Words**

*Wa qālū a-idhā kunnā ʿiẓāman wa rafātan a-innā la-mabʿūthūna khalqan jadīdā* — the verse reproduces the objection in direct speech, with its incredulity intact. The opening interrogative *a-* and the emphatic *a-innā la-* convey the tone: *what — us? after we’re bones and dust? really?*

*ʿIẓām* — bones, the hardest remaining part of the body after decay. *Rafāt* — that which has been crushed to powder, disintegrated dust. The two terms mark the two stages of dissolution, and the objection is that both are beyond recovery. The word *khalqan jadīdā* — a new creation — is the object of their incredulity: not merely revival but *re-creation*.

The same objection recurs throughout the Qur’an: 23:35, *“Does he promise you that when you are dead and become dust and bones, you will be brought forth?”*; 23:82; 36:78, *“And he strikes for Us a likeness, and forgets his own creation, saying: ‘Who will give life to bones when they are rotten?’”*; 37:16–17; 56:47; 79:10–11; and in this sūrah again at 17:98.

**Why the Qur’an Quotes the Objection at Length**

The Qur’an does not caricature its opponents’ arguments; it states them accurately and answers them. This is a feature of its method worth noting. The objection from decomposition is not a frivolous one — it is the strongest empirical argument available against resurrection, and the Qur’an engages it repeatedly rather than dismissing it.

**The Structure of the Answer That Follows**

Verses 50–51 answer in a specific order, and the order is logical. First: the material difficulty is not a difficulty for God, since He could give life to stone or iron — materials that never had life. Second: the agent is identified — the One who originated you the first time. Third: the timing question is answered separately. Each response addresses a distinct component of the objection: the material, the agent, and the time.

The verse’s own argumentative force lies in the *asymmetry* it exposes. The objector grants that he was created once from nothing — dust, then a clot, then a child. He then denies that the same agent can reconstitute what He made. The Qur’an’s response, developed in the following verses, is that re-creation is *less* demanding than origination, since the origination had no precedent and the re-creation does.

**The Psychological Root of the Denial**

The Qur’an identifies the objection’s source not as evidence but as wish.

At 75:3–5:

> *“Does man think that We shall not assemble his bones? Yes indeed — We are able to reshape his very fingertips. But man desires to deny what is before him.”*

The phrase *yurīdu li-yafjura amāmah* — “he desires to go on sinning ahead of him” — locates the motive: the denial of resurrection is a desire to continue without constraint.

This is a claim about motivation rather than argument, and the Qur’an makes it carefully. It does not say the objector has no argument; it says his argument is powered by a preference. Anyone who has watched a person resist a conclusion because of what accepting it would require recognizes the diagnosis.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:50

> **Say, “Be you of stone, or of iron,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From the Harder Case**

*Qul kūnū ḥijāratan aw ḥadīdā* — “Say: Be you stone, or iron.” The imperative *kūnū* is not a command to become stone but a hypothetical: *suppose you were*. The Prophet ﷺ is instructed to concede the material premise entirely and then exceed it.

The choice of stone and iron is precise. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī explain the reasoning: these are the materials the Arabs regarded as most remote from life — the hardest, most inert, most resistant to change. Stone does not decay; iron does not soften. If the objector’s difficulty is that bones and dust are too far gone, the answer escalates to materials infinitely further from life than bones ever were.

**Why the Escalation Works**

The argument is a standard *a fortiori*: if God can give life to what never had it, He can restore life to what once had it. Al-Zamakhsharī states the point sharply: bones were once alive by God’s act; stone and iron never were. The resurrection of a human being is therefore the *easier* of the two, not the harder. The objector has misjudged the difficulty.

The same argument appears at 36:79:

> *“Say: He will give them life Who originated them the first time. And He knows every creation.”*

And at 30:27: *“He it is Who originates creation, then brings it back again, and it is easier for Him.”* The phrase *wa huwa ahwanu ʿalayhi* — “and it is easier for Him” — states the principle directly, though the commentators note that “easier” here is relative to human estimation, not to God, for whom nothing is easier than anything.

**The Rhetorical Form**

The verse is a fragment — it breaks off mid-command, with the apodosis supplied in verse 51. This is characteristic Qur’anic style: the speaker leaves the conclusion for the hearer to complete. The effect is that the objector must himself supply the answer, which is more persuasive than being given it. The Qur’an uses the device repeatedly, and here it forces the interlocutor to finish the sentence: *if we were stone or iron... He could still bring us back.* Having said it, he has conceded.

**What the Verse Assumes About God**

The argument depends on a specific conception of divine power: that God’s creative act is not a manipulation of pre-existing material but a bestowal of existence. If creation were rearrangement, then stone and iron would present a genuine difficulty. Because creation is origination, the material is irrelevant — the question is not *what* is being made but *whether the Maker wills it*. This is why the Qur’an’s resurrection argument is inseparable from its creation argument: the one who cannot conceive of making something from nothing cannot conceive of resurrection, and the one who accepts the first has already accepted the second.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:51
