> **or some other created thing more difficult [to resurrect] to your minds.” Then they will say, “Who will bring us back?” Say, “He Who originated you the first time.” And they will shake their heads at thee and say, “When will it be?” Say, “It may well be nigh.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Open-Ended Escalation**

*Aw khalqan mimmā yakburu fī ṣudūrikum* — “or a created thing that is great [difficult] in your breasts.” The verse does not name a third material; it invites the objector to name whatever he considers most impossible. The phrase *yakburu fī ṣudūrikum* — “grows large in your chests” — locates the impossibility in the objector’s own imagination, which is a subtle move: the difficulty is not in the thing but in his conception of it.

The rhetorical effect is that the argument cannot be evaded by proposing a harder case. Whatever is proposed falls under the same answer.

**The Second Question: “Who Will Bring Us Back?”**

*Fa-sayaqūlūna man yuʿīdunā* — “they will say: who will restore us?” The Qur’an reports the question in advance, which the commentators note as a sign of the Prophet’s ﷺ prophethood: he described the conversation before it happened, and it happened as described.

The question is not genuinely seeking an answer — the objector knows who the Muslims say will do it. It is a way of restating the incredulity: *who could possibly?* The answer is therefore not new information but a reminder of what the objector already concedes.

**The Answer: The First Origination**

*Qul alladhī faṭarakum awwala marrah* — “Say: He Who originated you the first time.” The verb *faṭara* means to originate, to split open, to bring into being with an innate constitution; the same root gives *fiṭrah*, the primordial human disposition of 30:30. The choice of *faṭara* rather than *khalaqa* is significant: it emphasizes the *initial* bringing-forth, the act that had no model.

The argument’s force is that the objector already accepts it. He does not claim to be eternal; he knows he came into being. The question of who originated him the first time is one he has either answered (God) or left open. If he has answered it, the resurrection follows. If he has left it open, he has no standing to declare resurrection impossible, since he cannot account for his own existence.

The Qur’an states the argument in its cleanest form at 52:35: *“Or were they created from nothing? Or are they the creators?”* The trilemma exhausts the possibilities, and only the first has an answer that permits resurrection.

**The Third Question: “When Will It Be?”**

*Fa-sayunghiḍūna ilayka ruʾūsahum wa yaqūlūna matā huwa* — “and they will shake their heads at thee and say: when is it?” The gesture is described with unusual vividness: *yunghiḍūna ruʾūsahum* — they will wag their heads, the universal gesture of mocking disbelief. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as the shaking of the head in dismissal.

The question about timing is a deflection. Having failed to refute the possibility, the objector asks for a date — a demand the prophets are consistently told to refuse. 7:187: *“They ask thee about the Hour, when it will come. Say: the knowledge of it is only with my Lord.”* And 67:25–26: *“They say: when is this promise, if you are truthful? Say: the knowledge is only with God, and I am only a clear warner.”*

**“Say: It May Well Be Nigh”**

*Qul ʿasā an yakūna qarībā* — the exception to the general refusal. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note the commentators’ observation that *ʿasā* from God indicates something that will certainly occur, not something merely possible — the same principle applied to *ʿasā* in verse 8. The answer is not a date but a warning: it is nearer than you assume.

The Prophet ﷺ reinforced this. Al-Bukhārī (6504) and Muslim (2950) record:

> *“I and the Hour have been sent like these two” — and he joined his index and middle fingers.*

And Muslim (2940) records: *“The Hour and I are like these two,”* with the same gesture. The image is of two things so close together that no third can fit between them.

The theological reasoning behind the nearness is stated at 54:1: *“The Hour has drawn near, and the moon has split,”* and at 21:1: *“The reckoning of mankind has drawn near, yet they turn away in heedlessness.”* From the perspective of the eternal, the interval between the Prophet’s ﷺ mission and the Hour is short — and from the perspective of any individual, it is shorter still, since each person’s Hour arrives at his death. Al-Rāzī notes this: the answer *it may well be nigh* is true for every hearer personally, whatever the cosmic schedule.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:52

> **The Day when He calls you, you will respond by praising Him, and you will think that you tarried but a short while.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universal Response to the Summons**

*Yawma yadʿūkum fa-tastajībūna bi-ḥamdih* — “the Day He calls you, and you respond with His praise.” The verb *tastajībūna* is the tenth form of *j-w-b*, to respond, to answer a call.

The Qur’an describes the same scene at 30:25: *“And among His signs is that the heaven and the earth stand by His command; then when He calls you with a single call from the earth, behold, you come forth.”* And at 39:75:

> *“And thou wilt see the angels circling around the Throne, glorifying the praise of their Lord.”*

The response is *bi-ḥamdih* — with His praise. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī identify three grounds for the praise at that moment: the recognition of God as Creator, now inescapable; the recognition of His power to resurrect, now demonstrated; and the recollection of His blessings, now fully visible. Al-Zamakhsharī adds that the praise is an expression of total obedience — the response is immediate and unhesitating, unlike the response to the same call in worldly life.

**The Praise That Comes Too Late to Help**

The commentators are careful to note the tragic dimension: the recognition and obedience of those who refused both in earthly life bring them no benefit. Compare 40:84–85: *“But when they saw Our might, they said: ‘We believe in God alone, and we disbelieve in what we associated with Him.’ But their faith did not avail them when they saw Our might — the wont of God which has passed among His servants.”* And 32:12: *“And if thou couldst see, when the guilty hang their heads before their Lord: ‘Our Lord, we have seen and heard, so return us; we will do good — truly we are certain.’”*

The verse therefore contains one of the Qur’an’s central warnings: faith compelled by sight is not faith. The response at the summons is universal precisely because it is no longer a choice — and the value of faith lies entirely in its being chosen when it was not compelled.

**“You Will Think That You Tarried But a Short While”**

*Wa taẓunnūna in labithtum illā qalīlā* — the verb *ẓanna* here means to reckon, to estimate. The estimate will be that the stay was *qalīl* — brief. The Qur’an specifies the estimate elsewhere with striking consistency: 18:19, *“They will say: we tarried a day or part of a day”*; 23:113, *“They will say: we tarried a day or part of a day — ask those who count”*; 10:45, *“as though they had tarried but an hour of a day”*; 30:55; 46:35, *“as though they had tarried but an hour of a day”*; 79:46, *“It will be as though they had tarried but an evening or its morning”*; 20:104.

**Why the Duration Collapses**

The commentators offer two explanations, and both are illuminating. The first is comparative: the Hereafter is eternal, and any finite duration, however long, is negligible beside it. A thousand years is nothing next to forever. The second is phenomenological: the intensity of the Day compresses the memory of everything before it, as a great event compresses the years preceding it in the recollection of anyone who has lived through one.

The Qur’an makes the comparative argument explicitly at 23:115: *“Did you think that We created you in vain, and that you would not be returned to Us?”* — the question that only makes sense if the earthly interval is genuinely short relative to what follows.

**The Practical Force of the Verse**

The verse is a meditation on proportion, and its application does not require accepting the eschatology to feel its truth. Every human being who has reached the end of anything — a career, a marriage, a life — reports the same collapse: it seemed long while it was happening and short in retrospect. The Qur’an takes that universal experience and extends it to its limit: the entire span of worldly life, from the vantage of what follows, will register as *qalīl*.

The verse’s function is therefore to recalibrate the scale on which decisions are made. Most of what consumes a person’s attention is urgent within a frame that the verse declares to be brief. The one who internalizes this does not thereby neglect the world — the Qur’an commands work, trade, family, and effort — but he stops treating the brief as though it were the final. The same recalibration appears at 109:1–2 in a different register, and at 103, where the whole of humanity is described as being in loss except those who believe and do good: the loss is precisely the spending of the brief interval on what does not survive it.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:53

> **Say unto My servants that they should say that which is more virtuous. Surely Satan provokes ill feeling between them. Surely Satan is a manifest enemy unto man.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command About Words, Not About Arguments**

*Wa qul li-ʿibādī yaqūlū allatī hiya aḥsan* — “and say to My servants that they should say that which is *aḥsan*.” The elative *aḥsan* — the better, the best — implies comparison: among the things one could say, choose the one that is better. Al-Zamakhsharī reads it as a general command to speak gently, without harshness; al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note the elided feminine noun, most commonly *kalimah*, so that the phrase is “say the word that is best.”

The verse’s placement is significant. It follows the description of the disbelievers’ coverings, deafness, and accusations (vv. 45–48) — that is, it addresses the believer’s response to provocation. The Qur’an is legislating the tone of a community under sustained verbal attack.

**The Reported Occasion**

Al-Qurṭubī and others record that the verse was revealed concerning an incident in which ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was insulted by an Arab and cursed or threatened him in return; the verse instructed pardon instead. Whether or not the specific report is accepted, the verse’s logic fits it exactly: the harsh reply escalates, and escalation is Satan’s objective.

**Why the Better Word Is Strategically Correct**

Al-Rāzī gives the reasoning: harsh speech provokes the opponent to answer in kind, entrenching him in his opposition. The Qur’an states the same principle at 6:108: *“And revile not those whom they call upon besides God, lest they revile God in enmity without knowledge.”* The prohibition on reviling false gods — even though they deserve nothing — is justified by its *consequence*: it would produce blasphemy against God.

This is a consequentialist argument inside a revealed text, and it is worth noting how clearly the Qur’an makes it. The moral quality of a statement is not determined solely by its truth; its effects enter into its evaluation. A true statement delivered so as to harden the hearer against truth has failed in the very purpose for which speech exists.

Al-Qurṭubī records the recommended form of reply to an insult: *“May God have mercy upon you.”* The formula converts the exchange entirely — the insulter receives a prayer rather than a counter-insult, and there is nothing to answer.

**“Satan Provokes Ill Feeling Between Them”**

*Inna al-shayṭāna yanzaghu baynahum* — the verb *nazagha* means to insert, to intrude, to prod; it denotes a small, sharp intervention that sets something in motion. The Qur’an uses it at 7:200, *“And if a provocation (nazgh) from Satan provokes thee, seek refuge in God,”* and at 41:36. The image is of a wedge driven into a joint.

The verse identifies Satan’s method with precision: he does not usually attack faith directly; he attacks *relationships*. Compare 5:91, *“Satan only seeks to sow enmity and hatred between you through wine and gambling, and to turn you from the remembrance of God and from prayer”*; and 12:100, where Joseph says to his brothers, *“It was Satan who incited evil between me and my brothers.”* The story of Joseph is the Qur’an’s fullest case study of the mechanism: a family destroyed not by an external enemy but by an internal provocation that no one recognized at the time.

**“A Manifest Enemy”**

*Inna al-shayṭāna kāna li-l-insāni ʿaduwwan mubīnā* — *ʿaduww* means enemy; *mubīn* means clear, manifest, evident. The enmity is not hidden or ambiguous; it was declared at the beginning, in the refusal to prostrate and the vow that follows in verses 62–64. Compare 2:168, 2:208, 6:142, 12:5, 28:15, 35:6, 36:60, 43:62. And 35:6: *“Truly Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy.”*

**The Practical Discipline**

The verse yields a discipline that is testable in daily life. Before speaking in conflict, the question is not “am I right?” but “is this the better word?” — the word most likely to produce good, among all the true things available. Often the better word is shorter, softer, or absent. The Qur’an does not command the believer to abandon truth or to accept injustice; it commands him to select, among truthful options, the one with the best outcome. This is why the tradition treats restraint of the tongue as a primary virtue. Al-Bukhārī (6474) records:

> *“Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent.”*

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:54

> **Your Lord knows you best. If He wills, He has Mercy upon you, and if He wills, He punishes you, and We have not sent thee as a guardian over them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Knowledge Preceding Disposition**

*Rabbukum aʿlamu bikum in yashaʾ yarḥamkum aw in yashaʾ yuʿadhdhibkum* — “your Lord knows you best; if He wills, He has mercy on you, or if He wills, He punishes you.” Al-Ṭabarī explains that “knows you best” means God has full knowledge of the states of human beings and arranges their affairs accordingly. The mercy and the punishment are thus not arbitrary; they proceed from knowledge.

The commentators differ on the referent. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī offer: the believers, being delivered from persecution or left under it; or the disbelievers, being guided to faith or punished for rejecting it. Both readings agree on the doctrine: the disposition of affairs belongs to God, and it is grounded in His knowledge rather than in human performance.

**The Reassurance in the Verse**

For a community being tortured in Makkah — Bilāl under the stone, the family of Yāsir, Khabbāb on the coals — the verse is a statement about timing, not indifference. God knows you best; the arrangement of your affairs is not a verdict on your worth. The Qur’an repeatedly separates worldly outcome from spiritual standing, and here it does so in the most absolute terms: mercy and punishment are His to distribute, and their distribution in this world does not disclose His final judgment.

**“We Have Not Sent Thee as a Guardian Over Them”**

*Wa mā arsalnāka ʿalayhim wakīlā* — *wakīl* is the trustee, the disposer of affairs, the same word as in verse 2’s *“Take no guardian apart from Me.”* The Prophet ﷺ is not a *wakīl* over people: he does not answer for their choices, control their outcomes, or bear their accountability. His function is conveyance.

The Qur’an states this repeatedly: 5:92, *“And obey God and obey the Messenger, and beware.

But if you turn away, know that upon Our Messenger is only the clear conveyance”*; 6:66; 10:108, *“Say: O mankind, the truth has come to you from your Lord. Whoever is guided is guided for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment — and I am not a guardian (wakīl) over you”*; 39:41; 42:6; 88:21–22:

> *“So remind; thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a dictator over them.”*

**Why This Matters to the Conveyor**

The verse frees the one who conveys from a burden he cannot carry. The Prophet ﷺ grieved over the disbelief of his people — the Qur’an addresses that grief directly at 18:6, *“Perhaps thou wouldst kill thyself with grief that they believe not in this discourse”*; and at 35:8, *“Let not thy soul waste away in grief over them.”* The verse’s answer is structural: your responsibility ends at delivery. What happens after delivery is between the hearer and God.

This is a discipline every teacher, parent, and preacher needs. The alternative — believing oneself responsible for another’s response — produces either coercion or despair, both of which the Qur’an forbids.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:55

> **And thy Lord knows best whosoever is in the heavens and the earth. And We indeed favored some of the prophets over others, and unto David We gave the Psalms.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Universal Knowledge as the Frame for a Specific Claim**

*Wa rabbuka aʿlamu bi-man fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* — the verse moves from knowledge of the believers (v. 54) to knowledge of everything in the heavens and the earth. The expansion is preparatory: the One who knows all of creation is competent to distribute excellence among its members.

**“We Favored Some of the Prophets Over Others”**

*Wa laqad faḍḍalnā baʿḍa al-nabiyyīna ʿalā baʿḍ* — the verse states a hierarchy among the prophets, and it does so in the same terms used of human beings in verse 21.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that while all the prophets occupy the highest human station, God gave them gifts of varying kinds and degrees. The parallel is 2:253:

> *“Those messengers — We favored some of them above others. Among them are those to whom God spoke, and some He raised in degrees.”*

The Qur’an distinguishes carefully between this hierarchy and the believer’s attitude. 2:285: *“The Messenger believes in what was sent down to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in God, His angels, His Books, and His Messengers: ‘We make no distinction between any of His Messengers.’”* The commentators reconcile the two: the believer makes no distinction *in belief* — he accepts all of them as prophets — while acknowledging that God *gave* them different gifts and ranks.

The highest ranks are named elsewhere: the *ulu al-ʿazm*, the messengers of steadfastness (46:35), and the two *khalīl* and *kalīm* relationships — Abraham as God’s intimate friend (4:125) and Moses as the one to whom God spoke directly (4:164). The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is described at 17:1 by the title *ʿabd*, and his distinction is established by the journey and the vision, not by a competing list.

**“And Unto David We Gave the Psalms”**

*Wa ātaynā Dāwūda zabūrā* — the *Zabūr* is the Psalms. The mention of David is a specific example of the general principle of differential favor, and David is chosen because his gifts were unusually varied: kingship, prophethood, the Psalms, the softening of iron (34:10), the submission of the mountains and birds to his praise (38:18–19), the speech of birds (27:16), and the judgment he rendered with Solomon (21:78–79).

Al-Qurṭubī records the description of the Psalms as a revealed book containing no religious law — only praise and glorification of God. If that characterization is accepted, it makes David’s gift distinctive in kind: a scripture of worship rather than of legislation. This is consistent with the Qur’an’s description of David at 34:10–11: *“And We gave David favor from Us: ‘O mountains, hymn with him, and you birds!’ And We made the iron soft for him — ‘Make full coats of mail, and measure in the links.’”*

**Why the Verse Is Here**

The placement follows the discussion of the false deities. The Qur’an has just argued (vv. 56–57 will continue) that whatever is worshipped besides God is a servant seeking approach to Him. Verse 55 supplies the counter-example: genuine excellence exists — David was given a Book, the prophets were ranked — and excellence is a *gift*, not a divinity. The one who receives a scripture remains a servant. This is the answer to the idolater’s implicit reasoning: excellence does not make its possessor an object of worship.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:56

> **Say, “Call upon those whom you claim apart from Him, but they have no power to remove affliction from you, nor to change [it].”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Test Proposed**

*Quli dʿū alladhīna zaʿamtum min dūnihi fa-lā yamlikūna kashfa al-ḍurri ʿankum wa lā taḥwīlā* — “Say: Call upon those you claim apart from Him; they have no power to remove harm from you, nor to transfer it.” The verse proposes an experiment, and it is the Qur’an’s most direct empirical challenge to idolatry.

The verb *zaʿama* — to claim, to assert — carries in Arabic a connotation of unsupported assertion; it is the word used for a claim that may well be false. The Qur’an uses it of false claims at 6:148 and 4:117. The choice of verb is a quiet verdict on the claim before the experiment is even run.

**The Two Powers Denied**

*Kashf al-ḍurr* — the removal of harm — and *taḥwīl* — its transfer or alteration. Al-Qurṭubī explains the distinction: the idols can neither lift the affliction nor redirect it elsewhere. The two together cover every form of relief: cessation and displacement. The verse excludes both, and therefore excludes any possible assistance.

The same argument runs through the Qur’an. 7:194–195: *“Truly those you call upon besides God are servants like yourselves.

Call upon them, and let them answer you, if you are truthful. Have they feet with which they walk? Or hands with which they grasp? Or eyes with which they see? Or ears with which they hear?”* And 10:106: *“And call not upon, apart from God, that which neither benefits thee nor harms thee; for if thou dost, then thou art of the wrongdoers.”* And 34:22:

> *“They possess not so much as an atom’s weight in the heavens or the earth, nor have they any share in either, nor is there for Him any supporter from among them.”*

**The Argument From Crisis**

The Qur’an’s most powerful version of this argument appeals not to theory but to observed behavior. In a crisis, the idolater calls only on God. 6:40–41: *“Say: Have you considered — if the punishment of God comes upon you, or the Hour comes — will you call upon other than God, if you are truthful? Nay, but to Him you will call, and He will remove that for which you call upon Him, if He wills, and you will forget what you associated.”* And 10:12: *“And when affliction touches man, he calls upon Us, whether lying on his side, or sitting, or standing; but when We remove his affliction from him, he passes on as though he had never called upon Us for an affliction that touched him.”* And 29:65: *“And when they embark upon a ship, they call upon God, sincere to Him in religion; but when He has brought them safely to land, behold, they associate.”*

The argument is phenomenological: the idolater’s own behavior in extremis testifies against his theology. This sūrah will make the point explicitly at verses 67–69, using the experience of the sea.

**Why the Verse Demands a Public Test**

The command is *say* — announce it. The challenge is to be made openly, so that the failure is public. This is characteristic of the Qur’an’s polemical method: it does not argue privately with sophisticated refutations but proposes tests that anyone can run. The one who calls on his god in a crisis and receives nothing has learned more than any argument could teach him.

The verse also contains an implicit anthropology. The Qur’an assumes that human beings already know this — that the innate disposition (*fiṭrah*) recognizes the true object of reliance, which is why the crisis strips away the acquired theology. The idolater is not ignorant of God; he is inconsistent, and the crisis exposes the inconsistency.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:57

> **It is they who make supplication, seeking a means of approach to their Lord. Which of them is nearer? And they hope for His Mercy and fear His Punishment. Truly the Punishment of thy Lord is something of which to be wary.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objects of Worship Are Themselves Worshippers**

*Ulāʾika alladhīna yadʿūna yabtaghūna ilā rabbihimu al-wasīlah* — “those whom they call upon themselves seek the means of approach to their Lord.” The pronoun reversal is the whole argument: the ones being called upon are themselves calling. Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify the referents variously as the angels (whom the Arabs called God’s daughters), the jinn (whom they took as partners — 6:100, *“They make the jinn partners with God, though He created them”*), or the righteous human beings whose tombs and memories were venerated. All three readings yield the same conclusion.

The verse connects directly to verse 42: whatever is worshipped besides God seeks a way to God. A being that needs a way to God is not God; a being that supplicates is not the one supplicated. The logic is airtight and does not depend on identifying the beings.

**Al-Wasīlah: The Means of Approach**

*Wasīlah* means that by which one draws near — a means, a connection, an access. The Qur’an commands its pursuit at 5:35: *“O you who believe, fear God and seek the means of approach (al-wasīlah) to Him, and strive in His path.”* The word became a technical term in later Islamic piety, and the verse is its scriptural anchor.

The verse’s polemical point is that *wasīlah* is real but is not what the idolaters think.

The means of approach to God is not another being interposed between oneself and Him; it is what the verse describes in the following words — hope, fear, and supplication. Compare 7:56, *“And call upon Him in fear and hope”*; and 32:16:

> *“They rise from their beds, calling upon their Lord in fear and hope.”*

**“Which of Them Is Nearer?”**

*Ayyuhum aqrab* — the interrogative is read as a challenge: which of these supposed intercessors is actually nearer to God, such that you have chosen him? The question cannot be answered, because the idolaters have no criterion. The verse exposes the arbitrariness of the choice: they select intermediaries without any basis for preferring one over another, which is itself evidence that the selection reflects custom rather than knowledge.

**Hope and Fear as the Structure of Faith**

*Wa yarjūna raḥmatahu wa yakhāfūna ʿadhābah* — “and they hope for His mercy and fear His punishment.” The two are joined as the essential disposition of the servant, and the Qur’an joins them constantly: 7:56, 15:50–51, 32:16, 39:9, 76:9. The classical spiritual writers made the pair the two wings of the spiritual life — the bird cannot fly on one. Hope without fear produces presumption; fear without hope produces despair; both are named as sins (12:87, *“and despair not of the Spirit of God; truly none despairs of the Spirit of God but the disbelieving people”*; 15:56, *“and who despairs of the mercy of his Lord but those astray?”*).

The verse’s point about the supposed deities is that they too are in this condition. The angels, the jinn, the righteous dead — all hope and all fear. A being that fears cannot protect; a being that hopes cannot grant.

**“Truly the Punishment of Thy Lord Is Something to Be Wary Of”**

*Inna ʿadhāba rabbika kāna maḥdhūrā* — *maḥdhūr* means that which one guards against, that from which one flees. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the force of the statement: if the punishment is to be feared even by the prophets — and the Qur’an has the Prophet ﷺ say at 10:15, *“Truly I fear, if I disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day”* — then it is to be feared by all.

The verse thus closes the argument against intermediary worship by relocating the reader’s fear to its proper object. The idolater fears his idols, or fears their displeasure, or fears the loss of their favor. The verse says: the thing worth fearing is the punishment of your Lord, and the beings you fear cannot remove it — as verse 56 has just established.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:58

> **There is no town, save that We shall destroy it before the Day of Resurrection, or punish it with a severe punishment. That is inscribed in the Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Universal Statement About Towns**

*Wa in min qaryatin illā naḥnu muhlikūhā qabla yawmi al-qiyāmati aw muʿadhdhibūhā ʿadhāban shadīdā* — the construction *in min ... illā* is the strongest universal negation in Arabic: there is not a single town except that one of two things will happen to it. The Qur’an, which so often speaks of *specific* destroyed towns, here states that *every* town has an appointed end.

**Two Fates, Distinguished**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī offer the interpretation that gives the verse its moral content: the righteous town is “destroyed” through the ordinary course of death — its people die and it empties — while the wicked town is destroyed through severe punishment, the terrifying destruction that befell the towns of the prophets. On this reading the verse is not a prediction of universal catastrophe but a statement that every community ends, and that the *manner* of its ending corresponds to its character.

The alternative reading takes both branches as calamity, differing in degree. Either way the verse establishes that no settlement is permanent — which is a claim against the assumption, natural to every flourishing city, that it will outlast its inhabitants’ behavior.

**The Qur’an’s Precedent**

The sūrah has already established the pattern at verse 16: *“And when We desire to destroy a town, We command those who live a life of luxury within it...”* And the Qur’an’s catalogue of destroyed towns is extensive: 7:4, *“How many a town have We destroyed! Our might came upon it by night, or while they napped at midday”*; 7:59–101, the accounts of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb; 21:11; 22:45, *“How many a town have We destroyed whose people were wrongdoers, so it lies fallen upon its roofs!”*; 28:59.

**“That Is Inscribed in the Book”**

*Kāna dhālika fī al-kitābi masṭūrā* — *masṭūr* means written in lines, inscribed.

Al-Ṭabarī and others take *the Book* as the Preserved Tablet, of which 85:21–22 speaks: *“Nay, it is a glorious Qur’an, in a Preserved Tablet.”* The same idea appears at 7:34, *“And for every nation there is a term; when their term comes, they cannot delay it an hour, nor hasten it”*; at 15:4, *“And We destroyed no town but that it had a known decree”*; and at 18:59:

> *“And those towns — We destroyed them when they wronged, and We appointed for their destruction a term.”*

The inscription of the term has two consequences. The first is that the delay is real: the town is not destroyed arbitrarily but at a set time, which means the period before it is a genuine period of opportunity. The second is that the delay is finite: the appointed hour cannot be extended by wealth, power, or negotiation.

**The Verse as a Meditation on Civilizations**

The verse is one of the Qur’an’s few statements about the fate of human settlements as such, and it has been read across the centuries as a philosophy of history. Every city the reader knows — including the ones that seem permanent — has a term written. The ruins the Quraysh passed on their trade routes were not anomalies; they were previews. And the cities of the reader’s own age are not exempt.

The practical application is not fatalism but proportion. The verse does not say that nothing can be done; it says the end is fixed and the interval is for use. A community that understands this does not build as though it were eternal, nor does it neglect building; it builds within a frame. The same frame is applied to the individual at 63:11, *“And God will never delay a soul when its term has come”* — the personal version of the same law.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:59

> **Naught hinders Us from sending signs, save that those of old denied them. And We gave unto Thamūd the she-camel as a clear portent, but they wronged her. And We do not send down Our signs, save to inspire fear.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Miracles and Its Refusal**

*Wa mā manaʿanā an nursila bi-l-āyāti illā an kadhdhaha bihā al-awwalūn* — “and nothing prevents Us from sending signs except that the former peoples denied them.” The verse is read by Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī as a response to the Quraysh’s repeated demands for spectacular proof — including the reported request that the Prophet ﷺ turn the hill of Ṣafā into gold.

The demands are recorded elsewhere: 6:37, *“And they say: if only a sign were sent down upon him from his Lord!”*; 10:20, *“And they say: if only a portent were sent down upon him from his Lord! Say: the Unseen belongs to God alone, so wait — I am with you among those who wait”*; 7:203; 29:50–51.

**The Reason for the Refusal**

The verse gives a reason that is not obvious and requires careful reading. God is not unable to send signs; He declines because previous peoples asked for them, received them, and then denied them — and the denial of a requested sign brings immediate destruction. 6:157: *“Or lest you say: ‘If only the Book had been sent down to us, we would have been better guided than they.’ Now has come to you a clear proof from your Lord, a guidance and a mercy; and who is more unjust than one who denies the signs of God and turns away from them?”* And 6:4: *“And no sign comes to them of the signs of their Lord but that they turn away from it.”*

The commentators draw the conclusion explicitly: the withholding of the requested signs was an act of *mercy* toward the Quraysh. Had Ṣafā been turned to gold and the Quraysh still refused — which the precedent indicates they would have — the punishment would have followed immediately, as it followed every previous people granted its requested sign. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī and others state this plainly.

**The Case of Thamūd**

*Wa ātaynā Thamūda al-nāqata mubṣiratan fa-ẓalamū bihā* — the example is precise and well known. Thamūd asked for a sign; God sent the she-camel from the rock, as their own prophets had specified (7:73; 11:64; 26:155). They hamstrung her (7:77, 11:65, 91:14), and the destruction followed within three days (11:65–67; 54:23–31).

The phrase *fa-ẓalamū bihā* — “they wronged [themselves] by it” or “they wronged her” — compresses the whole sequence: a requested sign, granted, and answered with violence. The Qur’an returns to Thamūd repeatedly precisely because it is the clearest case of the pattern: request, grant, denial, destruction.

**The Exception of the Splitting of the Moon**

The commentators note that one requested sign *was* granted: the splitting of the moon. Al-Bukhārī (4864) and Muslim (2802) record from Anas and from Ibn Masʿūd:

> *“The moon was split in the time of the Messenger of God ﷺ into two halves, one half behind the mountain and one half above it. And the Messenger of God ﷺ said: ‘Bear witness.’”* (al-Bukhārī 4865)

And the Qur’an refers to it at 54:1–2: *“The Hour has drawn near, and the moon has split. And whenever they see a sign, they turn away and say: ‘Continuing sorcery!’”* The response was exactly as predicted — the sign was attributed to bewitchment, which is the same accusation reported in verse 47 of this sūrah. The incident thus illustrates the verse: the sign came, the denial followed, and only God’s forbearance postponed the consequence.

**“We Send Not Signs Save to Inspire Fear”**

*Wa mā nursilu bi-l-āyāti illā takhwīfā* — *takhwīf* means the inspiring of fear, in the sense of admonition and warning. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain that signs are sent as warnings, not as entertainment or as proofs for the merely curious. The purpose of a sign is to produce *taqwā*, not amazement.

This is why the Qur’an treats the demand for signs as evidence of bad faith rather than sincere inquiry. A person who says “I will believe if you do X” has already decided that his belief is conditional on his own terms; and if X is done and he still refuses — as the precedent shows — the refusal reveals that the demand was never about evidence. The verse’s refusal is therefore not a failure of power but a refusal to participate in a negotiation that the demandant does not intend to honor.

**The Modern Parallel**

The pattern is entirely current. The conditional demand — “prove it to my satisfaction and I’ll consider it” — is a common way of postponing a decision one does not wish to make. The verse identifies the mechanism and declines it, offering instead what verse 82 will call a cure: a Book that addresses the heart rather than a spectacle that addresses the senses.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:60

> **And [remember] when We said unto thee, “Surely thy Lord encompasses mankind.” We did not ordain the vision that We showed thee, save as a trial for mankind, and the Accursed Tree in the Quran. And We inspire fear in them, but it increases them in naught but great rebellion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**“Thy Lord Encompasses Mankind”**

*Wa idh qulnā laka inna rabbaka aḥāṭa bi-l-nās* — *aḥāṭa* means to surround, to encircle, to comprehend. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī read it as an assurance: God encompasses mankind in power and knowledge, and nothing they do can overcome His protection of the Prophet ﷺ. Others read it as a promise of eventual victory — the conquest at Badr and beyond.

The placement immediately before the mention of the vision is significant. The Prophet ﷺ was about to be commanded to announce something that would cost him followers; the assurance of encompassing power precedes the command.

**“The Vision That We Showed Thee”**

*Wa mā jaʿalnā al-ruʾyā allatī araynāka illā fitnatan li-l-nās* — the majority of commentators, including al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, identify the *ruʾyā* with the Night Journey of verse 1. The word *ruʾyā* can mean a waking vision or a dream-vision, and the exegetes discuss both; the connection to verse 1 within the same sūrah makes the identification compelling.

The verse states its purpose: *illā fitnatan li-l-nās* — “except as a trial for mankind.” And the trial was real. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Ṭabarī record that when the Prophet ﷺ reported the journey, some who had believed left the faith, finding it incredible; and the Quraysh used it to mock him, asking him to describe Jerusalem, which he did. Al-Bukhārī records in the long ḥadīth of the Isrāʾ that the Quraysh asked him about the caravan and about the city, and he answered.

The alternative readings — the dream of the pilgrimage fulfilled at Ḥudaybiyyah, or a vision of victory at Badr or the conquest of Makkah — are recorded by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī, who note the difficulty: those events are Madinan, and this sūrah is Makkan.

**The Theological Point of the Trial**

That God would ordain a vision *as a trial* is a claim of considerable importance. It means the difficulty of believing was intended. A miracle so overwhelming that belief became compulsory would not produce faith; it would produce submission. The Isrāʾ was reported rather than displayed — the Prophet ﷺ announced it, and the audience had to decide whether to trust him. The trial was therefore a trial of *trust in the messenger*, which is the actual content of faith, rather than a trial of sensory evidence.

This explains why the Qur’an so consistently refuses spectacle (v. 59) while granting the Isrāʾ: the Isrāʾ was not offered as public proof but as a private experience publicly reported. Those who trusted the reporter believed; those who did not, did not. The mechanism of faith was preserved intact.

**“And the Accursed Tree in the Qur’an”**

*Wa-l-shajarata al-malʿūnata fī al-qurʾān* — the reference is to the tree of Zaqqūm, described at 37:62–66, 44:43–46, and 56:52–54.

At 37:64–65: *“Truly it is a tree that springs from the bottom of Hell; its spathes are as though they were the heads of satans.”* And 44:43–44:

> *“Truly the tree of Zaqqūm is the food of the sinner.”*

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī explain the “curse”: those who eat from it are cursed, and its fruit is poisonous; or it is remote from all good and from God’s mercy.

The Quraysh ridiculed the description, and their objection is recorded: fire consumes everything, including stones (2:24, *“the Fire whose fuel is men and stones”*), so how can a tree grow in it? Abū Jahl is reported to have mocked it, saying they knew *zaqqūm* as rich food they would enjoy. The verse names the tree alongside the vision as the second thing that became a trial — two reports the audience could not verify and had to accept or reject on the basis of trust.

**“We Inspire Fear in Them, But It Increases Them in Naught but Great Rebellion”**

*Wa nukhawwifuhum fa-mā yazīduhum illā ṭughyānan kabīrā* — *ṭughyān* is exceeding bounds, tyrannical transgression; the same root gives *ṭāghūt*, the tyrant or false object of worship. The verse states that the warnings produced not caution but escalation.

This is the same dynamic as verse 41 and is a recurring Qur’anic observation: 5:64, 5:68, 25:60, 35:42, 71:6 — Noah’s people, warned repeatedly, *“increased in flight.”* The mechanism is a familiar one: a person warned of a consequence he does not wish to accept does not usually reconsider; he escalates his denial, because accepting the warning would require changing his life. The greater the warning, the greater the denial required — hence *ṭughyānan kabīrā*.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:61

> **And when We said unto the angels, “Prostrate before Adam,” they all prostrated, save Iblīs. He said, “Shall I prostrate before one whom Thou hast created of clay?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene Recalled**

*Wa idh qulnā li-l-malāʾikati usjudū li-Ādama fa-sajadū illā iblīs* — the account appears at 2:34, 7:11, 15:29–31, 18:50, 20:116, and 38:73–74. Its recurrence indicates its centrality: the Qur’an treats the refusal of Iblīs as the archetypal sin, the pattern from which all others derive.

The prostration is understood by the commentators as an act of honor commanded by God, not an act of worship directed to Adam — God is the one who commanded it, and obedience to the command is the worship. Ibn Kathīr states this explicitly, noting that the angels’ prostration was obedience to God and honor to Adam.

**The Superiority It Establishes**

The commentators connect the prostration to Adam’s knowledge, established at 2:31–33: *“And He taught Adam the names, all of them. Then He presented them to the angels and said: ‘Inform Me of the names of these, if you are truthful.’ They said: ‘Glory be to Thee! We have no knowledge save what Thou hast taught us.’”* The one who was taught the names is honored before those who were not. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr draw the conclusion: the spiritually perfected human being, particularly in respect of knowledge, holds a rank above the angels.

This is a remarkable claim in the hierarchy of beings, and it is the ground of the Islamic understanding of the human being’s dignity — which verse 70 of this sūrah will state directly.

**“Shall I Prostrate Before One Thou Hast Created of Clay?”**

*Qāla a-asjudu li-man khalaqta ṭīnā* — the interrogative is not a request for information but an expression of refusal: *am I supposed to prostrate to this?* The verse records the reasoning, and the reasoning is the point.

At 7:12 and 38:76 the reasoning is made explicit: *“I am better than him; Thou createdst me of fire and createdst him of clay.”* Iblīs’s argument is a syllogism: fire is nobler than clay; I am of fire; he is of clay; therefore I am nobler than he, and the nobler does not bow to the less noble.

**The Flaw in the First Sin**

The classical commentators analyze the syllogism with care, because identifying its flaw identifies the structure of every sin that follows. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī note several errors at once.

The first is the *premise*: that fire is nobler than clay is an assertion, not a demonstration. Clay is the source of growth, stability, and nourishment; fire is destructive and unstable. The claim rests on the felt superiority of the luminous over the dense — a preference, dressed as a fact.

The second is the *standard*: Iblīs measures nobility by origin of substance, while God had measured it by the capacity for knowledge. He applies a criterion God did not adopt, and then objects to God’s verdict on the basis of his own criterion.

The third, and most fundamental, is the *refusal of the command*. Even if the premise were true, the prostration was commanded by God. The sin is not the reasoning but the subordination of the command to the reasoning. Al-Rāzī identifies this as the essence of *kibr* — pride: the elevation of one’s own judgment above the revealed command.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ defined pride as he did, in the ḥadīth recorded by Muslim (91):

> *“Pride is rejecting the truth and despising people.”* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 91)

Both halves are present in Iblīs: he rejected the command and despised Adam. The definition is not a moral generality but a description of the first sin.

**The Analogical Reasoning Gone Wrong**

Iblīs’s argument is sometimes described in the Islamic tradition as the first *qiyās* — analogical reasoning — and as an example of analogy set against text. Ibn Taymiyyah discusses the case in this light: reasoning is legitimate, but when it contradicts an explicit command, the command governs. The one who uses his reason to *escape* an obligation rather than to understand it has inverted the purpose of reason.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:62

> **He said, “Dost Thou see this, which Thou hast honored above me? If Thou dost grant me reprieve till the Day of Resurrection, I shall surely gain mastery over his progeny, all save a few.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address and Its Audacity**

*Qāla a-raʾaytaka hādhā alladhī karramta ʿalayya* — “Have You seen this one whom You have honored above me?” The construction *a-raʾaytaka* is an idiom of challenge: *tell me about this*, *do You really consider this?* The commentators note the extraordinary character of the speech: Iblīs is disputing God’s judgment to God’s face, and disputing it *after* being commanded.

The verb *karramta* — “You have honored” — is the same root as *karramnā* in verse 70, *“We have honored the Children of Adam.”* The verse thus records the origin of the dispute that verse 70 will settle: the human being’s honor is a divine bestowal, and Iblīs’s objection to it is the first objection to human dignity in the history of creation.

**The Request for Reprieve**

*La-in akhkhartani ilā yawmi al-qiyāmati la-aḥtanikanna dhurriyyatahu illā qalīlā* — the request for delay is granted in the parallel accounts: 7:14–15, *“He said: Thou art of those reprieved. He said: Because Thou hast led me astray, I will surely sit in ambush for them on Thy straight path”*; 15:36–38, *“He said: My Lord, reprieve me until the Day they are resurrected. He said: Thou art of those reprieved, until the Day of the time appointed”*; 38:79–81. Here the granting is implicit.

The reprieve has a fixed limit — *the Day of the time appointed* — which the commentators understand as the Day of Resurrection or the first trumpet-blast. Iblīs is not immortal; he is deferred.

**“I Shall Surely Gain Mastery Over His Progeny”**

The verb *iḥtanaka* is from *ḥ-n-k*, related to the palate; it denotes gaining control over someone, as one who puts a rope in an animal’s mouth and leads it. The image is of domination through the point of appetite and speech. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī explain it as leading them astray.

The vow is stated at 7:16–17 in its fullest form:

> *“Because Thou hast led me astray, I will surely sit in ambush for them on Thy straight path. Then I will come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right hands and from their left hands, and Thou wilt not find most of them grateful.”*

The four directions describe total encirclement — and the stated *objective* is significant: not merely sin, but ingratitude. The goal is to destroy *shukr*, which is why the sūrah opened by naming Noah a *thankful servant*.

**“All Save a Few”**

*Illā qalīlā* — Iblīs himself concedes an exception. The parallel at 15:40 and 38:83 makes the exception explicit and identifies its cause: *“Except Thy servants among them, the sincere (al-mukhlaṣīn).”* The few are not few by chance; they are those whose sincerity places them beyond his reach, as verse 65 will state.

The concession is theologically important: the adversary himself acknowledges that his power has a boundary, and that the boundary is defined by the quality of the servant rather than by his strength.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:63

> **He said, “Go! And whosoever among them should follow thee, surely Hell shall be thy recompense—an ample recompense!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Single Word of Banishment**

*Qāla idhhab* — “Go.” The commentators note the terseness. After the long speech of Iblīs, the divine response is one word. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī observe that the brevity is itself the rebuke: the one who has overstepped is not argued with but dismissed. The parallel formulations are equally short: 7:13, *“Get thee down from it”*; 15:34, *“Get thee out of it”*; 38:77, *“Get thee out of it, for thou art cursed.”*

The dismissal is total in another sense: Iblīs is not destroyed. He is permitted to continue, which is both a mercy withheld and a mercy granted — he asked for reprieve and received it, and the reprieve is the condition of the test.

**“Whosoever Among Them Follows Thee”**

*Fa-man tabiʿaka minhum* — the verb *tabiʿa* means to follow. The verse places the decisive act with the human being: following. Iblīs does not compel; he invites, and the following is the human act for which the human is accountable. This is the Qur’an’s consistent position, stated most plainly by Satan himself on the Day of Judgment at 14:22:

> *“God promised you a true promise, and I promised you and failed you. And I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me. So blame me not, but blame yourselves.”*

The verse is one of the Qur’an’s clearest statements on free will: the adversary disclaims authorship of human sin, and God accepts the disclaimer.

**“Hell Shall Be Thy Recompense — An Ample Recompense”**

*Fa-inna jahannama jazāʾukum jazāʾan mawfūrā* — *mawfūr* means complete, full, abundant; from *w-f-r*, to be plentiful. The word is used elsewhere of provision, as at 17:20’s *ʿaṭāʾ*, and here it is turned into an irony: the followers of Iblīs will receive their fill.

The pronoun is plural — *jazāʾukum*, “your recompense” — addressed to Iblīs and his followers together. Ibn Kathīr notes this: the threat is collective, as at 15:43, *“And truly Hell is the promised place for them all”*; 38:85, *“I will fill Hell with thee and with those who follow thee among them, all together”*; and 32:13. The leader and the led share the destination, which is a claim about the nature of following: to follow someone is to accept where he is going.

**The Word “Jazāʾ”**

*Jazāʾ* means recompense, requital — the same word used of reward. The Qur’an uses one term for both outcomes, which is deliberate: Hell is not an arbitrary infliction but a *settling*, the appropriate return for the choice made. The same logic runs through verse 7 (*“if you do good, you do good for your own souls; and if you do evil, it is against them”*) and verse 82 (*“it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss”*). The punishment is described as what the act *earns*, not as what God *imposes*.

**The Verse’s Function in the Sequence**

Verse 62 records the vow; verse 63 records the answer. The answer does not prevent the vow’s execution — Iblīs will indeed gain followers — but it fixes the outcome. The reader is thereby given the information needed to make the choice: here is the offer, here is the offerer’s destination, and here is the fact that following him means going where he goes. The moral argument is complete before the tactics are described in the next verse.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:64

> **So incite whomsoever thou canst among them with thy voice, and bear down upon them with thy cavalry and thy infantry, and be their partner in wealth and children, and make them promises.” Satan promises them naught but delusion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Permission, Not a Command**

*Wa-stafziz mani staṭaʿta minhum bi-ṣawtika* — “and incite [or: unsettle] whomever you can among them with your voice.” Al-Zamakhsharī is emphatic that this is not a command to Iblīs but a declaration that God will not intervene in the struggle; al-Qurṭubī reads it as a challenge exposing Iblīs’s weakness, since verse 65 immediately limits his reach.

The form *istafziz* is the tenth form of *f-z-z*, to unsettle, to startle, to rouse; the intensive tenth form indicates the attempt to provoke a hasty, destabilized response. This connects directly to verse 11’s diagnosis: man is *hasty*. Iblīs’s method exploits the haste.

Al-Rāzī records the Muʿtazilite explanation of why God permits this at all: the intensification of moral challenge increases the reward available for overcoming it. The test is real only if the temptation is real.

**“With Thy Voice”**

*Bi-ṣawtika* — the commentators offer a range of readings. Al-Qurṭubī and others record the view that it refers to music and singing, and the verse was cited by those who argued for prohibiting music. The exegetes note honestly that the verse does not mention music; the more probable meaning, as al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī state, is the *whisper* — the internal suggestion, the *waswās* described at 7:20, 20:120, and in the whole of Sūrah al-Nās (114:4, *“from the evil of the whisperer who withdraws”*).

The Islamic tradition’s actual position on music is varied, as the commentators acknowledge: some jurists prohibit all musical forms beyond Qur’anic recitation and the call to prayer, while music and musical expressions of piety have always had a place in Islamic culture — beginning with the song *Ṭalaʿa al-badru ʿalaynā*, “The Full Moon Has Risen over Us,” sung at the Prophet’s ﷺ arrival in Madinah and still sung today.

**“Bear Down Upon Them With Thy Cavalry and Thy Infantry”**

*Wa ajlib ʿalayhim bi-khaylika wa rajlika* — *ajlib* means to shout, to drive forward with a cry, to bring up one’s forces. *Khayl* is cavalry, horsemen; *rajl* is foot soldiers. The idiom *bi-khaylika wa rajlika* was proverbial in Arabic for “with all your might,” and the Qur’an uses it here as a military image.

Ibn Kathīr, al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī read the horsemen and footmen as all those — jinn or human — who ride or walk in acts of disobedience. The image is of an army composed of the disobedient themselves, which is a precise account of how corruption spreads: not by an external force but by the accumulated momentum of those already committed. Compare 41:29 and 114:6, which identify the whisperers as being “from among jinn and mankind.”

**“And Be Their Partner in Wealth and Children”**

*Wa shārikhum fī al-amwāli wa-l-awlād* — the verse identifies the two domains in which the partnership occurs, and they are the same two named at verse 6 as the blessings given to Israel, and at 3:14 as the objects of human desire, and at 18:46 as the adornment of this world.

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Jaṣṣāṣ, Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī specify the forms of the partnership. In wealth: acquisition through usury or other illicit means, and expenditure in immoral ways. In children: children conceived through fornication and adultery; the mistreatment, misguidance, or killing of children; and pride in offspring as a mark of status.

The word *sharīk* — partner — is theologically loaded. It is the word used of the partners ascribed to God in shirk. Satan becomes a *partner* not in divinity but in the two things human beings value most, and the partnership consists in his having a hand in how they are obtained and used. The person who acquires wealth through usury has made Satan a partner in his wealth; the person who raises children without guidance has made him a partner in his children.

**“And Make Them Promises”**

*Wa ʿidhum* — the promise is Satan’s principal instrument. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Qurṭubī identify its content: that there will be no Resurrection and no Reckoning; or that the idols will intercede. Compare 4:120, *“He promises them and arouses desires in them, and Satan promises them naught but delusion”*; and 14:22, where Satan himself admits the failure of his promises.

**“Satan Promises Them Naught but Delusion”**

*Wa mā yaʿiduhumu al-shayṭānu illā ghurūrā* — *ghurūr* is deception, the presentation of the false as the attractive; the same root gives *al-gharrūr*, one of Satan’s titles at 31:33 and 35:5, “the Deluder,” and 57:14:

> *“the Deluder deceived you about God.”*

The verse’s closing contrast is with the Qur’an’s repeated assertion that *God’s promise is true*: 4:122, 10:4, 14:22, 18:21, 30:60, 35:5. Two promisers address the human being; one is truthful and one is a deceiver; and the entire moral life consists in determining which promise one is acting on. Every decision about wealth and children — the two domains the verse names — is a wager on one of the two promises.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:65

> **“As for My servants, truly thou hast no authority over them.” And thy Lord suffices as a Guardian.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Limit Declared by God**

*Inna ʿibādī laysa laka ʿalayhim sulṭān* — “My servants — you have no *sulṭān* over them.” *Sulṭān* is authority, warrant, dominion; the same word used at verse 33 of the heir’s authority over the murderer. Iblīs has no warrant, no standing, no enforceable claim over God’s servants.

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī identify *My servants* as the righteous specifically, supported by the parallel verses in which Satan himself makes the exception: 15:40, *“Except Thy servants among them, the sincere”*; and 38:83, *“By Thy Might, I will surely lead them all astray, except Thy servants among them, the sincere.”* The adversary’s own concession establishes the boundary.

**What “No Authority” Means**

Al-Zamakhsharī explains that it means Satan cannot succeed in leading them astray — not that he does not try. He will whisper to the righteous as to everyone; the difference is that the whisper does not become a decision. Compare 15:42: *“Truly My servants — thou hast no authority over them, save those who follow thee among the perverse.”*

Some commentators extend *ʿibādī* to all morally responsible beings and read the verse as a statement of Satan’s ultimate powerlessness over everyone, since the human being alone is responsible for his error. This reading is supported by 14:22, where Satan disclaims authority: *“I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me.”* On this reading the verse is about the *nature* of temptation: it never has authority, only invitation, and the response is always the human being’s.

Both readings are preserved and both are true in their own domain. The righteous are protected in fact; all human beings are free in principle.

**“And Thy Lord Suffices as a Guardian”**

*Wa kafā bi-rabbika wakīlā* — the same *wakīl* as in verses 2 and 54, and the same verb *kafā* as in verses 14 and 17. The formula — *God suffices as guardian* — recurs at 3:173, 4:81, 4:132, 4:171, 33:3, and 33:48. At 3:173 it appears in a specific historical moment: *“Those to whom the people said: ‘The people have gathered against you, so fear them’ — but it increased them in faith, and they said: ‘God suffices us, and how excellent a Guardian (wakīl) is He!’”*

The verse’s function here is to complete the picture the preceding three verses have drawn. Iblīs has been reprieved, has been permitted his tactics, and has been given access to wealth and children. The reader may reasonably ask what protection remains. The answer is one sentence: God suffices as guardian. And the *condition* of that guardianship has been stated: it belongs to *My servants* — the sincere.

**The Practical Doctrine**

The verse establishes that protection from misguidance is available and that it is conditional on servitude. This is not a passive guarantee; the classical spiritual tradition treats it as the outcome of active practice. The means are specified throughout the Qur’an and Sunnah: the recitation of Sūrah al-Nās and al-Falaq, the *istiʿādhdhah* before recitation (16:98), the remembrance of God at the points of entry and exit, and above all sincerity — *ikhlāṣ* — which is the exact quality Satan himself identified as placing human beings beyond his reach.

The Prophet ﷺ taught the specific supplication recorded by Abū Dāwūd (5052) and al-Tirmidhī (3401):

> *“O God, I take refuge in Thee from the withdrawal of Thy blessing, from the change of Thy protection, from the suddenness of Thy wrath, and from all that displeases Thee.”*

The verse’s assurance and the supplication belong together: the guardianship is promised to the servants, and the servant asks to remain one.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:66

> **Your Lord is He Who makes the ships sail upon the sea, that you might seek of His Bounty. Verily, He is Merciful unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ship as a Standing Sign**

*Rabbukumu alladhī yuzjī lakumu al-fulka fī al-baḥri li-tabtaghū min faḍlih* — “Your Lord is He Who drives the ships for you upon the sea, that you may seek of His bounty.” The verb *yuzjī* means to drive along, to urge forward, to propel — the image is of God as the one who moves the vessel.

The ship is among the Qur’an’s most frequently invoked signs: 2:164, 14:32, 16:14, 30:46, 31:31, 35:12, 40:80, 42:32, 43:12, 45:12.

At 55:24 the ships are described as *“like mountains upon the sea”*; at 42:32:

> *“And of His signs are the ships upon the sea, like mountains.”*

**Why a Wooden Object on Water Is a Proof**

The Qur’an’s reasoning is not sentimental but analytical. A ship floats because of a property of water that no human being established; it carries weight because of a relationship between density and displacement that no human being negotiated; it moves because of wind or current that no human being produces. Every element of the arrangement is given. Al-Rāzī develops this: the human contribution is the shaping of the wood; the water’s buoyancy, the wind’s force, and the sea’s bearing capacity are not his.

The Qur’an makes the same argument about the human being’s own dependence at 22:65: *“Hast thou not seen that God has made subservient to you whatever is in the earth, and the ships that run upon the sea by His command?”* The word *bi-amrihi* — by His command — is the operative clause.

**“That You Might Seek of His Bounty”**

*Li-tabtaghū min faḍlihi* — the same phrase used at verse 12 of the day’s purpose. The Qur’an treats trade and travel as religiously legitimate pursuits instituted by divine arrangement. The sea voyage is not a departure from the religious life; it is one of the channels through which God’s *faḍl* — surplus, grace — reaches human beings.

Compare 62:10: *“And when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the land and seek of the bounty of God,”* and 73:20, *“and others traveling in the land, seeking of the bounty of God.”* The Qur’an never frames economic activity as a distraction from worship; it frames it as an occasion for gratitude.

**“Verily, He Is Merciful Unto You”**

*Innahu kāna bikum raḥīmā* — the verse closes with the Name of mercy, and the placement is meaningful. The sea is dangerous; the verse has just described it as a place of peril in the verses that follow. The mercy lies not in the removal of the danger but in the *possibility of the crossing* — the arrangement by which a creature made for land can traverse water and return.

**The Verse’s Function in the Argument**

The sūrah has been arguing against intermediary worship. Verse 66 shifts to a phenomenon every Makkan knew: the caravan to Syria, the ships of the Red Sea, the trade on which Quraysh’s wealth depended. The argument is: you already know who provides. Your livelihood arrives by an arrangement you did not make, through a medium you do not control, to a destination you reach only if the sea permits. The next two verses will draw the conclusion.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:67

> **And whenever affliction befalls you at sea, forgotten are those whom you would call upon, save for Him. Then when He has delivered you safely to land, you turn away. Man is ever ungrateful!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crisis as Disclosure**

*Wa idhā massakumu al-ḍurru fī al-baḥri ḍalla man tadʿūna illā iyyāh* — “and when affliction touches you at sea, *lost* is whoever you call upon, except Him.” The verb *ḍalla* here means to vanish, to be lost, to slip from awareness. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain it: in the moment of danger, the false deities simply disappear from the mind — they seem suddenly useless and contemptible.

This is one of the Qur’an’s most powerful epistemological claims. It asserts that beneath the acquired theology there is an unacquired knowledge, and that danger strips away the acquisition. The idolater does not need to be argued out of idolatry; he needs to be frightened, and he will do it himself.

**The Parallel Passages**

The Qur’an returns to this scene repeatedly, which indicates how much weight it places on the observation. 10:22–23:

> *“He it is Who makes you travel by land and sea, until, when you are in the ships and they run with them with a fair wind and they rejoice therein — a storm wind comes upon them and the wave comes upon them from every side, and they think they are encompassed. They call upon God, sincere to Him in religion: ‘If Thou deliverest us from this, we will surely be among the grateful.’ But when He has delivered them, behold, they act unjustly in the earth without right.”*

And 29:65; 31:32; 6:40–41.

**The Reversion**

*Fa-lammā najjākum ilā al-barri aʿraḍtum* — “but when He delivered you to land, you turned away.” The verb *aʿraḍa* means to turn the face aside, to withdraw — the same verb used at verse 46 of the disbelievers turning from the mention of God. The moment of sincerity lasts exactly as long as the danger.

The Qur’an’s diagnosis of this reversion is not that the person is lying during the crisis but that his sincerity is *situational*. He means it while the waves are high; he does not carry it to shore. The character that survives the crisis is the character that existed before it — the crisis reveals but does not reform.

**“Man Is Ever Ungrateful”**

*Wa kāna al-insānu kafūrā* — *kafūr* is the intensive form of one who covers or denies, from *k-f-r*, the root of *kāfir*. The Qur’an’s use of the same root for both disbelief and ingratitude is not a coincidence of etymology but a statement of identity: to disbelieve is to be ungrateful, because the disbeliever covers over the favors he has received. Compare 14:34, *“And He gave you of all that you asked Him; and if you counted the favor of God, you could not number it. Truly man is unjust, ungrateful (ẓallūm kafār)”*; 22:66; 25:50; 100:6, *“and truly he is severe in the love of good”*; 80:17.

**The Psychological Precision**

The verse describes a mechanism anyone can verify in himself. Gratitude is easy during deliverance and difficult during comfort. The person who has just been saved feels it vividly; the person for whom nothing has gone wrong feels nothing. Safety produces amnesia about the danger, and the amnesia extends to the deliverer. This is why the Qur’an legislates *remembrance* — dhikr, the daily prayers, the fixed times of verse 78 — as an artificial maintenance of a state that would otherwise decay.

**The Application Beyond the Sea**

The sea is the Qur’an’s chosen example because it is the situation of maximum helplessness in the pre-modern world: no technology, no rescue, no escape. The modern equivalents are numerous — the diagnosis in a doctor’s office, the turbulence at thirty thousand feet, the night a child is ill. In each, the person discovers what he actually relies on. The verse’s warning is not about the moment of discovery but about the shore.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:68

> **Do you feel secure that He will not cause the shore to engulf you or unleash a torrent of stones upon you? Then would you find no guardian for yourselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The False Security of the Shore**

*Afa-amintum an yakhsifa bikum jāniba al-barri* — “Do you feel secure that He will not cause a side of the land to swallow you?” The interrogative *a-fa-amintum* — “do you then feel secure?” — targets precisely the state produced by the deliverance described in verse 67. The one who has just been saved to land concludes that the land is safe. The verse says: the danger followed you.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī connect *yakhsifa* to the punishment of Qārūn at 28:81: *“So We caused the earth to swallow him and his house”*; and 34:9. The verb *khasafa* means to cause the ground to collapse and swallow. The one who escaped the sea may be swallowed by the earth.

**“Or Unleash a Torrent of Stones Upon You”**

*Aw yursila ʿalaykum ḥāṣiban* — *ḥāṣib* is a stoning wind, a storm that hurls stones; al-Ṭabarī notes it may also mean a violent destructive wind on land.

The Qur’an uses it of the punishment of Lot’s people at 11:82, 15:74, and 54:34, and the commentators connect it to the stones of Sijjīl sent against the People of the Elephant at 105:1–4:

> *“And He sent upon them birds in flocks, hurling against them stones of baked clay.”*

**“Then You Would Find No Guardian for Yourselves”**

*Thumma lā tajidū lakum wakīlā* — the *wakīl* again: the one to whom affairs are entrusted. The verse’s argument is that in the moment of such a punishment, the person who has taken guardians apart from God will discover that none of them functions. Compare 6:94, *“And you have come to Us alone, as We created you the first time, and you have left behind what We provided for you.”*

**The Rhetorical Method**

The verse is a rhetorical question, and it does not predict that these punishments will occur. It challenges the assumption of security — which is the actual error. The Qur’an’s repeated warnings against false security are among its most consistent themes: 7:97–99, *“Do the people of the towns feel secure that Our might will not come upon them by night while they sleep? ... None feels secure from the plan of God except the losing people”*; 12:107; 16:45–47; 67:16–17.

**The Logical Principle**

The argument is one of *consistency*: if God’s power operated at sea, it operates on land. The person who acknowledges the danger at one location and denies it at another has not reasoned; he has relocated. The verse exposes the relocation.

The practical application is to the general human tendency to treat safety as a *place* rather than a *condition*. People feel secure in their homes, in their countries, in their routines — and the feeling is not based on any analysis of what could happen there. It is based on the absence of recent harm. The verse identifies this as unfounded and asks the reader to substitute reliance on God for reliance on location.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:69

> **Or do you feel secure that He will not cause you to return to it another time, and unleash upon you a tempestuous wind, and drown you for your having been ungrateful? Then you would find no avenger therein against Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Voyage**

*Am amintum an yuʿīdakum fīhi tāratan ukhrā* — “or do you feel secure that He will not return you to it another time?” The verse anticipates the obvious: the traveler will sail again. Trade requires it; livelihood depends on it. And the deliverance of the previous voyage does not guarantee the next.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī read the verse as a statement of continued liability: the person delivered once remains exposed, and his exposure is now aggravated by ingratitude.

**“And Drown You for Your Having Been Ungrateful”**

*Fa-yughriqakum bimā kafartum* — the drowning is explicitly attributed to the ingratitude, not to the weather. The *bi-mā* construction gives the reason. This is the Qur’an’s moral reading of events: the physical cause is real, but the verse locates the operative cause in the moral condition.

The two levels of causation are not in competition in the Qur’anic worldview.

The wind is the wind; the reason it drowns *this* person at *this* time is stated as the ingratitude. Compare 71:25:

> *“Because of their wrongs they were drowned and made to enter a Fire.”*

**“Then You Would Find No Avenger Against Us”**

*Thumma lā tajidū lakum ʿalaynā bihi tabīʿā* — *tabīʿ* is one who pursues a claim, a pursuer of vengeance or restitution; the same root as verse 33’s context of retribution. The verse states that there will be no one to pursue the claim against God.

This is a claim about the finality of divine action. In human affairs, every injury has a claimant — the family, the tribe, the court. The verse asserts that where God acts, there is no such claimant, because there is no party with standing. Compare 4:123, *“And God is not unjust to the servants,”* and 10:44, *“Truly God does not wrong mankind in anything, but mankind wrong themselves.”* The absence of an avenger is not because the action is unjust but because the justice is not in question.

**The Two Verses as a Unit**

Verses 68 and 69 cover the two domains — land and sea — and the two forms of punishment — engulfment and drowning. The coverage is total, which is the point: there is no location in which the person is beyond the reach of the One he has declined to thank. The argument of verses 66–69 is complete: God provides the means of your livelihood (66), you recognize it only in crisis (67), and your security is nowhere (68–69). The natural conclusion, which the verse leaves the reader to draw, is that the acknowledgment which the crisis produces should be the permanent state.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:70

> **We have indeed honored the Children of Adam, and We carry them over land and sea, and provide them with good things, and We have favored them above many We have created.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Human Dignity**

*Wa laqad karramnā banī Ādam* — “and We have certainly honored the Children of Adam.” The emphatic *la-qad* and the verb *karrama* — to honor, to ennoble, to make noble — make this the Qur’an’s most direct statement of human dignity, and it is the verse cited throughout the Islamic tradition whenever the intrinsic worth of the human being is affirmed.

The honor is bestowed on *banī Ādam* — the children of Adam, the species as such. It is not conditioned on faith, on righteousness, on ethnicity, or on capacity. The disbeliever and the believer are equally *Children of Adam*. The classical jurists drew extensive legal consequences from this: the prohibition of mutilation even of an enemy’s body, the requirement of burial, the prohibition of selling a free person, the rules protecting the dignity of the accused.

**What the Honor Consists In**

The commentators enumerate the components. Al-Ṭabarī and others cite the authority given to human beings over other creatures and the subjection of creation to them: 14:33, *“And He made subservient to you the sun and the moon, constant in their courses, and made subservient to you the night and the day”*; 16:12; 45:13. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī add the use of hands and arms in a manner no other animal employs; al-Rāzī adds intellect, discernment, and speech; others add the capacity to know God.

The Qur’an’s own account of Adam’s distinction is threefold and precise. He was created with God’s two Hands: 38:75, *“What prevented thee from prostrating before what I created with My two Hands?”* He was given of God’s Spirit: 15:29, 32:9, 38:72, *“and breathed into him of My Spirit.”* And he was taught the names: 2:31, *“And He taught Adam the names, all of them.”* The three — direct creation, the breathing of spirit, and knowledge — constitute the Qur’anic anthropology.

**“And We Carry Them Over Land and Sea”**

*Wa ḥamalnāhum fī al-barri wa-l-baḥr* — the same verb *ḥamala* used of the passengers of Noah’s Ark in verse 3. The connection is deliberate: the carriage that saved Noah’s progeny is the same carriage that operates daily for all of Adam’s children. Over land by riding animals — 16:8, *“And horses, mules, and donkeys, that you may ride them, and as an adornment”*; 40:79 — and over sea by the ships of verse 66.

**“And Provide Them With Good Things”**

*Wa razaqnāhum mina al-ṭayyibāt* — *ṭayyibāt* means the good, pure, wholesome things.

The Qur’an uses the term for lawful and pleasant provision: 2:168, *“O mankind, eat of what is in the earth, lawful and good (ḥalālan ṭayyibā)”*; 7:157, where the Prophet ﷺ is described as:

> *“making lawful for them the good things (al-ṭayyibāt) and forbidding them the foul.”*

The verse thus includes in the honor the quality of the provision: not mere survival but the wholesome and the pleasant.

**“And We Favored Them Above Many We Have Created”**

*Wa faḍḍalnāhum ʿalā kathīrin mimman khalaqnā tafḍīlā* — the phrase *above many* has been read in two ways. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī record the view that it means above all creation, including the angels, on the basis of the commanded prostration (2:34; 7:11; 17:61; 15:29; 18:50; 20:116; 38:73–74). Others take *many* literally, excluding some categories.

The commentators also distinguish the two verbs: *karramnā* — We honored — may refer to worldly blessings, while *faḍḍalnā* — We favored — refers to spiritual rank or the blessings of the Hereafter.

**The Verse as the Answer to Iblīs**

The verse’s placement immediately after the Iblīs narrative (vv. 61–65) is decisive. Iblīs objected: *shall I prostrate to one You created of clay?* — a refusal of human dignity based on origin. Verse 70 is the divine reply, and it does not argue about clay. It states the honor as a fact of bestowal: *We have honored them.* The dignity is not derived from the material; it is conferred by the Conferrer.

This has a practical consequence that the tradition has drawn repeatedly. Since human dignity is bestowed rather than earned, it cannot be forfeited by failure. The sinner remains a child of Adam; the disbeliever remains honored in his person. The honor that is given cannot be taken back by the recipient’s conduct — only its *fruit* in the Hereafter varies, which is what the following verses address.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:71

> **On the Day We shall call every people by their imam, whosoever is given his book in his right hand, it is they who shall read their book, and they shall not be wronged so much as the thread of a date stone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-Imām: That Which Is Followed**

*Yawma nadʿū kulla unāsin bi-imāmihim* — “the Day We call every people by their *imām*.” The word *imām* derives from *a-m-m*, to intend, to head toward; an *imām* is that which stands in front and is followed. The term is value-neutral in Arabic, and the Qur’an uses it of both the righteous and the wicked.

Of the righteous: 2:124, *“And [remember] when his Lord tried Abraham with words, and he fulfilled them.

He said: ‘I will make thee an imām for mankind’”*; and 21:73, *“And We made them imāms guiding by Our command.”* Of the wicked: 28:41:

> *“And We made them imāms calling to the Fire, and on the Day of Resurrection they will not be helped.”*

**The Five Readings**

Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī record a range of interpretations.
