The *prophet*: each community will be called by the name of its prophet — the community of Moses, the community of Jesus, the community of Muhammad. This is supported by 4:41, *“And how will it be when We bring a witness from every nation, and bring thee as a witness against these?”*

The *revelation*: each community by its Book — the people of the Torah, the people of the Gospel, the people of the Qur’an. Compare 45:28–29.

The *religion or way followed*: each group by the path it took.

The *book of deeds*: al-Qurṭubī notes that *imām* is used at 36:12 of the record — *“and everything We have enumerated in a clear imām”* — and that the verse’s subsequent mention of receiving the book supports this reading. The book is an *imām* because it is consulted as an authoritative register.

The *spiritual leaders or schools followed*: al-Qurṭubī extends it to the scholars and schools of thought a person adhered to.

Al-Rāzī records an inward reading of real interest: that the *imām* is the defining trait of the person — generosity, the desire for knowledge, courage, or hatred, desire, anger — the quality that led him and therefore names him. On this reading, each person is called by the thing he actually followed, which is often not what he claimed to follow.

**The Shīʿite Reading**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record the Shīʿite interpretation, citing the eighth Imām ʿAlī al-Riḍā: *“On the Day of Judgment He will call all people by the imām of their time, the Book of their Lord, and the sunnah of their prophet.”* And from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, that everyone will be called by the Imām of his time, and the Imāms will be called by the Prophet ﷺ. The Twelver tradition holds that the twelfth Imām, in occultation since 329/941, continues to guide and is the *ṣāḥib al-zamān*, the master of the age.

**The Right Hand and the Left**

*Fa-man ūtiya kitābahu bi-yamīnihi fa-ulāʾika yaqraʾūna kitābahum* — the book in the right hand indicates divine contentment; in the left, divine wrath. The scene is developed at 69:19–25 and 84:7–10. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the contrast: those given the book in the right hand read it willingly and with joy, while those given it in the left are so overwhelmed by fear, shame, and regret that they cannot read it.

The reading here connects to verse 14: *“Read your book! On this Day, your soul suffices as a reckoner against you.”* The same command, now differentiated by the outcome: the one whose record is good reads it gladly; the one whose record is evil cannot bear to.

**“They Shall Not Be Wronged the Thread of a Date Stone”**

*Wa lā yuẓlamūna fatīlā* — *fatīl* is the thread in the groove of a date stone, the thin filament; by extension, the smallest imaginable quantity. The Qur’an uses the same idiom at 4:49, *“Do they not see that God does not wrong anyone so much as an atom’s weight (mithqāla dharrah)?”*; and 4:77, 124; 19:60; 21:47.

The verse’s assurance is that the reckoning is exact. Not one filament of good will be lost, and not one filament of evil will be added. This is the Qur’an’s answer to the fear that the accounting might be unfair — the fear that keeps many people from examining their record at all. The precision is the comfort: the one who has done little good will receive that little in full, and the one who fears he has been overcharged has a guarantee against it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:72

> **And whosoever was blind in this [life] will be blind in the Hereafter, and further astray from the way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Continuity of the Condition**

*Wa man kāna fī hādhihi aʿmā fa-huwa fī al-ākhirati aʿmā* — the verse states a continuity between the two states, and the pronoun *hādhihi* — “this” — is read by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī as referring to this world (*al-dunyā*). The one blind here will be blind there.

The claim is not that blindness is *punished* by more blindness as an external penalty, but that the condition *persists* — that it is the same condition, continuing. This is consistent with the sūrah’s repeated theme: the book on the neck (v. 13), the soul as reckoner (v. 14), the self-authored record. Nothing external is imposed; the interior state is made permanent.

**The Blindness Is of the Heart**

The Qur’an is explicit that the blindness in question is not physical. 22:46: *“Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts go blind.”* Compare 2:18, *“Deaf, dumb, blind — so they will not return”*; 2:171; 5:71; 6:50. And the Prophet’s physical blindness is nowhere treated in the Qur’an as a spiritual deficiency — 80:1–10, the opening of Sūrah ʿAbasa, is a rebuke to the Prophet ﷺ for turning from a blind man who came seeking guidance, and it establishes the opposite: physical blindness is no barrier and no disgrace.

**“And Further Astray From the Way”**

*Wa aḍallu sabīlā* — the comparative is significant. The blindness of the Hereafter is not merely equal to that of this world but *worse*, and the straying is *further*. Al-Rāzī explains: in this world, the blind person retains some capacity to recover — the message continues to arrive, the reminder continues to sound, the opportunity to turn remains. In the Hereafter, none of that remains. The blindness is now without remedy, and the straying is without return.

Compare 20:124–26:

> *“And whoever turns away from My remembrance, his shall be a constricted life, and We shall raise him on the Day of Resurrection blind. He will say: ‘My Lord, why hast Thou raised me blind, when I was seeing?’ He will say: ‘Thus — Our signs came to thee and thou didst forget them; and likewise today thou art forgotten.’”*

The exchange in that passage makes the mechanism explicit: the blindness of the Hereafter is the *forgetting* of the signs made permanent.

**The Counter-Image**

The Qur’an supplies the opposite at 50:22: *“Thou wast heedless of this; now We have removed thy covering from thee, and thy sight today is piercing (ḥadīd).”* The covering removed, the sight sharp — for the one who will benefit from seeing. The same removal occurs for the one who will not, and it is worse for him, because he sees what he refused to see while there was time to act.

**The Verse’s Practical Force**

The verse makes a claim about the formation of character that is independently verifiable: habits of inattention become capacities of inattention. The person who has spent decades not looking at a certain fact does not simply decline to look; he has lost the ability. The verse extends this to its final term and states the consequence: what becomes fixed in this life is what one arrives with in the next.

This is why the Qur’an’s spiritual program is largely about *attention* — the fixed prayer times of verse 78, the recitation, the remembrance, the night vigil of verse 79. These are not merely acts of worship; they are the maintenance of the faculty of sight. The verse supplies the reason they are prescribed: the faculty atrophies without use, and the atrophy is not reversible at will.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:73

> **And they were about to tempt thee away from that which We revealed unto thee, that thou mightest falsely ascribe unto Us something other than it, whereupon they would surely have taken thee as a friend.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Attempt and Its Nature**

*Wa in kādū la-yaftinūnaka ʿani alladhī awḥaynā ilayka* — “and they were near to tempting thee away from what We revealed to thee.” The construction *in kādū la-* indicates proximity: they came close to succeeding. The verb *fatana* means to test, to tempt, to seduce, to divert.

The objective is specified: *li-taftariya ʿalaynā ghayrah* — “that thou mightest fabricate against Us something other than it.” The verb *iftarā* means to fabricate, to invent falsely; it is the Qur’an’s standard term for false attribution to God. The temptation was not to abandon the revelation but to *modify* it — to add something to it or subtract from it.

**The Reported Occasions**

Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and others record the context. One report concerns the people of al-Ṭāʾif (Thaqīf), who asked the Prophet ﷺ for a year’s respite to continue worshipping their deity al-Lāt, or for their land to be made inviolable like Makkah, or both. Another report concerns the Quraysh’s request that he approach and touch their idols — in some versions in exchange for permission to touch the Black Stone.

The reports indicate that the Prophet ﷺ was inclined toward some accommodation, attracted by the possibility of a peaceful opening. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī note that the verses document the attempt and its defeat.

The demand made to the Prophet ﷺ is recorded explicitly elsewhere, in terms that leave its character unmistakable:

> *“And when Our clear signs are recited unto them, those who look not for the meeting with Us say: ‘Bring a Qur’an other than this, or change it.’”* (10:15)

**Why the Temptation Was Attractive**

The commentators are careful not to suggest any moral defect in the Prophet ﷺ. The attraction was not to the idols but to the *outcome* — the conversion of a tribe, the end of persecution, the opening of Makkah. The offer was framed as a strategic concession, not as an abandonment of faith. This is what makes the temptation real: it appealed to a legitimate good through an illegitimate means.

The verse’s lesson is therefore about the nature of compromise in matters of revelation. A concession in the *content* of the message, however small and however well-intentioned, would have altered the thing itself. The Qur’an’s position is that the message is not a negotiable instrument; it may be conveyed in different ways, at different times, to different people, but its content is not subject to amendment for the sake of receptivity.

**“Whereupon They Would Surely Have Taken Thee as a Friend”**

*Wa idhan la-ttakhadhūka khalīlā* — *khalīl* is an intimate friend, a bosom companion; the Qur’an uses the term of Abraham at 4:125, *“And God took Abraham as a khalīl.”* The verse identifies the price of the concession: friendship with those who had persecuted the believers. The transaction is named plainly — modify the revelation, and the hostility ends.

The Qur’an’s warning about such friendship is consistent: 3:28, 4:139–140, 5:51, 60:1. The point is not that Muslims may not be on good terms with non-Muslims — 60:8 explicitly commands kindness and justice toward those who do not fight them — but that the *cost* of such friendship must never be paid from the content of the revelation.

**The Verse’s Permanence**

The situation is not historical. Every generation faces its version of the offer: adjust the teaching slightly, and acceptance follows. The verse records that the offer was made to the Prophet ﷺ himself, that it was attractive, and that it was refused. The precedent is the point: if the concession was unavailable to him, it is unavailable to anyone.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:74

> **And had We not made thee firm, thou wouldst certainly have inclined toward them a little.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Firming**

*Wa law lā an thabbatnāka laqad kinta rakinta ilayhim shayʾan qalīlā* — “and had We not made thee firm, thou wouldst have inclined toward them a little.” The verb *thabbata* is the intensive of *th-b-t*, to make firm, to establish, to steady; the Qur’an uses it of God’s strengthening of the believers at 8:11 and of the strengthening of the heart at 11:120.

The verse states a counterfactual: the firmness was God’s act, and without it the inclination would have occurred. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī read this as the clearest statement in the Qur’an of the doctrine of *ʿiṣmah* — the prophets’ divinely guaranteed protection from sin. The protection is not a property of the prophet’s own strength; it is a divine bestowal, and the verse says so explicitly.

**“A Little”**

*Shayʾan qalīlā* — the qualification is significant and the commentators discuss it at length. The inclination would have been slight, not total. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that the phrase indicates the *nearness* of the temptation rather than any actual failing: the verse describes what would have happened, and what happened was that it did not.

Some commentators read *a little* as indicating the degree of inclination that the human heart is always capable of, even in the best of people. On this reading the verse is a statement about the human condition as much as about the Prophet ﷺ: no one is so firm that, absent divine support, he would not incline somewhat toward a plausible accommodation.

**The Prayer the Verse Produced**

Al-Zamakhsharī records that when verse 75 was revealed, the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *“O God! Do not entrust me to my own soul, even for the blink of an eye.”*

This supplication became one of the most cited in the Islamic devotional tradition, precisely because of its origin. The one who was divinely protected asked not to be left to himself for the duration of a blink — which establishes the posture the tradition expects of everyone else.

**Firmness Is Not Self-Generated**

The verse’s teaching for the ordinary believer is precise: firmness is not self-generated. The person who believes his resistance to compromise is a product of his own strength has misidentified its source, and the misidentification is dangerous, because it produces the confidence that precedes the fall. The correct posture is the one the Prophet ﷺ modeled — to ask, continually, not to be left alone with oneself.

This is why the Islamic spiritual tradition treats reliance on one’s own state (*al-rukūn ilā al-nafs*) as a specific spiritual disease, and why the standard remedy is the constant invocation. The verse supplies the theological ground: the firmest of human beings needed firming.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:75

> **Then We would have made thee taste double in life and double in death. Then thou wouldst have found for thyself no helper against Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Double Punishment**

*Idhan la-adhaqnāka ḍiʿfa al-ḥayāti wa ḍiʿfa al-mamāt* — “then We would have made thee taste double in life and double in death.” The verb *adhāqnā* — to make taste — is used in the Qur’an of both blessing and punishment, and its sensory force is deliberate: the punishment is not abstract but experienced.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains that the double punishment in death may refer to the punishment of the grave before the Resurrection and the punishment of the Fire after it. Others read the doubling as the multiplication appropriate to the prophet’s rank: the higher the station, the graver the deviation.

**The Principle of Proportional Responsibility**

The verse states a principle that runs through the Qur’an and Islamic ethics: responsibility is proportional to knowledge and station.

The wives of the Prophet ﷺ were told at 33:30:

> *“O wives of the Prophet, whoever of you commits a manifest indecency, the punishment for her will be doubled. And that is easy for God.”*

And those who lead others astray bear an additional burden: 7:38, 11:19–20, 16:25.

The logic is that the same act committed by a person of greater influence causes greater harm and represents a greater betrayal of trust. The teacher who errs misleads his students; the leader who errs misleads his community. The doubling is not arbitrary; it corresponds to the reach of the act.

**“Thou Wouldst Have Found No Helper Against Us”**

*Thumma lā tajidu laka ʿalaynā naṣīrā* — *naṣīr* is a helper, an ally in conflict. The verse states that in such a situation there would be no ally against God. The formula recalls verses 68 and 69’s *no guardian* and *no avenger*.

**The Counterfactual as Instruction**

The verse is entirely conditional — it describes what *would* have happened, not what happened. The commentators note that its function is didactic: it teaches the community the gravity of altering the revelation by showing what the cost would have been for the one who was most protected.

This is a standard method of Qur’anic instruction: to address the Prophet ﷺ with a warning whose actual referent is the community. 39:65, *“And it was revealed to thee and to those before thee: if thou associatest partners, thy work will surely come to naught, and thou wilt surely be among the losers”* — the Prophet ﷺ never committed shirk, and the warning is for those who might. The method has a specific effect: it removes the excuse of the listener who thinks the standard applies only to others.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:76

> **And they were about to incite thee from the land, in order to expel thee therefrom, whereupon they would not have tarried after thee, save a little—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Plot to Expel**

*Wa in kādū la-yastafizzūnaka mina al-arḍi li-yukhrijūka minhā* — the verb *istafazza* is the same tenth form used of Iblīs’s incitement in verse 64: to unsettle, to startle into movement, to drive out. The Quraysh’s objective was the Prophet’s ﷺ removal from Makkah.

Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify the referents as the Quraysh, whose persecution eventually produced the Hijrah. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note the important distinction: the Quraysh did not *forcibly expel* the Prophet ﷺ; he left by divine command. Had they expelled him by force, the immediate punishment would have followed.

The plot is described in the Qur’an in a verse that names all three of the options the Quraysh considered:

> *“And [remember] when those who disbelieved plotted against thee, to imprison thee, or to kill thee, or to expel thee. And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters.”* (8:30)

**“They Would Not Have Tarried After Thee, Save a Little”**

*Wa idhan lā yalbathūna khilāfaka illā qalīlā* — *khilāfaka* means after thee, behind thee, in thy absence. The verse states that their remaining would have been brief. This is the Qur’an’s law of the destroyed peoples: the expulsion or killing of a prophet is followed by the community’s annihilation. Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, Lot, and Moses all present the same sequence.

**The Alternative Reading**

Some commentators read *li-yastafizzūnaka* as “to kill thee,” in which case the verse refers to the plot on the night of the Hijrah, and *they would not have tarried after thee save a little* means that the Quraysh did not remain in their former power for long — within two years, Badr significantly weakened them. This reading is recorded by al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and others, and it fits the historical outcome.

**The Minority Reading and Its Problem**

A minority view holds that the verse concerns some Jews of Madinah who urged the Prophet ﷺ to leave for Syria, the land of the earlier prophets. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī record this but note the difficulty: the sūrah is Makkan and predates the Madinan period. The objection is textual and is generally accepted.

**Why the Quraysh Were Spared**

The verse contains an implicit mercy, and the commentators draw it out. The Quraysh plotted, persecuted, and drove the Prophet ﷺ out — but they did not *expel* him in the technical sense, and they did not kill him. Because the specific act that triggers immediate destruction did not occur, the respite continued, and many of them lived to embrace Islam — including Abū Sufyān, Hind, and the very men who had plotted at Dār al-Nadwah. The distinction between what they did and what they nearly did had enormous consequences for the history of Islam.

The lesson is that the divine law operates on the act, not on the intention alone — and that the gap between the two is where mercy lives.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:77

> **the wont of those among Our messengers whom We sent before thee. And thou wilt find no change in Our wont.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sunnat Allāh: The Divine Wont**

*Sunnata man qad arsalnā qablaka min rusulinā wa lan tajida li-sunnatinā taḥwīlā* — *sunnah* means a way, a course, an established practice. *Sunnat Allāh* — the wont of God — is a technical Qur’anic term for the consistent manner in which God deals with human communities. It appears at 33:62, 35:43, 40:85, and 48:23, always with the same assertion of invariability.

At 48:23: *“The wont of God which has passed before — and thou wilt find no change in the wont of God.”* At 35:43:

> *“So do they await aught but the wont of the former peoples? And thou wilt find no change in the wont of God, nor any alteration in the wont of God.”*

**The Content of the Wont**

The commentators identify it here as the destruction of communities that expel or kill their messengers. The examples are the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, Lot, and Moses — all of whom are named in the Qur’an, and all of whom met the same end. The wont is not arbitrary wrath; it is a rule that has been applied consistently and can therefore be predicted.

**Why the Invariability Matters**

The assertion of invariability has two functions. The first is warning: those who oppose the messenger can look at the record and know the outcome. The second, less obvious, is *reassurance to the messenger*: the pattern means that his suffering is not evidence of abandonment. Every previous messenger was opposed, and every previous opposition ended the same way. The one who knows the wont does not despair in the middle of it.

This is why the Qur’an so often addresses the Prophet ﷺ with the histories of the earlier prophets — 11:120, *“And all that We relate to thee of the tidings of the messengers is that whereby We make firm thy heart.”* The function of sacred history in the Qur’an is not antiquarian; it is stabilizing.

**The Concept’s Reach**

*Sunnat Allāh* became a foundational concept in Islamic thought precisely because of verses like this one. It asserts that God’s dealings with human beings follow a knowable pattern — that moral causation is real, that consequences are consistent, and that history is not a sequence of accidents. Ibn Khaldūn’s sociology, with its account of the rise and fall of dynasties according to fixed principles, is explicitly built on this Qur’anic concept.

For the ordinary reader the concept is equally useful. It means that the moral order the reader observes — communities that flourish when just and decay when corrupt, individuals who prosper when disciplined and suffer when indulgent — is not a tendency but a law, and laws can be relied upon. The person who acts on the assumption that the pattern will hold for him is acting on knowledge rather than hope.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:78

> **Perform the prayer at the declining of the sun till the darkening of the night. And the recitation at dawn—truly, the recitation at dawn is ever witnessed!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Contains the Five Prayers**

*Aqimi al-ṣalāta li-dulūki al-shamsi ilā ghasaqi al-layli wa qurʾāna al-fajr* — “Establish the prayer from the *dulūk* of the sun until the *ghasaq* of the night, and [establish] the *qurʾān* of the dawn.”

The commentators read this verse, together with 11:114 and 20:130, as containing the Qur’anic basis of the five daily prayers. The Prophet’s ﷺ practice then specified the times and forms precisely.

**Dulūk al-Shams: The Declining of the Sun**

*Dulūk* is from *d-l-k*, to decline, to pass the meridian; the word also carries the sense of rubbing, and some connect it to the rubbing of the eyes at the sun’s decline. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Bayḍāwī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī take it as the moment the sun passes its zenith, which is the time of Ẓuhr. A minority read it as sunset, i.e., Maghrib.

If *dulūk* is the zenith, then the span “from the declining of the sun to the darkening of the night” covers four prayers: Ẓuhr, ʿAṣr, Maghrib, and ʿIshāʾ. The dawn prayer is named separately, giving five.

**Ghasaq al-Layl: The Darkening of the Night**

*Ghasaq* is the onset of darkness, the deepening of night. Al-Qurṭubī notes that in some Sunni schools it may mean the disappearance of the sun; the Jaʿfarī school takes it as the disappearance of the sun’s rays or the beginning of the middle of the night. This difference underlies the Shīʿite practice of combining the two afternoon prayers (Ẓuhr and ʿAṣr) and the two evening prayers (Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ) — three prayer times rather than five — while Sunnis generally observe five distinct times with permission to combine in specific circumstances such as travel.

**Qurʾān al-Fajr: The Recitation of Dawn**

*Qurʾāna al-fajr* — literally “the recitation of the dawn” — is read by al-Bayḍāwī, al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Qurṭubī as the Fajr prayer, whose defining feature is the lengthened recitation. Some specify that it is the Qur’anic recitation within the prayer that is meant.

The choice of the word *qurʾān* rather than *ṣalāh* for this prayer is significant: the dawn prayer is distinguished by its recitation, and the verse names it by its distinguishing feature. The Prophet ﷺ lengthened the recitation in Fajr more than in any other prayer, and the practice is documented in the ḥadīth collections.

**“The Recitation of Dawn Is Ever Witnessed”**

*Inna qurʾāna al-fajri kāna mashhūdā* — *mashhūd* means witnessed, attended. The commentators, citing aḥādīth, explain that the dawn recitation is witnessed by the angels of the night and the angels of the day, who overlap at that hour. Al-Bukhārī (4717) records from Abū Hurayrah:

> *“The angels of the night and the angels of the day gather at the Fajr prayer.”* Then Abū Hurayrah said: “Recite, if you wish: *‘Truly the recitation at dawn is ever witnessed.’*“

The parallel ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī (1341) and Muslim (632) describes the angels ascending and reporting to God what they found the servants doing.

The witnessing has a specific weight: Fajr is the hardest prayer for the one who sleeps, since it requires rising before the day’s obligations begin, when no one is watching and no social pressure operates. The verse’s assurance that it *is* witnessed addresses exactly that difficulty.

**A Note on Chronology**

The commentators note honestly that this sūrah is Makkan, while the detailed ritual form of the prayers was established through Prophetic practice largely in the Madinan period. The verse gives the times; the Sunnah gives the forms. Some Sufi commentaries, recorded by al-Qushayrī, read the verse additionally as an instruction in continuous inward prayer — the “prayer of the heart,” the constant remembrance (*dhikr*) that the Sufi seeks to make uninterrupted.

**Why Prayer Is Timed at All**

The verse’s structure — prayer indexed to celestial motion — is not arbitrary. A devotion tied to the sun’s position cannot be postponed indefinitely, cannot be performed only when convenient, and cannot be compressed into a single weekly observance. It interrupts the day five times, which means it interrupts the day’s preoccupations five times. The verse that follows will add the night, so that no portion of the twenty-four hours is without its claim.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:79

> **And keep vigil in prayer for part of the night, as a supererogatory act for thee. It may be that thy Lord will resurrect thee in a praiseworthy station.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Night Vigil**

*Wa mina al-layli fa-tahajjad bihi nāfilatan laka* — “and from the night, keep vigil with it [the Qur’an] as a *nāfilah* for thee.” The verb *tahajjada* means to keep vigil, to stay awake for prayer — from *h-j-d*, which in one of its Arabic senses denotes sleeping and in its tenth form denotes the casting off of sleep. The *tahajjud* is therefore prayer performed after having slept, in the latter part of the night, as al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī specify.

**“A Supererogatory Act for Thee”**

*Nāfilatan laka* — *nāfilah* is an addition, a supererogatory act beyond what is obligatory. The commentators note the apparent difficulty: the night prayer was *obligatory* for the Prophet ﷺ according to most, on the basis of 73:1–4, *“O thou enwrapped in thy robes, keep vigil in the night, save a little — half of it, or diminish from it a little, or add to it — and recite the Qur’an in measured tones.”*

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī resolve it in two ways: the term is figurative for the Prophet ﷺ, or it indicates that the night prayer is supererogatory for all other Muslims, which is the established ruling (73:20 relaxes the obligation for the community). The verse’s *laka* — “for thee” — is read as singling out the Prophet’s ﷺ specific relationship to the practice.

**The Praiseworthy Station**

*ʿAsā an yabʿathaka rabbuka maqāman maḥmūdā* — “it may be that thy Lord will raise thee to a *maqām maḥmūd*.” The commentators identify the Praiseworthy Station with the Prophet’s ﷺ intercession on the Day of Judgment, and with his being seated near the Throne bearing the Banner of Praise (*liwāʾ al-ḥamd*).

Al-Bukhārī (4718) records the ḥadīth of intercession in which, on the Day of Judgment, humanity goes from prophet to prophet seeking relief, each saying “I am not for this,” until they come to Muhammad ﷺ, who says:

> *“I am for it. Then I will go and prostrate before my Lord, and He will open for me such praises of Him as He has not opened for anyone before me.”* (al-Bukhārī 4718; Muslim 194)

This is the *maqām maḥmūd* — the station of praise — and the tradition connects it to the verse explicitly.

**The Connection Between the Vigil and the Station**

The verse links the night prayer to the exaltation with *ʿasā* — “it may be” — which, as the commentators note at verse 8, indicates near-certainty when used by God. The connection is not mechanical but formative: the one who stands before God in the darkness, alone, without witness, is prepared for the station before God in the light, before all creation. The private discipline produces the public rank.

Al-Qurṭubī states the general principle that all supererogatory acts exist for the expansion, joy, and ennoblement of the soul, and that the night prayer is of particular benefit because it is performed in complete solitude with God.

**The Ḥadīth Literature on the Night Prayer**

The practice is among the most heavily attested in the tradition. Al-Bukhārī (1121) and Muslim (758) record:

> *“The best prayer after the obligatory prayers is the prayer in the middle of the night.”* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1163)

And al-Tirmidhī (3549) records:

> *“Hold to the night prayer, for it was the practice of the righteous before you, and it is a means of nearness to your Lord, an expiation for wrong deeds, a prevention from sin, and a driving away of disease from the body.”* (graded ḥasan)

**The Psychological Function**

The verse’s placement after the command to the five prayers is instructive. The five prayers structure the day around its natural divisions — noon, afternoon, sunset, night, dawn. The night vigil adds a prayer that has no place in the day’s structure at all: it belongs to the hours when nothing else happens, when no one expects anything, when the person is alone with the fact of his existence.

This is why the tradition treats it as the most reliable indicator of sincerity. The five prayers can be performed from habit and social expectation. The night prayer cannot: it costs sleep, it has no audience, and no one will know. The verse attaches to it the highest promise in the sūrah, which is a claim about what private, unobserved devotion is worth.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:80

> **And say, “My Lord! Make me enter in a sincere manner, and make me go forth in a sincere manner, and ordain for me, from Thy Presence, an authority to help me.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Taught Prayer for Every Threshold**

*Wa qul rabbi adkhilnī mudkhala ṣidqin wa akhrijnī mukhraja ṣidqin wa-jʿal lī min ladunka sulṭānan naṣīrā* — the verse supplies a duʿāʾ in the imperative: *say*. The Qur’an frequently teaches the Prophet ﷺ specific words, and the practice of learning Qur’anic supplications verbatim derives from verses of this kind.

**Mudkhal Ṣidq and Makhraj Ṣidq**

*Mudkhal ṣidq* — “an entering of truth”; *makhraj ṣidq* — “a going forth of truth.” The construction is the Arabic idiom in which a noun of place is qualified by an abstract quality: an entering characterized by truthfulness, sincerity, and rightness.

Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read the verse in the context of the Hijrah: the entering is into Madinah, the going forth from Makkah. The connection to verses 76–77 is explicit — the Quraysh were about to expel him, and God commanded the migration before they could carry out the expulsion, thereby averting their destruction.

Al-Ṭabarī and others record a second, more universal reading: the entering is into the grave, and the going forth from it at the Resurrection. A third extends it to every situation the Prophet ﷺ entered or left, including his prophetic mission.

The three readings are not in competition; the duʿāʾ has been recited for all three purposes across the tradition. Its general form — *make my entering true and my leaving true* — is applicable to any threshold: a house, a job, a marriage, a country, a life.

**Why Truth Is Asked for the Entering and the Leaving**

The request is not for safety, wealth, or success but for *ṣidq* — truth, sincerity, rightness. The commentators explain the reasoning: an entering may be corrupted by a false motive (entering a position for status rather than service), and a leaving may be corrupted by a false manner (leaving with resentment, or leaving a duty undone). The duʿāʾ asks that both transitions be internally sound.

This is a prayer of unusual sophistication. Most people pray for favorable outcomes; this prayer asks that whatever the outcome, the *character* of the transition be truthful. The one who enters a position sincerely and leaves it sincerely has not lost anything regardless of what happened in between.

**“An Authority to Help Me, From Thy Presence”**

*Wa-jʿal lī min ladunka sulṭānan naṣīrā* — *sulṭān* is authority, warrant, dominion; *naṣīr* is one who gives victory.

The phrase *min ladunka* — “from Thy presence” — is significant: the authority sought is not acquired by maneuvering but granted from God’s own side. Compare 18:10, the prayer of the Companions of the Cave:

> *“Our Lord, grant us mercy from Thy presence (min ladunnā) and prepare for us right guidance in our affair.”*

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Jaṣṣāṣ read the request as being for the authority to establish the religion and to overcome its opponents — a prayer answered at Madinah, where the Prophet ﷺ acquired the social and political capacity to build a community on Qur’anic principles.

**The Practical Use of the Verse**

The duʿāʾ has entered Muslim practice as the prayer recited at departures and arrivals. Its logic is available to anyone facing a transition: the outcome is largely outside one’s control, but the manner of the entering and the leaving is not. Asking God for a true entry and a true exit is asking for the one thing that, if granted, makes every outcome bearable.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:81

> **Say, “Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Recited at the Fall of the Idols**

*Wa qul jāʾa al-ḥaqqu wa zahaqa al-bāṭil* — “and say: Truth has come, and falsehood has perished.” Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that the Prophet ﷺ recited this verse continuously as he struck the idols around the Kaʿbah on the day Makkah was opened in 8/630. Al-Bukhārī (4287) records the scene: the Prophet ﷺ entered the sanctuary, and there were three hundred and sixty idols; he began striking them with a stick in his hand, saying:

> *“Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing.” And: “Say: Truth has come, and falsehood neither originates nor returns.”* (34:49)

The verse is therefore not only a proposition but a record of an event — and the event is the moment the verse was enacted.

**Zahaqa: The Verb of Perishing**

*Zahaqa* means to perish, to be annihilated, to expire — it is used of a soul departing a body. The Qur’an uses it of the destruction of Pharaoh’s people at 21:95 and of the perishing of the disbelievers at 8:42. The choice of verb is exact: falsehood does not merely retreat; it *dies*. Its existence was never substantive.

**“Truly Falsehood Is Ever Vanishing”**

*Inna al-bāṭila kāna zahūqā* — *zahūq* is the intensive form, denoting a thing characterized by perishing. The word *kāna* makes it a permanent description, not an event. Falsehood is *by nature* perishable; perishing is not something that happens to it but what it is.

The metaphysical claim behind the verse is developed by al-Rāzī and later by the Sufi commentators: *al-Ḥaqq* — the Truth, the Real — is one of the divine Names (22:6, 62; 20:114). Truth is Being; falsehood is the absence or distortion of Being. Whatever is contingent is in the process of vanishing; only the Necessary is permanent. Falsehood therefore has no ontological standing to compete with truth; it is a shadow, and a shadow does not defeat the light that casts it.

Compare 21:18: *“Nay, but We hurl the Truth against falsehood, and it crushes its brain; and behold, it vanishes.”* The image is violent and precise: truth does not argue falsehood into submission; it *strikes* it, and the striking is fatal because falsehood has no substance to absorb the blow.

**The Verse’s Logical Claim**

The verse makes a claim that requires care. It does not say that falsehood never prevails temporarily — the Qur’an documents its temporary prevalence extensively, including the thirteen years of Makkan persecution during which the Prophet ﷺ and his followers were the weaker party. It says that falsehood is *ever vanishing* — that its nature is impermanent, so that its victories are always temporary and its defeats are final.

This is a claim about the structure of reality rather than about any particular moment, and it is the ground of the Qur’anic attitude toward history. The one who believes falsehood is perishable by nature can endure its temporary dominance without concluding that it is real. The one who does not believe this will make terms with it, since he expects it to last.

**The Verse as a Political and Intellectual Formula**

The commentators note that this is among the most quoted verses in the Islamic tradition, cited in both political and intellectual contexts — in declarations of the triumph of Islam, in refutations of false doctrine, and in moments of collective crisis. Its use is not a claim that victory has already occurred but an assertion about the direction of things: whatever the present appearance, this is where the movement is going.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:82

> **And We send down of the Quran that which is a cure and a mercy for the believers. And it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shifāʾ: The Qur’an as Cure**

*Wa nunazzilu mina al-qurʾāni mā huwa shifāʾun wa raḥmatun li-l-muʾminīn* — “and We send down of the Qur’an that which is a *shifāʾ* and a mercy for the believers.” The word *shifāʾ* means healing, cure, remedy.

This is the only verse in the Qur’an that describes the revelation explicitly as a cure, though 10:57 implies it:

> *“O mankind, there has come to you an exhortation from your Lord, and a cure (shifāʾ) for what is in the breasts, and a guidance and a mercy for the believers.”*

The commentators identify the diseases cured. Al-Rāzī and the Sufi exegetes list them: ignorance, doubt, hypocrisy, rancor, hatred, and enmity. These are diseases of the heart in the Qur’anic sense — not emotions but settled conditions of the soul, and each is a genuine affliction that its sufferer recognizes.

Al-Rāzī records the saying: *“Whosoever does not seek a cure from the Qur’an, God will not cure him.”* The statement is transmitted as a report rather than as an authenticated ḥadīth, and the commentators cite it as such; its force lies in its meaning — that the cure requires the patient’s cooperation.

**Why the Qur’an Is Called a Mercy as Well as a Cure**

The two terms are distinct and both are needed. A cure removes a disease; a mercy bestows a good. Al-Rāzī explains that the Qur’an is a mercy insofar as it produces virtue, perfection of character, wisdom, and knowledge — positive attainments, not merely the absence of ailment. A person could be cured of hatred without thereby acquiring love; the mercy supplies the love.

**“It Increases the Wrongdoers in Naught but Loss”**

*Wa lā yazīdu al-ẓālimīna illā khasārā* — the same medicine produces the opposite effect in a different constitution. The verse states this without explanation, and the mechanism is supplied elsewhere in the Qur’an: 2:26, 9:124–25, 5:64, 17:41, 25:60, 35:42, 71:6.

The mechanism is not arbitrary. A cure works on an organism that accepts it; the same agent applied to a hostile organism aggravates the condition. The person who approaches the Qur’an with the intention of finding fault does not receive its content as content; he receives it as material for his existing project, and the project is strengthened. This is a claim about the *reader* as much as about the text — and it explains a phenomenon visible in every age: the same book that transforms one person confirms another in his opposition.

**The Verse’s Requirement of the Reader**

The verse specifies *the believers* as those for whom it is cure and mercy, which means the effect is conditional. The condition is not intellectual capacity but *receptivity* — the disposition to be changed rather than to evaluate. The Qur’an states the requirement at 50:37: *“Truly in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart, or gives ear while he is witnessing.”* The phrase *or gives ear while witnessing* — that is, listening with full presence — identifies the posture in which the cure operates.

**Reading as Cure or as Material**

The verse’s division is visible in contemporary reading. Two people read the same passage: one is asking “what does this require of me?” and the other is asking “what can I say about this?” The first may be changed; the second will not be. Neither is unintelligent, and the difference is not in the text. The verse names the difference and locates it in the reader, which is both a warning and an invitation: the cure is available, and its availability depends on a posture one can adopt.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:83

> **And whenever We bestow a blessing upon man, he turns away and withdraws. And whenever evil befalls him, he is in despair.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Failures in One Verse**

*Wa idhā anʿamnā ʿalā al-insāni aʿraḍa wa naʾā bi-jānibih* — “and when We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws off at his side.” The two verbs describe the same movement: *aʿraḍa* is to turn the face away; *naʾā bi-jānibih* is to turn the side — the posture of one who withdraws his body from a conversation.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain the sequence: the blessing produces not gratitude but withdrawal from the Giver. The person who has what he wants no longer needs to attend to the One who gave it. Compare 41:51, *“And when We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws off; and when evil touches him, he is full of long supplication”*; 11:9–10; 39:49; 41:50.

**“And Whenever Evil Befalls Him, He Is in Despair”**

*Wa idhā massahu al-sharru kāna yaʾūsā* — *yaʾūs* is the intensive form of one who despairs, from *y-ʾ-s*. The verse pairs the two failures precisely: blessing produces withdrawal, and harm produces despair. Neither is the appropriate response.

The Qur’an’s diagnosis of the two errors identifies a single underlying defect: the person relates to circumstances rather than to God. When circumstances are good, he forgets the source; when they are bad, he forgets the source’s power to change them. In both cases God is absent from the equation, which is why the responses are wrong in the same way.

**The Prescribed Responses**

The Qur’an supplies the alternatives.

For blessing: gratitude — 16:53, *“And whatever blessing you have is from God”*; 14:7:

> *“If you are grateful, I will surely increase you.”*

For harm: patience — 2:155–157, *“And give glad tidings to the patient, who, when affliction strikes them, say: ‘Truly we are God’s, and unto Him we return’”*; 31:17; 22:35.

The two prescribed responses are symmetrical and equally demanding. Gratitude in prosperity requires acknowledging dependence at the moment one feels independent; patience in adversity requires maintaining hope at the moment one feels abandoned. Both require holding God present against the evidence of the immediate circumstance, which is why the Qur’an legislates *remembrance* as a practice — the fixed prayers, the daily dhikr — rather than relying on feeling.

**The Verse as Psychology**

The description is exact enough to be testable. Observe a person in sudden prosperity: the first thing to change is usually his availability — he becomes harder to reach, less present to those he needed when he had nothing. Observe a person in sudden adversity: the first thing to change is usually his horizon — the future contracts to the size of the present problem. Both movements are involuntary, which is why they require a counter-practice rather than a resolution.

**Its Place in the Sūrah**

The verse follows the description of the Qur’an as cure and mercy. The diseases it names — withdrawal in prosperity and despair in adversity — are precisely among the conditions the cure addresses. The sequence is: here is the medicine (v. 82); here are two of the illnesses (v. 83). And verse 84 will supply the general principle that governs both.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:84

> **Say, “Each acts according to his disposition, and your Lord knows well who is more rightly guided on the way.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shākilah: The Formed Disposition**

*Qul kullun yaʿmalu ʿalā shākilatih* — “Say: Everyone acts according to his *shākilah*.” The word is from *sh-k-l*, meaning form, manner, resemblance; *shākilah* denotes one’s formed character, the distinctive way in which a person is constituted. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī read it as one’s inward nature or inclination; al-Rāzī as one’s spiritual path; others as one’s religion and accepted doctrines. Al-Qurṭubī adds that it may mean one’s intentions.

The commentators converge on a meaning: a person’s action flows from his formed interior. He does not perform discrete acts in isolation; he acts out of a character that has been formed by his beliefs, habits, and choices, and the acts are its expression.

**Why the Verse Is Both a Description and a Warning**

In its historical context the verse was a statement to the disbelievers of Makkah, comparable to 11:121:

> *“And say to those who believe not: Act according to your position; we too are acting.”*

It carries the tone of a final separation: go your way; we go ours; the outcome will disclose which was right.

But the verse also states a general truth with a practical edge. If each acts according to his formed disposition, then the disposition is what must be attended to. A person who tries to change his behavior without changing his formation will fail, because the behavior is the symptom. This is why the Qur’an’s program is directed at the heart rather than at the act list — and why verse 82 described the revelation as a *cure* rather than a code.

**“Your Lord Knows Well Who Is More Rightly Guided”**

*Wa rabbukum aʿlamu bi-man huwa ahdā sabīlā* — the verse ends by locating the judgment with God. The comparative *ahdā* — more rightly guided — implies that guidance admits of degrees and that the ranking is not visible from outside.

This has two effects. The first is humility: the person cannot know his own rank relative to another’s, and the one who claims to know has exceeded his knowledge. The second is seriousness: the ranking exists, and it is known, even though it is not published. The verse therefore forbids both self-satisfaction and despair about one’s standing, and replaces both with effort.

**The Verse’s Place After Verse 83**

Verse 83 described two failure modes; verse 84 explains their source. The man who withdraws in prosperity and despairs in adversity is not making isolated errors; he is expressing a *shākilah* — a formed disposition in which circumstances, rather than God, are the reference point. Changing the response requires changing the formation, and the formation is changed by what the sūrah has just prescribed: the Qur’an received as a cure, the prayers established at their times, the vigil kept in the night.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:85

> **They ask thee about the Spirit. Say, “The Spirit is from the Command of my Lord, and you have not been given knowledge, save a little.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question and the Answer’s Brevity**

*Wa yasʾalūnaka ʿani al-rūḥ* — “and they ask thee about the Spirit.” The Qur’an records several occasions on which the Prophet ﷺ was asked about something and the answer given was shorter than the question: about the Hour (7:187), about the mountains (20:105), about the Dhu al-Qarnayn (18:83). This is one of them, and it is the most cited.

*Quli al-rūḥu min amri rabbī* — “Say: the Spirit is of the command of my Lord.” The answer does not describe; it locates. The Spirit belongs to the *amr* — the domain of divine command — and the verse then closes the subject: *wa mā ūtītum mina al-ʿilmi illā qalīlā*, “and you have not been given of knowledge except a little.”

**Why the Question Was Asked**

Al-Ṭabarī and others record that the question came from a group of Madinan Jews or Makkan idolaters, and some reports indicate they posed it as a test: if he answers, he is not a prophet; if he refuses, he is. The answer given — a refusal to describe accompanied by a statement about the limits of human knowledge — is reported to have satisfied neither party’s expectation and to have been recognized by some as itself the mark of prophethood, since a fabricator would have produced a description.

**What Rūḥ Means**

The commentators preserve several readings.

The *life-principle*: al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and others read it as the spirit that is the source of human life, which God breathed into Adam (32:9, *“and fashioned him and breathed into him of His Spirit”*; 38:72). On this reading the Spirit is the source of human life, knowledge, perception, and spiritual capacity — and therefore of moral obligation, since only a being with these capacities can be obligated.

The *Archangel Gabriel*: al-Zamakhsharī records this, supported by 16:102, *“Say: the Holy Spirit (rūḥ al-qudus) has brought it down from thy Lord in truth,”* and 2:87 and 2:253, where Jesus is strengthened by the Holy Spirit.

The *Qur’an*: another recorded opinion, on the basis of 40:15:

> *“He casts the Spirit of His command upon whom He wills of His servants.”*

Al-Ṭabarī records from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib a tradition describing the Spirit as an angel with seventy thousand heads, each with seventy thousand tongues praising God — a tradition of the kind the exegetes transmit without endorsing as definitive.

**ʿĀlam al-Amr: The Realm of Command**

The verse became the scriptural basis for a distinction that shaped much of Islamic philosophy and Sufism: the division between *ʿālam al-khalq* — the realm of creation, which unfolds through time, causes, and measurable processes — and *ʿālam al-amr*, the realm of command, which is immediate and beyond such processes. The verse’s phrase *min amri rabbī* is the anchor, and it is joined to 42:52: *“And thus We revealed to thee a Spirit of Our command”*; 16:2; 97:4, *“The angels and the Spirit descend therein by the leave of their Lord, with every command.”*

The distinction has a specific function: it marks the boundary of what can be investigated. The realm of creation is open to examination; the realm of command is not, because its mode is not that of created causation.

**“You Have Not Been Given of Knowledge Except a Little”**

This clause is one of the Qur’an’s most important epistemological statements. It is not a discouragement from seeking knowledge — the Qur’an commands it repeatedly — but a calibration of what has been attained. The knowledge given to human beings is *qalīl*: little. Not worthless, not false, but limited in scope, and limited in a way that is not remediable by more effort within the same domain.

The verse’s placement of this statement immediately after the question about the Spirit makes its function clear: there are matters on which the correct answer is that the question exceeds the asker’s capacity, and knowing which matters those are is itself knowledge. The one who cannot distinguish the investigable from the non-investigable will waste his capacity on the second and neglect the first.

**The Discipline of Saying “I Do Not Know”**

The verse establishes a rule for speech about God: when asked what one does not know, the correct response is to say so, and to say why. The Prophet ﷺ — the one who received revelation — answered a direct question with a statement of limits. The precedent governs the tradition’s entire approach to speculation: it is not forbidden, but it is bounded, and the boundary is stated in this verse.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:86

> **And if We willed, We could take away that which We revealed unto thee. Then thou wouldst not find, for thyself, any guardian against Us,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Possibility of Withdrawal**

*Wa la-in shiʾnā la-nadhhabanna bi-lladhī awḥaynā ilayka* — “and if We willed, We would surely take away what We revealed to thee.” The construction *la-in ... la-* expresses a real possibility with emphatic certainty in the consequence.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī note that although the address is second-person singular — to the Prophet ﷺ — most commentators read it as directed to people generally, or to the Prophet ﷺ and his community together. The final clause of verse 87, however, is widely held to be addressed to the Prophet ﷺ specifically.

**What Removal Would Mean**

Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain the removal as the erasure of the Qur’an from both the memories of human beings and the written copies. The tradition preserves striking statements on this. ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd is reported to have said:

> *“The first thing that will be lost from your religion is trust, and the last that will be lost is prayer, so that people will pray but will have no religion. And you will awaken one day and there will be nothing of the Qur’an with you... It will be taken by night, and people will awake in the morning and all of it will have been lifted from the written copies and removed from the hearts.”* (reported by al-Zamakhsharī)

And ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar is reported to have said: *“The Hour will not come until the Qur’an is returned whence it descended”* — and that when it returns, it will complain that the people recited it but did not act on it (al-Qurṭubī).

**“Then Thou Wouldst Not Find Any Guardian Against Us”**

*Thumma lā tajidu laka bihi ʿalaynā wakīlā* — the *wakīl* again, in the same formula as verses 68, 69, and 75. The point is that once the revelation is withdrawn, no one can restore it. Not the scholars, not the community, not the Prophet ﷺ himself. The Qur’an is not a human possession that can be recovered by human effort; it is a gift that exists only as long as it is given.

**The Theological Point**

The verse makes a claim that the Islamic tradition has taken seriously: the preservation of the Qur’an is a divine act, not a human achievement. Compare 15:9, *“Truly We have sent down the Reminder, and truly We are its Guardian”* — the guardianship is God’s, and verse 86 states the counterfactual: if He did not will its continuance, it would not continue.

This has a practical consequence for the community’s attitude toward the text. Preservation is not a task completed by the compilers of the muṣḥaf; it is an ongoing divine action in which human effort participates. The memorization of the Qur’an by generation after generation is understood in the tradition not as a human accomplishment but as the visible form of the divine guardianship — which is why the verse’s warning is directed at complacency rather than at anxiety.

**The Warning and the Mercy**

Verse 87 supplies the exception, and the commentators read it in two ways: God may choose not to remove it, or He may return it after removing it. Either way the verse establishes that the Qur’an’s presence among human beings is an act of mercy — repeated, moment by moment — and not a permanent endowment.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:87

> **save a mercy from thy Lord. Truly His Bounty toward thee is ever great.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Governs the Warning**

*Illā raḥmatan min rabbik* — “except a mercy from thy Lord.” The exception is attached to the entire preceding statement: the revelation is not taken away because of a mercy. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that the exception may mean either that God wills not to remove it, or that He would restore it after removal.

The theological significance is that mercy, not necessity, is the reason the Qur’an remains. It is not indestructible in itself; it is *kept*. Compare 2:64, 4:83, 4:113, 4:175, 24:10, 14, 20, where mercy and bounty are paired in the same manner.

The Qur’an pairs bounty and mercy in a verse addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in a moment of danger:

> *“And but for the Bounty of God upon thee and His Mercy, a faction of them would have endeavored to lead thee astray; yet they lead none astray but themselves, nor can they harm thee in any way.”* (4:113)

**“Truly His Bounty Toward Thee Is Ever Great”**

*Inna faḍlahu kāna ʿalayka kabīrā* — the address returns to the Prophet ﷺ, and al-Bayḍāwī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī read the *faḍl* — bounty, superabundant gift — as referring to his station of excellence among human beings and to his status as the seal of the prophets (33:40). Compare verse 79’s *maqām maḥmūd*.

The word *kabīr* — great — is the same adjective used at verse 9 of the believers’ reward (*ajran kabīrā*). The parallel is deliberate: the reward promised to the community and the bounty given to its Prophet are described in the same terms.

**The Verse’s Sequence With Verse 85**

Verse 85 told the questioners that they had been given only a little knowledge. Verse 86 warns that even the little can be taken. Verse 87 states that it is not taken, and that the reason is mercy. The three verses together form a complete epistemology: human knowledge is limited; what has been given is held by grace, not by right; and the grace is great.

The practical effect is to produce an attitude toward knowledge that is neither proud nor anxious. Not proud, because the little is little. Not anxious, because the One who gave it is described as bountiful. The attitude the verses cultivate is gratitude — which is, as verse 3 established with Noah, the characteristic of the thankful servant.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:88

> **Say, “Surely if mankind and jinn banded together to bring the like of this Quran, they would not bring the like thereof, even if they supported one another.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge in Its Strongest Form**

*Qul la-ini ijtamaʿati al-insu wa-l-jinnu ʿalā an yaʾtū bi-mithli hādhā al-qurʾāni lā yaʾtūna bi-mithlih* — “Say: If mankind and jinn gathered together to bring the like of this Qur’an, they would not bring the like of it.”

The Qur’an issues the challenge in three escalating forms, and this is the most comprehensive. At 2:23 the challenge is to produce *one sūrah* like it: *“And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, bring one sūrah like it, and call your witnesses apart from God, if you are truthful.”* At 10:38, *“bring a sūrah like it.”* At 11:13, *“bring ten sūrahs like it, invented.”* Here the challenge is to produce the *whole* Qur’an, and the collaborators include the jinn.

**Why the Jinn Are Included**

The inclusion of the jinn is significant and the commentators note it. The jinn, in Qur’anic anthropology, possess intelligence, language, and moral responsibility — 55:15, 72:1–15. They are not merely spirits but beings capable of speech and composition. Their inclusion removes the possible objection that the challenge was limited to human eloquence and could be met by a non-human intelligence.

**The Doctrines of Iʿjāz**

The verse is the scriptural basis of *iʿjāz al-Qurʾān* — the doctrine of the Qur’an’s inimitability — held universally in the Islamic tradition. The commentators enumerate the dimensions in which imitation is held to be impossible.

Al-Zamakhsharī emphasizes *eloquence* (faṣāḥah) and *the beauty of organization and composition* (naẓm) — the specific arrangement of words, which he treats as the locus of the miracle.

Al-Ṭabarī and others emphasize the *knowledge* it contains, the *excellence of its content*, and the *refinement of its expression*. Later scholars, notably al-Bāqillānī in *Iʿjāz al-Qurʾān*, added the *absence of internal inconsistency* — a claim the Qur’an makes for itself at 4:82:

> *“Do they not reflect upon the Qur’an? If it had been from other than God, they would have found in it much inconsistency.”*

Al-Rummānī and al-Khaṭṭābī, writing in the fourth/tenth century, developed the doctrine systematically, and ʿAbd al-Qāhir al-Jurjānī’s theory of *naẓm* — that the miraculous quality lies in the specific syntactic and semantic ordering, not in the vocabulary — became the standard account.

**The Historical Context**

Al-Ṭabarī records that the verse was revealed in response to a request by some Madinan Jews that the Prophet ﷺ produce a new verse of revelation as proof. His response was that they already know the Qur’an is from God, since its teachings accord with the Torah’s. The verse then shifts the ground: the proof is not a new verse but the impossibility of producing this one.

**The Structure of the Argument**

The challenge has a specific logical form that the commentators have analyzed. It is not an assertion that no one has succeeded; it is a prediction that no one will succeed, issued publicly, to a hostile audience with every motive to falsify it, in a culture that prized verbal composition above every other achievement. The Arabs of the seventh century had the motive, the skill, and the opportunity. The challenge has stood for fourteen centuries.

The argument’s force is that the failure to meet it is itself the evidence. A claim that could be falsified by any competent poet, and has not been falsified, carries a specific evidential weight — and the Qur’an’s original audience, who were the best judges of Arabic composition available, recognized it. Al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah’s reported assessment is the tradition’s most cited testimony: he heard the Qur’an and said of it that it was not the speech of mankind, and that it shatters what is below it and rises above it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:89

> **And indeed We have employed every kind of parable for mankind in this Quran. Yet most of mankind refuse aught but disbelief.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Kind of Parable**

*Wa laqad ṣarrafnā li-l-nāsi fī hādhā al-qurʾāni min kulli mathal* — the same verb *ṣarrafa* as in verse 41, indicating deliberate variation. The Qur’an has employed *min kulli mathal* — of every kind of likeness, parable, example.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabarī’s successors explain it as God’s having provided in the Qur’an every indication, proof, and mode of expression human beings need.

Ibn Kathīr states it as every argument, conclusive proof, and explanation required to understand the truth. Compare 18:54, *“And We have employed for mankind in this Qur’an every kind of parable, but man is in most things contentious”*; 30:58; 39:27:

> *“And We have set forth for mankind in this Qur’an every kind of parable, that they may take heed.”*

**The Range of the Qur’an’s Parables**

The commentators note the variety. There are extended allegories, such as the parable of the two gardens at 18:32–43. There are short metaphorical images: 10:24, the life of this world as water that brings forth the earth’s adornment and then the earth becomes dry stubble scattered by the winds; 13:17, the foam that passes away and the metal smelted for adornment; 14:24–26, the good word as a good tree and the evil word as an uprooted tree; 16:75–76 and 16:112; 29:41, the spider’s house; 62:5, the donkey carrying books. And there are symbolic images of great metaphysical depth, of which the Light Verse (24:35) is the most famous.

The range is the point of the verse: no single mode of presentation has been omitted. Argument, narrative, image, law, warning, promise, dialogue, oath — every register is employed, which is why verse 41’s claim of deliberate variation can be made.

**“Yet Most of Mankind Refuse Aught but Disbelief”**

*Fa-abā aktharu al-nāsi illā kafūrā* — the verb *abā* means to refuse, to decline, to be unwilling. It denotes not inability but *refusal*, which is a decisive word. The verse does not say most of mankind *cannot* understand; it says they *refuse*.

The same statement recurs at 11:17, 12:103, and 40:59. The Qur’an returns to it because it is the central puzzle of revelation: the guidance is complete and the response is refusal.

**Why Refusal and Not Ignorance**

The distinction matters. If the problem were ignorance, more information would solve it. The verse, following immediately after the claim that every kind of parable has been provided, excludes that explanation. The information is there. The refusal is elsewhere — in the will, in the attachments, in what accepting the message would cost.

This connects to verse 84’s *shākilah*: each acts according to his formed disposition. The disposition formed around something the Qur’an demands surrendering will refuse the parable however clearly it is stated. The Qur’an’s own diagnosis at 74:49–51 is blunt: *“So what is wrong with them, that they turn away from the admonition — as though they were startled asses fleeing from a lion?”*

**The Verse’s Function**

Placed after the challenge of verse 88, the verse explains why the challenge has not been met. The inimitability of the Qur’an is not a secret that investigation would reveal; it is a fact that a specific disposition refuses to register. The reader is thereby told where to look for the obstacle — not in the text but in himself.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:90

> **And they say, “We shall not believe in you till you make a spring gush forth for us from the earth,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional Refusal**

*Wa qālū lan nuʾmina laka ḥattā tafjura lanā mina al-arḍi yanbūʿā* — “and they said: we will never believe you until you make a spring gush forth for us from the earth.” The particle *lan* expresses a strong negation of the future: *we will not, ever*. The *ḥattā* makes belief conditional on the performance.

The verb *fajjara* is the intensive of *f-j-r*, to cleave, to cause to gush; *yanbūʿ* is a spring, a source of water. In the desert environment of Makkah, a spring is not a convenience but the foundation of settlement — the difference between a caravan stop and a city. The demand is for the transformation of the economic reality of the Hijaz.

**The Historical Setting**

Al-Ṭabarī and others record that the leaders of Quraysh first tried to persuade the Prophet ﷺ to desist from preaching, then attempted to bribe him with wealth, marriage, and status — offers recorded in the biographical sources, most famously in the negotiation with ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah. When that failed, they escalated to demands for signs. The four verses that follow record the full list.

The same conditional demand is recorded elsewhere in the Qur’an, in the plainest terms:

> *“And those who disbelieve say: ‘If only a sign were sent down unto him from his Lord!’”* (13:27)

**The Structure of the Demands**

The demands fall into two categories, which the commentators distinguish. The first is *economic and material*: a spring, a garden of palms and vines with streams, a house of gold. The second is *supernatural and evidential*: the sky falling in pieces, God and the angels appearing, an ascent to heaven with a physical book brought down.

The mixture is revealing. The demanders do not appear to have a coherent criterion of what would convince them; they list whatever would be impressive. This is the signature of a demand not seriously intended — which is why the Qur’an responds as it does at verse 93.

**What the First Demand Reveals**

The request for a spring is diagnostic. The Quraysh’s grievance against the Prophet ﷺ was not primarily theological; it was social and economic. His message threatened the Kaʿbah’s status as a pan-Arab sanctuary, and with it the trade and prestige that status produced. The demand for a spring is, in effect, a demand that he demonstrate the capacity to *provide* — to be a source of worldly benefit comparable to what the existing order delivered.

The Qur’an’s refusal is therefore not a refusal to perform but a refusal of the *terms*. A prophet who produces springs on demand is a functionary of material provision, and the mission is something else. The response at the end of verse 93 — *“Am I aught but a human being, a messenger?”* — is the answer to the entire list.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:91

> **or till you have a garden of date palms and grapevines, and you make streams gush forth in the midst of it,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Escalation to Agricultural Wealth**

*Aw takūna laka jannatun min nakhīlin wa ʿinabin fa-tufajjira al-anhāra khilālahā tafjīrā* — “or that you have a garden of date palms and grapevines, and cause rivers to gush through it, gushing.”

The specification is precise and locally meaningful. Date palms and grapevines were the two most valuable cultivated crops of the Hijaz — the date palm of the oases and the vines of al-Ṭāʾif. A garden of these two, irrigated by flowing streams rather than by drawn water, represented the highest form of agricultural wealth available in that environment.

The cognate adverb *tafjīrā* intensifies the verb: not merely causing streams but causing them to *gush abundantly*.

**Why the Demand Is for Personal Wealth**

Note the construction: *an takūna laka* — “that you have.” The demand is not for a public benefit but for the Prophet’s ﷺ personal enrichment. This exposes the demand’s actual premise: that prophethood is a position from which material advantage flows, and that its authenticity can be verified by the holder’s prosperity.

The premise is the one the Qur’an has already refuted. At 25:7–8 the disbelievers objected: *“What kind of messenger is this, who eats food and walks in the markets? Why was no treasure sent down to him, or an angel come with him?”* And at 26:111 they said of Noah’s followers, *“We will not believe; only the lowest follow you.”* The recurring objection is not that the message is false but that the messenger is not *impressive*.

The Qur’an’s answer is consistent: the messenger’s lack of wealth is not a defect but a sign. 6:50:

> *“Say: I do not say to you that I possess the treasuries of God, nor that I know the Unseen, nor that I am an angel. I only follow what is revealed to me.”*

**The Irony of the Demand**

The demand is ironic in a way the Qur’an exploits. The Garden of the Hereafter is described in exactly these terms — palms, vines, and rivers flowing beneath. 13:35: *“The likeness of the Garden promised to the reverent: beneath it rivers flow.”* 18:32–34 describes the two gardens, one of them of palms and vines with a river running through. The demanders are asking for the promised Garden in this world, in material form, as the price of believing in the Garden.

The refusal is therefore not a denial that such a Garden exists but a refusal to convert it into currency.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:92

> **or till you make the sky fall upon us in pieces, as you have claimed, or you bring God and the angels before us,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Their Own Destruction**

*Aw tusqiṭa al-samāʾa kamā zaʿamta ʿalaynā kisafā* — “or that you cause the sky to fall upon us in pieces, as you claimed.” The reference is to the Qur’an’s warnings of celestial punishment; the same threat appears at 34:9:

> *“If We will, We shall cause the earth to swallow them, or cause a fragment of the sky to fall upon them.”*

The demand is extraordinary: they ask for the punishment they have been warned of, as proof. The Qur’an records the same phenomenon at 8:32: *“And [remember] when they said: ‘O God, if this is the truth from Thee, then rain down upon us stones from the sky, or bring upon us a painful punishment.’”*

Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators note the significance: this is not a request for evidence but an expression of contempt — the posture of one who is certain nothing will happen and wishes to demonstrate the certainty.

**“Or You Bring God and the Angels Before Us”**

*Aw taʾtiya bi-Allāhi wa-l-malāʾikati qabīlā* — *qabīlā* means face to face, in direct confrontation, or as a guarantor. The demand is for the direct vision of God and the assembled angels.

Al-Ṭabarī notes the parallel with the Israelites’ demand at 2:55: *“And [remember] when you said: ‘O Moses, we will not believe thee until we see God plainly!’ — and the thunderbolt seized you while you looked on.”* The precedent is decisive: the demand was made before, it was granted, and the demanders were destroyed.

The Qur’an’s position on seeing God in this world is stated at 7:143, where Moses asks: *“My Lord, show me, that I may look upon Thee.”* The answer: *“Thou shalt not see Me (lan tarānī), but look upon the mountain; if it remains in its place, then thou shalt see Me.”* And when the Lord manifested to the mountain, it crumbled to dust and Moses fell in a swoon.

**Why the Demand Cannot Be Met**

The Qur’an’s reasoning is not that God is incapable of appearing but that the human constitution cannot receive the manifestation. This is why the Isrāʾ and Miʿrāj — in which the Prophet ﷺ did approach the Divine Presence — is presented as a unique miracle requiring a unique preparation, and why the Qur’an describes the nearness at 53:7–9 rather than narrating a vision.

The demand also confuses the categories. Faith is the recognition of the unseen on the basis of signs; a demand for the seen as the condition of faith abolishes faith rather than establishing it. The Qur’an makes this explicit at 6:111: *“And even if We sent down to them the angels, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered all things before them face to face, they would not believe unless God willed.”* The verse states that the demand, if granted, would not produce the promised belief — which the demanders’ own history confirms.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:93

> **or till you have a house of gold ornament, or you ascend to Heaven. And we shall not believe in your ascension till you bring down unto us a book we can read.” Say, “Glory be to my Lord! Am I aught but a human being, a messenger?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Two Demands**

*Aw yakūna laka baytun min zukhrufin aw tarqā fī al-samāʾ* — “or that you have a house of ornament [gold], or that you ascend into the sky.” *Zukhruf* means gold, or ornament generally; the Qur’an uses it of worldly adornment at 43:33–35, where God says that were it not that mankind would become a single community of disbelief, He would have made the houses of the disbelievers of silver with stairways of gold.

*Au tarqā fī al-samāʾ* — “or that you ascend into the sky.” The verb *raqiya* means to climb, to ascend — the same root as *miʿrāj*, the ascension. The demand, coming in a sūrah that opens with the Night Journey and Ascension, is remarkable: they are demanding the very thing that has occurred, in a public and verifiable form.

**“We Shall Not Believe in Your Ascension Till You Bring Down a Book We Can Read”**

*Wa lan nuʾmina li-ruqiyyika ḥattā tunazzila ʿalaynā kitāban naqraʾuh* — the demand adds a condition to the condition. The same demand for a physical book appears at 6:7: *“And if We sent down to thee a book upon parchment, and they touched it with their hands, those who disbelieve would say: ‘This is nothing but manifest sorcery’”*; and at 6:124; and at 4:153, where the People of the Book ask Moses for a book from heaven.

The verse at 6:7 is the decisive answer, and it should be read alongside this one: *even if the book were delivered physically, they would attribute it to sorcery.* The demand is therefore unfalsifiable by construction — every possible proof has a pre-prepared dismissal. This is what identifies it as insincere.

**The Response: “Glory Be to My Lord!”**

*Qul subḥāna rabbī* — the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to answer with *tasbīḥ*. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī explain the response as an expression of awe at the enormity of what is being asked: that a human being should be able to command the sky, produce gold, or bring God before an audience. The glorification is a refusal framed as reverence — it declines by pointing to the distance between the Creator and the creature.

**“Am I Aught but a Human Being, a Messenger?”**

*Hal anā illā basharun rasūlā* — the answer is a rhetorical question expecting the reply “no.” Two nouns: *bashar* — a human being, a mortal of flesh; *rasūl* — a messenger, one sent with a message. The verse holds both together, and the pairing is the Qur’an’s definitive statement of prophetic nature.

The same formula appears at 18:110: *“Say: I am only a human being like yourselves; it is revealed to me that your God is one God”*; and 41:6: *“Say: I am only a human being like yourselves; it is revealed to me...”* And the earlier messengers gave the same answer: 14:11:

> *“Their messengers said to them: ‘We are nothing but human beings like yourselves, but God bestows favor upon whom He wills of His servants.’”*

**Why This Answer Is Sufficient**

The two terms do specific work. *Bashar* excludes the supernatural powers the demands presuppose: a human being does not cause springs, produce gold, or bring God before an audience. *Rasūl* establishes what he actually is: a conveyor of a message, whose authority is in the message and not in personal capacity.

The answer relocates the entire question. The demanders asked what the messenger can *do*; the answer states what he is *for*. A messenger who performed miracles on demand would be a wonder-worker, and wonder-working is not the criterion of a message’s truth — Pharaoh’s magicians performed wonders. The criterion is the message, and the message is what verse 88 challenged them to imitate and verse 89 declared to be complete.

**The Formula That Governs All Excess**

The response governs the Islamic understanding of prophethood permanently. The Prophet ﷺ is honored, obeyed, and loved — but he is not a source of independent supernatural power, and requests addressed to him as though he were are refused by this verse. The tradition’s consistent position, articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah and others against various forms of excess, rests on this formula: *basharun rasūlā* — a human being, a messenger. Nothing more, and nothing less.

---
## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:94

> **And nothing hindered men from believing when guidance came unto them, save that they said, “Has God sent a human being as a messenger?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Obstacle Identified**

*Wa mā manaʿa al-nāsa an yuʾminū idh jāʾahumu al-hudā illā an qālū a-baʿatha Allāhu basharan rasūlā* — “and nothing prevented people from believing when guidance came to them, except that they said: Has God sent a human being as a messenger?”
