The verse states a causal claim with unusual directness: the *only* thing standing between the Quraysh and belief was this objection. Not the content of the message, not its moral demands, not its description of the Hereafter — but the humanity of the messenger.

**The Recurring Objection**

The Qur’an documents the objection across the entire prophetic history. 14:10–11: *“Their messengers said to them: ‘We are nothing but human beings like yourselves, but God bestows favor upon whom He wills of His servants... Our people said: you are nothing but human beings like us.’”* And 23:24, 33–34, 47; 26:154, 186; 36:15; 54:24; 64:6; 74:25. The objection is not particular to Makkah; it is the standard human response to prophecy.

The parallel expectation of an angel appears at 6:8, 11:12, 15:7, 25:7, 25:21, and 43:53.

**Why the Humanity of the Messenger Is Objected To**

The commentators identify several layers.

Al-Rāzī notes the surface reason: the messenger eats, walks in the markets, has a family, and is known to his neighbors — and familiarity breeds the inability to see rank. 25:7 records the objection precisely:

> *“What kind of messenger is this, who eats food and walks in the markets?”*

There is a deeper layer, which the tradition identifies as envy. 2:90 records the reaction of those who rejected Muhammad ﷺ: *“How wretched is that for which they sold their souls — that they should disbelieve in what God has sent down, resenting that God should send down of His bounty upon whom He wills of His servants.”* The word *baghyan* — resentful envy — names the mechanism. The objection to the messenger’s humanity is often a way of objecting to *which* human being was chosen.

And there is a third layer, which is the one the Qur’an most often addresses: a human messenger can be *imitated*. An angel cannot be followed in his conduct, because his nature differs; a human messenger’s life becomes a model, and a model makes demands. Accepting a human messenger means accepting that his way of eating, speaking, trading, and treating people is a standard one is expected to meet.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:95

> **Say, “Were there angels walking about upon the earth in peace, We would have sent down upon them an angel from Heaven as a messenger.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Principle of Correspondence**

*Qul law kāna fī al-arḍi malāʾikatun yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna la-nazzalnā ʿalayhim mina al-samāʾi malakan rasūlā* — “Say: If there were angels upon the earth, walking about in settled habitation, We would have sent down to them from heaven an angel as a messenger.”

The argument is one of proportion: the messenger corresponds to the community addressed. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī connect it to the Qur’an’s consistent principle that God chooses messengers from among the people to whom they are sent: 3:164, 7:35, 63, 69; 9:128; 10:2; 16:113; 23:32; 38:4; 62:2. Since human beings walk the earth, human messengers are sent to them.

The phrase *yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna* — walking about in settled habitation, at ease — describes the ordinary condition of dwelling, moving, living. A messenger must share that condition to address it.

**The Practical Reason**

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī add the practical dimension: angels are not perceptible to most human beings, and a messenger who cannot be perceived cannot convey.

Compare 6:8–9:

> *“And they say: If only an angel had been sent down to him! But if We sent down an angel, the matter would be decided, and they would be given no respite. And if We had made him an angel, We would have made him in the form of a man, and We would have confused for them what they are confusing.”*

The last clause is decisive: an angel sent as a messenger would have to appear in human form, at which point the objection would recur unchanged. Gabriel appeared to Mary *“in the likeness of a perfect man”* (19:17) precisely because that is the form in which a message can be received.

**The Argument’s Completion**

Verses 94–95 answer the objection completely. If the messenger were an angel, he could not be perceived and could not be followed; if he were an angel in human form, the objection would return; and since human beings are what inhabit the earth, a human messenger is the only coherent possibility. The objection therefore proves the opposite of what it intends: the humanity of the messenger is not a defect but a necessity.

The Qur’an states the same conclusion at 6:9: the confusion the objectors claim to suffer is one they have created, and the arrangement they demand would not resolve it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:96

> **Say, “God suffices as a Witness between you and me. Verily, of His servants He is Aware, Seeing.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Witness Invoked**

*Qul kafā bi-Allāhi shahīdan baynī wa baynakum* — “Say: God suffices as a witness between me and you.” The formula *kafā bi-* — “it suffices” — recurs throughout the sūrah (vv. 14, 17, 25, 65, 96) and expresses the sufficiency of God for a specific function. Here the function is *shahādah*, testimony.

The same invocation appears at 6:19: *“Say: What thing is greatest in testimony? Say: God is a witness between me and you”*; and at 4:79, 4:166, 13:43, 29:52, 46:8, 48:28.

The same invocation appears in another sūrah, with the addition that states the ground of the testimony:

> *“Say: God suffices as a Witness between me and you. He knows what is in the heavens and the earth.”* (29:52)

**Why a Witness Is Needed**

The situation is a dispute in which the two parties disagree about what has been communicated and how it has been received. The Prophet ﷺ says he conveyed; the Quraysh say he fabricated or is deceived. There is no human arbiter — the Quraysh are the judges in their own case. The verse resolves the impasse by invoking a witness who is present to both parties’ interiors.

**“Verily, of His Servants He Is Aware, Seeing”**

*Innahu kāna bi-ʿibādihi khabīran baṣīrā* — the same pair that closed verses 17 and 30. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the double function: consolation for the Prophet ﷺ, who is fully known and therefore fully vindicated; and threat for the disbelievers, who are fully known and therefore fully accountable.

The two functions are inseparable because they are the same fact seen from two sides. That God is aware of His servants is comfort to the one who has been misunderstood and terror to the one who has misrepresented. The verse does not choose between the two readings; it states the fact and leaves each party to locate itself.

**Closing the Debate Before a Witness**

Placed at the end of the exchange about the demands for signs, the verse closes the debate. The demands have been answered (vv. 90–93), the objection to the messenger’s humanity has been answered (vv. 94–95), and now the dispute itself is referred to a Witness. There is nothing further to argue. The Qur’an frequently ends a polemical exchange this way — not by producing a knockout argument but by locating the argument before God.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:97

> **Whomsoever God guides, he is rightly guided; and whomsoever He leads astray, thou wilt find no protectors for them apart from Him. And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces—blind, dumb, and deaf—their refuge shall be Hell. Every time it abates, We shall increase for them a blazing flame.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sovereignty of Guidance**

*Wa man yahdi Allāhu fa-huwa al-muhtad* — “whomsoever God guides, he is the rightly guided.” The construction is definitive: the one God guides *is* guided, without qualification, and no one can undo it. The converse follows: *wa man yuḍlil fa-lan tajida lahum awliyāʾa min dūnihi* — “and whomsoever He leads astray, thou wilt find no protectors for them apart from Him.”

The commentators divide on the interpretation, and the division is one of the oldest in Islamic theology. Some read the verse as indicating that God alone determines guidance and misguidance. Others, following the reading of 7:178, hold that God leads astray only as a consequence of prior disbelief and iniquity. The Qur’an supports the second reading at 61:5: *“And when they turned aside, God turned aside their hearts”*; and at 2:26: *“He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby, and He misleads none but the transgressors.”*

The mainstream Sunni position holds both: God’s guidance and misguidance are real and sovereign, and they are exercised in accordance with His knowledge of the human being’s own choice. The verse’s function here is not to settle the metaphysics but to end the Prophet’s ﷺ anxiety: guidance is not his to produce, and its absence in some is not his failure.

**“Upon Their Faces”**

*Wa naḥshuruhum yawma al-qiyāmati ʿalā wujūhihim* — “and We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces.” The image is of being dragged face-down. Al-Bukhārī (4760) records the exchange it provoked:

> *“A man said: ‘O Messenger of God, how will the disbeliever be gathered upon his face?’ He said: ‘Is the One who made him walk upon his feet in this world not able to make him walk upon his face on the Day of Resurrection?’”*

The answer is an argument from the greater to the lesser: the One who established upright walking can reverse it. The reversal is also symbolically exact — the one who walked arrogantly upon the earth (v. 37) is gathered upon his face.

**“Blind, Dumb, and Deaf”**

*ʿUmyan wa bukman wa ṣumman* — the three faculties of verse 36 — sight, speech, and hearing — are here removed. The Qur’an uses the same triad of those who refuse guidance at 2:18: *“Deaf, dumb, blind — so they will not return”*; and at 2:171; 6:39; 8:22.

The connection to verse 36 is exact and the commentators note it: the faculties that were accountable, and were misused, are withdrawn. The person who would not see, would not listen truthfully, and would not speak truthfully arrives without any of the three.

**“Every Time It Abates, We Increase for Them a Blaze”**

*Kullamā khabat zidnāhum saʿīrā* — *khabat* means to subside, to die down as a fire loses intensity; *saʿīr* is a blazing flame. The verse describes a fire that is continually replenished.

The parallel is 4:56: *“Truly those who disbelieve in Our signs — We shall roast them in a Fire. Every time their skins are burned through, We shall replace them with other skins, that they may taste the punishment.”* The mechanism in both verses is the same: the capacity to suffer is renewed precisely when it is exhausted. The punishment is not a finite quantity to be endured but a condition that does not deplete.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:98

> **That is their recompense for having disbelieved in Our signs. And they say, “What! When we are bones and dust, shall we indeed be resurrected as a new creation?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recompense Stated as Earned**

*Dhālika jazāʾuhum bi-annahum kafarū bi-āyātinā* — “that is their recompense, because they disbelieved in Our signs.” The word *jazāʾ* — recompense — is the same used at verse 63 of Hell as Iblīs’s and his followers’ recompense. The preposition *bi-anna* gives the reason: the punishment is *because of* the disbelief, not in addition to it or independent of it.

This is the Qur’an’s consistent structure of justice, stated at verse 7 and repeated at 10:44, 16:33, 3:117, and 29:40: the punishment is the consequence of the act, not an arbitrary imposition. The verse’s function is to close off the complaint that the punishment is disproportionate — it is measured by the thing that produced it.

The Qur’an states the consequence of disbelieving in the signs in a single verse:

> *“Those are they who disbelieve in the signs of their Lord and in the meeting with Him; so their works have come to naught, and We shall assign no weight to them on the Day of Resurrection.”* (18:105)

**The Return of the Skeptical Question**

*Wa qālū a-idhā kunnā ʿiẓāman wa rafātan a-innā la-mabʿūthūna khalqan jadīdā* — the identical objection from verse 49 returns, word for word. The repetition is significant and the commentators note it. The Qur’an does not repeat because it has run out of material; it repeats because the objection recurs, and because the answer given at verses 50–51 remains the answer.

The recurrence also makes a point about the objectors: they have not advanced. After the argument from stone and iron, after the identification of the Originator, after the discussion of guidance and misguidance, the same question returns in the same words. This is what verse 89 described — *most of mankind refuse aught but disbelief* — and what verse 41 described as being increased in nothing but aversion. The refusal is not an intellectual position that could be updated by evidence; it is a settled state.

**Why the Qur’an Repeats Objections Verbatim**

The practice has a pedagogical function. The reader encounters the objection in its original form and its answer, and then encounters the objection again with the answer already in hand. The second encounter is a test of the reader: will he recognize it, and will he recognize the answer? The Qur’an’s repetitions are exercises in recognition, and they train the reader to identify a recurring argument rather than to treat each appearance as new.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:99

> **Have they not considered that God, Who created the heavens and the earth, has the power to create the like of them? And He has ordained for them a term, about which there is no doubt. Yet the wrongdoers refuse aught but disbelief.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From the Greater Creation**

*Awa lam yaraw anna Allāha alladhī khalaqa al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa qādirun ʿalā an yakhluqa mithlahum* — “Have they not seen that God, Who created the heavens and the earth, is able to create the like of them?” The argument is a standard *a fortiori*: if the greater has been accomplished, the lesser follows.

The Qur’an states the same argument at 40:57:

> *“The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most mankind know not.”*

And at 46:33; 21:30; 29:19; 14:19. The heavens and the earth are, by any measure, greater objects than a human body; the One who made them is not limited by the smaller task.

**“And He Has Ordained for Them a Term”**

*Wa jaʿala lahum ajalan lā rayba fīh* — *ajal* is an appointed term, a fixed deadline. The commentators note that everything with God is according to a measure (13:8; 15:21; 25:2), and therefore every creature has a decreed term — its lifespan and its eventual judgment. Terms are decreed for individuals (6:60; 11:3; 19:75), for communities (7:34), and for the world itself (30:8; 46:3).

The phrase *lā rayba fīh* — “about which there is no doubt” — asserts the certainty of the term. Compare 3:9, 25; 4:87; 6:12; 22:7; 40:59; 45:6.

**The Function of the Term in the Argument**

The mention of the appointed term completes the resurrection argument. The objectors asked “when?” (v. 51). The answer is that a term has been set, that it is certain, and that its exact moment is not disclosed. The combination is precisely calibrated: certainty removes the excuse of doubt, and non-disclosure removes the possibility of postponement. A person who knows the deadline is certain but not the date cannot plan around it.

**“Yet the Wrongdoers Refuse Aught but Disbelief”**

*Fa-abā al-ẓālimūna illā kafūrā* — the same verb *abā* (to refuse) as in verse 89, but now applied to *al-ẓālimūn* — the wrongdoers — rather than to most of mankind. The narrowing is significant: the refusal is not a general human failing but the specific achievement of those who persist.

The verse thus ends the resurrection debate where verse 89 ended the parable debate: not with a further argument but with a diagnosis. The argument has been given in full — from origination, from the greater creation, from the appointed term — and the refusal continues. The Qur’an’s conclusion is that the obstacle is not in the reasoning.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:100

> **Say, “Were you to possess the treasuries of my Lord’s Mercy, you would surely withhold them, out of fear of spending. Man is ever miserly!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hypothetical of Unlimited Wealth**

*Qul law antum tamlikūna khazāʾina raḥmati rabbī idhan la-amsaktum khashyata al-infāq* — “Say: If you possessed the treasuries of my Lord’s mercy, then you would withhold, out of fear of spending.”

The hypothetical is deliberately extreme. The treasuries are God’s — infinite by definition — and the verse supposes them transferred to human ownership. The predicted outcome is not generosity but *imsāk*, withholding, driven by *khashyat al-infāq*, the fear of expenditure.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the treasuries as worldly goods within the divinely apportioned provision (*rizq*); al-Zamakhsharī extends it to all blessings, worldly and other. Either way the claim is the same: infinite resources in human hands would not produce infinite giving.

**Why the Prediction Holds**

Al-Rāzī gives the reasoning, and it is one of the most penetrating psychological observations in the exegetical tradition. Human beings are, as creatures, in a state of *existential poverty* — they do not possess their own existence, let alone their provision. This ontological need produces an ineradicable fear of want. And the fear of want produces miserliness, because the miserly person is not guarding a quantity but guarding against a possibility he cannot exclude.

The reasoning explains a phenomenon everyone has observed: the accumulation of wealth does not reliably reduce the fear of losing it. The person with little fears poverty; the person with much fears poverty differently but not less. If the fear were proportional to the amount, the wealthy would be the most generous; they are frequently the least.

**“Man Is Ever Miserly”**

*Wa kāna al-insānu qatūrā* — *qaṭūr* is the intensive of one who restrains, who holds back; it denotes a settled disposition toward withholding.

The Qur’an states the same at 4:128, *“and souls are prone to avarice (ḥuṣṣat al-anfusu al-shuḥḥ)”*; and at 70:19–21:

> *“Truly man was created anxious — when evil touches him, impatient; and when good touches him, withholding (manūʿā).”*

**The Contrast With God**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī draw the contrast that gives the verse its theological weight: God, who has no need, gives without limit — to the obedient and the disobedient alike. The miserliness is not a property of wealth but of the *needy holder* of wealth. Since only God is free of need (47:38, *“God is the Rich, and you are the poor”*), only God gives unconditionally.

The verse is therefore not merely a criticism of stinginess but a diagnosis of its root. The remedy is not exhortation but the recognition of the actual ownership: the wealth one holds is not one’s own, and the fear of losing what one does not own is a category error. This is the theological ground of zakāt — the compulsory alms — which is not charity but the return of a portion that was never the holder’s.

**The Verse’s Answer to the Demands**

Al-Rāzī notes the verse’s context: it answers the demands of verses 90–93. The Quraysh asked for springs, gardens, and gold. The verse’s answer is that even unlimited provision would not change them, because the defect is not in the amount. This is the same answer given at verse 89 in a different domain: the insufficiency is not in what is offered but in the one receiving it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:101

> **And We indeed gave unto Moses nine clear signs. So ask the Children of Israel. When he came unto them, Pharaoh said, “Truly I think that you, O Moses, are bewitched!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Nine Signs**

*Wa laqad ātaynā Mūsā tisʿa āyātin bayyināt* — “and We gave Moses nine clear signs.” The parallel is 27:12, where Moses is told to cast down his staff and put his hand in his bosom:

> *“among nine signs to Pharaoh and his people.”*

The commentators do not agree on the enumeration, and al-Rāzī explicitly holds that the verse should not be read as limiting Moses to nine signs. Combining the lists given by Ibn Kathīr, al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, the candidates include: the staff becoming a serpent (7:109; 20:19–21; 26:32); the hand becoming white (7:108; 20:22; 27:12); the untying of the knot from his tongue (20:27–28); the plagues of flood, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood (7:133), preceded by drought and scarcity (7:130); the parting of the sea (2:50; 26:63); the destruction of Pharaoh’s wealth in answer to Moses’s prayer (10:88); the streams from the rock (2:60; 7:160); the manna and quails (2:57; 20:80); and the mountain raised over the Israelites at Sinai (2:63; 7:171).

**The Alternative Reading: Nine Commandments**

Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record a ḥadīth identifying the nine signs with nine prohibitions given to Israel: associating partners with God, theft, adultery, murder, sorcery, usury, slandering chaste women, fleeing a just battle, and violating the Sabbath. Al-Tirmidhī (3144) preserves the report from Ṣafwān ibn ʿAssāl, in which a Jew asks the Prophet ﷺ about the nine signs and he enumerates them.

This reading is of considerable interest because it connects the nine signs to the very moral code laid out in verses 22–39 of this sūrah — and the overlap is extensive. The reading makes the verse’s placement meaningful: the sūrah has given its own code, and now points to the code given to Moses.

**“So Ask the Children of Israel”**

*Fa-sʾal banī isrāʾīla idh jāʾahum* — the command to ask is an appeal to a corroborating source available to the audience. The People of the Book possessed the account of Moses; the Qur’an invites verification rather than asserting without evidence. The same appeal appears at 10:94: *“And if thou art in doubt concerning what We sent down to thee, ask those who read the Book before thee.”*

**“Truly I Think That You, O Moses, Are Bewitched”**

*Fa-qāla innī la-aẓunnuka yā Mūsā masḥūrā* — the same accusation recorded against the Prophet ﷺ at verse 47. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note the readings: Pharaoh may be accusing Moses of being a sorcerer (the sense at 10:76; 20:57; 26:34; 27:13), or of being himself deceived by sorcery — misled and confused.

The recurrence is the point. The accusation made in Makkah in the seventh century was made in Egypt centuries earlier, in the same words, by a ruler facing the same demand. The Qur’an’s method is to show that the opposition to prophecy is not a reasoned evaluation but a repeating reflex — which means that its content tells one nothing about the truth of the message, and its recurrence should alert the hearer to its character.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:102

> **He said, “You certainly know that no one has sent these down as clear portents, save the Lord of the heavens and the earth. And truly I think that you, O Pharaoh, are doomed.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses’s Counter-Accusation**

*Qāla laqad ʿalimta mā anzala hāʾulāʾi illā rabbu al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi baṣāʾir* — “He said: You certainly know that none has sent these down as *baṣāʾir* except the Lord of the heavens and the earth.”

The verb *ʿalimta* — “you have known” — is decisive. Moses does not argue that Pharaoh *should* recognize the signs; he states that Pharaoh *does*. The knowledge is present and the denial is against it.

*Baṣāʾir* is the plural of *baṣīrah*, insight, perception, that by which one sees with the inner eye. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the signs are called *baṣāʾir* because they are meant to produce perception. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī gloss them as clear proofs; al-Qurṭubī as clear indications of God’s power and oneness.

**The Qur’an’s Confirmation of Pharaoh’s Inner Knowledge**

The claim Moses makes is confirmed elsewhere. 27:14: *“And they denied them, though their souls were convinced of them (wa staqannathā anfusuhum), wrongfully and arrogantly.”* The phrase is remarkable: the conviction was in the soul, and the denial was in the speech.

This is one of the Qur’an’s most serious claims about disbelief: that it is frequently not ignorance but *suppression*. Al-Rāzī develops the point: it should be obvious to anyone using his intellect that such signs could only be divine, and only willful stubbornness prevents the acknowledgment. The person who denies is not concluding; he is deciding.

**“And Truly I Think That You, O Pharaoh, Are Doomed”**

*Wa innī la-aẓunnuka yā Firʿawnu mathbūrā* — the structure mirrors Pharaoh’s accusation exactly. Pharaoh said *innī la-aẓunnuka yā Mūsā masḥūrā*; Moses replies *wa innī la-aẓunnuka yā Firʿawnu mathbūrā*. The same formula, the same verb of thinking, the same vocative — and opposite contents.

*Mathbūr* means destroyed, ruined; al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī gloss it also as prevented from all goodness, or devoid of reason.

The same word appears at 25:13–14 in the address to the inhabitants of Hell:

> *“Call not this Day for one destruction, but call for many destructions.”*

The exchange is a formal duel of verdicts, and the Qur’an’s narrative will confirm which was correct. The symmetry is rhetorically perfect: each party states what he believes about the other, and the drowning settles it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:103

> **And he desired to incite them from the land; so We drowned him and those with him all together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Verb as the Quraysh’s Plot**

*Fa-arāda an yastafizzahum mina al-arḍ* — “and he desired to *yastafizza* them from the land.” The verb is the same tenth form used at verse 76 of the Quraysh’s plot against the Prophet ﷺ, and at verse 64 of Satan’s incitement. The Qur’an’s use of the identical word binds the three episodes into one pattern: the tyrant’s project, the adversary’s project, and the ancient precedent.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note the range: to remove them by exile or by killing, or to unsettle them by harassment until they leave.

**The Reversal**

*Fa-aghraqnāhu wa man maʿahu jamīʿā* — “so We drowned him and those with him, all together.” The verse is a single sentence of complete reversal: the one who intended to expel was himself destroyed, along with everyone with him.

Al-Rāzī states the principle: *evil plotting besets none but its authors*, citing 35:43, *“And they plotted, and God plotted, and God is the best of plotters”*; and 40:45:

> *“And God guarded him from the evils of what they plotted, and Pharaoh’s people were encompassed by an evil punishment.”*

The drowning is recorded at 2:50, 7:136, 8:54, 10:90, and 43:55. At 10:90 the Qur’an preserves Pharaoh’s final words: *“I believe that there is no god except the One in whom the Children of Israel believe”* — and the answer: *“Now? And thou didst disobey before, and wast among the corrupters?”* The faith arrived at the moment it ceased to be faith, which is the doctrine verse 52 of this sūrah stated.

**The Warning to Quraysh**

The commentators are explicit about the verse’s function in a Makkan sūrah: Pharaoh’s fate is a warning to the Quraysh, who were persecuting the Prophet ﷺ and his followers in the same manner and with the same intention. The parallel is exact — a ruler seeking to expel a messenger — and the parallel’s outcome is what the verse offers.

The verse is also a comfort. The persecuted community in Makkah, watching a power far greater than itself, is shown the precedent: the power that intended expulsion was drowned, and the expelled inherited the land.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:104

> **And We said thereafter unto the Children of Israel, “Dwell in the land. And when the promise of the Hereafter comes to pass, We shall bring you as a mixed assembly.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Dwell**

*Wa qulnā min baʿdihi li-banī isrāʾīla uskunū al-arḍ* — “and We said after it to the Children of Israel: dwell in the land.” The commentators identify the land variously: parts of Egypt now emptied of Pharaoh’s people (al-Zamakhsharī), Palestine/Canaan (al-Ṭabarī), or both (al-Qurṭubī).

Compare 7:137:

> *“And We made the people who were oppressed to inherit the eastern and western parts of the land which We had blessed.”*

The command is a reversal of the expulsion Pharaoh intended. He wanted them out; they are told to dwell. The verse thus completes the reversal begun in verse 103.

**The Glad Tiding for the Prophet ﷺ**

Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as containing a promise to the Prophet ﷺ of the future conquest of Makkah. The reasoning is typological: as the oppressed community inherited the land after the destruction of the oppressor, so the Muslim community will inherit Makkah. The reading is supported by the verse’s placement in a Makkan sūrah addressed to a persecuted community, and it corresponds to what occurred in 8/630.

**“We Shall Bring You as a Mixed Assembly”**

*Fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu al-ākhirati jiʾnā bikum lafīfā* — *lafīf* means a mixed multitude, an assembly gathered from various sources. Al-Rāzī explains it as the gathering of all types among them — noble and base, obedient and disobedient, pious and iniquitous, strong and weak. Ibn Kathīr adds the reading that God will bring them together with their enemies for judgment.

**Why the Mixture Matters**

The phrase connects this verse to the Israelite cycle at the beginning of the sūrah. Verses 2–8 described two episodes of corruption and two devastations, with mercy in between. The *lafīf* is the final form of that history: the community will not be gathered as a single moral unit but as a mixture, and the judgment will be individual.

This is the same doctrine stated at verse 15 — *“no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another”* — and at verse 71, where every people is called by its imām and every individual reads his own book. The communal history does not determine the individual fate; the community arrives mixed, and each is separated.

The verse thus closes the sūrah’s treatment of Israel on a note of individual accountability, which is the sūrah’s persistent theme from verse 13 onward: the omen fastened to each neck, the book read by each soul, the reckoning borne by no one else.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:105

> **In truth We sent it down, and in truth it descended. And We sent thee not, save as a bearer of glad tidings and as a warner,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bi-l-Ḥaqq: The Double Assertion**

*Wa bi-l-ḥaqqi anzalnāhu wa bi-l-ḥaqqi nazal* — “and We sent it down in truth, and in truth it descended.” The repetition of *bi-l-ḥaqq* with two different verbs — *anzalnā* (We sent down) and *nazala* (it descended) — is deliberate.

Al-Ṭabarī explains the first as referring to the content: the Qur’an was sent down commanding justice and virtue and forbidding injustice and iniquity. Ibn Kathīr explains it as the impossibility of alteration — it was sent down in truth and therefore cannot be falsified. Al-Rāzī and others read the pair as covering both the origin and the arrival: it came from the Truth and it arrived as the Truth, unchanged in transit.

The word *ḥaqq* carries the full weight of the divine Name *al-Ḥaqq* (22:6, 62; 20:114), which verse 81 invoked:

> *“Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished.”*

The Qur’an’s claim about itself is that it belongs to the category of the Real rather than the contingent — which is why verse 88 can challenge humanity and jinn to produce its like.

**“Save as a Bearer of Glad Tidings and a Warner”**

*Wa mā arsalnāka illā mubashshiran wa nadhīrā* — the two functions are the standard Qur’anic description of the prophetic office. Compare 2:119, *“Truly We sent thee with the truth, as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner”*; 5:19; 25:56; 34:28. And the same description applies to all previous messengers: 2:213, *“And God sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warners”*; 4:165; 6:48; 18:56.

*Mubashshir* is from *b-sh-r*, to bring good news that shows on the face — the same root as *bashar*, human being, which appeared at verse 93. *Nadhīr* is a warner, one who alerts to danger in time to avoid it.

**Why Both Functions Are Necessary**

The pairing is not a stylistic balance but a requirement. A message of glad tidings alone produces presumption; a message of warning alone produces despair, and both states are named as sins (12:87; 15:56). The prophetic office holds them together, and the proportion between them is itself part of the message.

The verse also connects to verse 100 and to the whole preceding debate. The demanders asked for springs and gold — material proofs. The verse states what the messenger actually is: a conveyor of good news and warning. He is not a provider of worldly goods, and the demands were addressed to a function he does not hold.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:106

> **and [We sent it down] as a recitation We have divided in parts, that thou mayest recite it unto men in intervals, and We sent it down in successive revelations.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qurʾānan Farraqnāhu**

*Wa qurʾānan farraqnāhu li-taqraʾahu ʿalā al-nāsi ʿalā mukthin* — “and a Qur’an We have divided, that thou mayest recite it to mankind at intervals.” The verb *farraqnā* is the intensive of *f-r-q*, to separate, to distinguish, to divide; the intensive indicates a thorough, item-by-item division. The noun *mukth* means a pause, a deliberate interval, a lingering.

**The Two Modes of Descent**

Al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Ṭabarī explain the division as the successive, gradual revelation over the twenty-three years of the prophetic mission. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī record the tradition that the Qur’an was sent down as a whole to the lower heaven on the Night of Power (*Laylat al-Qadr* — one of the odd-numbered nights in the last ten days of Ramadan; see Sūrah 97), and then revealed to the Prophet ﷺ gradually. Some add that it was sent down as a whole into the Prophet’s ﷺ heart, on the basis of 26:192–94, and then disclosed to his consciousness over the period of the mission.

A third reading takes the division as the Qur’an’s division into distinct sections — verses and sūrahs of unequal length.

**The Stated Reason: Recitation at Intervals**

The verse gives the purpose explicitly: *that thou mayest recite it to men at intervals*. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī explain the wisdom: gradual revelation allowed the community time to learn, memorize, understand, and implement. A book delivered complete would have been a text to be studied; a book delivered over twenty-three years became a life to be lived.

The parallel verse at 25:32 answers the same objection from the Quraysh and adds a second reason:

> *“And those who disbelieve say: ‘Why was the Qur’an not sent down to him all at once?’ It is so, that We may make firm thy heart thereby. And We have recited it to thee in a measured pace (tartīlā).”*

Two reasons are thus given: the firming of the Prophet’s ﷺ heart, and the measured delivery to the community.

**The Historical Effect**

The gradual revelation had consequences the tradition documents. Laws were revealed in stages — the prohibition of intoxicants unfolded over three revelations, from 2:219’s acknowledgment of harm and benefit, to 4:43’s restriction before prayer, to 5:90–91’s prohibition. This staging is a documented feature of the revelation and reflects a pedagogy: a community’s habits are not reversed by a single command but by a sequence.

The verse’s principle extends to the manner of teaching. The one who delivers everything at once produces information; the one who delivers it at intervals produces transformation. The Qur’an’s own form is an argument for its method.

**“And We Sent It Down in Successive Revelations”**

*Wa nazzalnāhu tanzīlā* — the cognate accusative intensifies: a true, deliberate, staged sending down. The verse closes by affirming that the gradualness is not an accident of circumstance but a divine intention.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:107

> **Say, “Believe in it, or believe not.” Surely those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited unto them, fall down prostrate on their faces.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Indifference of Truth to Acceptance**

*Qul āminū bihi aw lā tuʾminū* — “Say: Believe in it, or do not believe.” Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī explain the import: the belief or disbelief of the Quraysh has no effect on God. Compare 3:97, *“And whoever disbelieves — truly God is independent of all beings”*; 3:176; 11:57; 47:32.

The formula is not a dismissal but a liberation — of the messenger.

Having conveyed, he is instructed to state that the outcome does not depend on him and does not affect the truth of the message. The verse is the rhetorical counterpart to verse 54’s:

> *“We have not sent thee as a guardian over them.”*

**The Contrast Supplied**

*Inna alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma min qablihi idhā yutlā ʿalayhim yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni sujjadā* — “truly those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited to them, fall down upon their chins, prostrating.” The Quraysh’s refusal is set against the response of those already possessed of scriptural knowledge.

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī identify them as the People of the Book, or the believing portion of them. The Qur’an states the same recognition at 2:146: *“Those to whom We gave the Book know it as they know their own sons”*; and 6:20. And at 28:52–53: *“Those to whom We gave the Book before it — they believe in it. And when it is recited to them, they say: ‘We believe in it; truly it is the truth from our Lord.’”*

**The Physical Response**

*Yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni sujjadā* — “they fall upon their chins, prostrating.” The verb *kharra* denotes a sudden, uncontrolled falling; *al-adhqān* is the plural of *dhaqan*, the chin. The image is of a person collapsing forward in prostration, overcome.

Ibn Kathīr explains the occasion: they prostrate in gratitude for having lived to see the prophet described in their scriptures. Al-Zamakhsharī describes the effect of the recitation: it softens their hearts and moistens their faces — that is, brings tears.

The parallel is 5:83: *“And when they hear what was sent down to the Messenger, thou seest their eyes overflow with tears, because of the truth they recognize. They say: ‘Our Lord, we believe; so write us down among the witnesses.’”*

**Why the Verse Contrasts the Two Responses**

The contrast is not merely comparative; it is diagnostic. The Qur’an argues that the correct response to its recitation is visible and physical — prostration, tears, submission — and that its absence indicates a condition of the hearer. 84:20–21 makes the argument explicitly: *“So what ails them, that they believe not, and when the Qur’an is recited to them, they do not prostrate?”*

The verse therefore offers the reader a test he can apply: not “do I understand the arguments?” but “does this move me?” The People of the Book recognized the Qur’an not primarily by argument but by recognition — the sense of encountering something they already knew.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:108

> **And they say, “Glory be to our Lord! The Promise of our Lord is indeed fulfilled.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Subḥāna Rabbinā: The Recognition Formula**

*Wa yaqūlūna subḥāna rabbinā in kāna waʿdu rabbinā la-mafʿūlā* — “and they say: Glory be to our Lord! Truly the promise of our Lord was to be fulfilled.”

The *tasbīḥ* here is not the declarative glorification of verse 43 but the exclamation of one who has just witnessed the confirmation of what he was told. The sequence is: recognition, then prostration (v. 107), then glorification, then the acknowledgment that the promise has been kept.

Al-Zamakhsharī identifies the promise as God’s commitment to send a future prophet — the promise recorded in their own scriptures.

Compare 7:157, where the Prophet ﷺ is described as:

> *“whom they find written with them in the Torah and the Gospel.”*

**“In kāna waʿdu rabbinā la-mafʿūlā”**

The construction *in ... la-* is emphatic: *truly* the promise of our Lord *was* to be fulfilled. *Mafʿūl* — done, enacted, carried out — is the same word used at verses 5 and 7 of the fulfillment of the promise against the Israelites. The Qur’an uses one term for the fulfillment of promises of mercy and promises of punishment, which is a claim about the reliability of divine speech: what God announces, happens, in both directions.

**Testimony From an Uninterested Witness**

The verse supplies testimony from an unexpected quarter. The Quraysh had dismissed the Prophet ﷺ as bewitched, a poet, a man taught by another. The verse brings forward witnesses who possessed prior revelation, who had no reason to flatter him, and who recognized him. Their recognition is offered as evidence precisely because they were not partisans.

This is a standard evidential structure, and the Qur’an uses it repeatedly: 26:197, *“Is it not a sign for them that the scholars of the Children of Israel know it?”* The testimony of the informed outsider carries weight that the testimony of the follower cannot.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:109

> **And they fall down on their faces, weeping, and it increases them in humility.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Repetition of the Prostration**

*Wa yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni yabkūna* — “and they fall upon their chins, weeping.” The verse returns to the image of verse 107 and adds two elements: the weeping and the increase in humility.

Al-Bayḍāwī explains the weeping as arising from being greatly moved by the Qur’an’s teachings; Ibn Kathīr as arising from humility toward God and belief in His Book and His Prophet. The parallel is 19:58, describing the prophets and the progeny of Noah and Abraham: *“When the signs of the Compassionate were recited to them, they fell down prostrate and weeping.”*

**“And It Increases Them in Humility”**

*Wa yazīduhum khushūʿā* — *khushūʿ* is humility, reverence, the inward softening that produces outward stillness. It is the quality the Qur’an attributes to the successful believers at 23:1–2: *“Successful are the believers, who are humble (khāshiʿūn) in their prayer.”*

The verb *yazīduhum* — it increases them — indicates that the humility is not produced from nothing but augmented. The one who already has humility receives more from the recitation; the effect is proportional to the prior state. This is the same principle as verse 82’s *“a cure and a mercy for the believers”* — the Qur’an’s effect depends on the constitution of the hearer.

**The Diagnostic Use of the Verse**

The commentators note that the differing responses to the Qur’an are treated throughout as indicators of inward state. 9:124–25: *“And whenever a sūrah is revealed, some of them say: ‘Which of you has it increased in faith?’ As for those who believe, it increases them in faith... but as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth.”* And 74:49–51; and 17:41.

The verse therefore supplies a self-examination available to any reader. The question is not whether one agrees with the text intellectually but what the text does to one. The one who hears the Qur’an and is unmoved has information about himself that the one who weeps does not — and the information is not favorable.

**Why Weeping Is Treated as Evidence**

The Islamic tradition treats tears in the presence of revelation as a genuine spiritual datum rather than a mere emotional discharge. Al-Bukhārī (715) records the Prophet ﷺ weeping during the recitation of the Qur’an; and al-Tirmidhī (1634) records:

> *“Two eyes the Fire will not touch: an eye that wept from the fear of God, and an eye that kept watch in the path of God.”* (graded ḥasan)

The weeping of verse 109 is thus not a sentimental detail but the observable sign of the humility the recitation produced — and the humility is the thing the recitation was sent to create.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:110

> **Say, “Call upon God, or call upon the Compassionate. Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names. And be not loud in your prayer, nor too quiet therein, but seek a way between.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Occasion: An Objection About Names**

*Quli dʿū Allāha awi dʿū al-Raḥmān* — “Say: Call upon God, or call upon the Compassionate.” Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn Kathīr record the occasion: the Makkan idolaters heard the Prophet ﷺ supplicating “O God, O Compassionate” — or, in another report, “O Compassionate, O Merciful” — and accused him of invoking two deities. The verse was revealed to establish that the Names refer to one God.

The objection was not frivolous from the objectors’ perspective: al-Raḥmān was known to them as a designation used in Yamāmah by the false prophet Musaylimah, and they may have taken it for the name of a distinct deity. The verse answers by declaring the equivalence of the Names.

**Al-Raḥmān as a Name of the Essence**

Al-Raḥmān — the Compassionate, the All-Merciful — is one of the most important divine Names, and the commentators treat it as pertaining to the divine Essence rather than to a specific attribute of action. It occurs as a proper Name throughout Sūrah 19 (19:18, 26, 44, 58, 61) and in the opening formula of every sūrah but one.

The verse’s theological claim is that the multiplicity of Names does not imply a multiplicity of named. *Ayyan mā tadʿū fa-lahu al-asmāʾu al-ḥusnā* — “whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names.” The Names are many; the Named is one.

**Al-Asmāʾ al-Ḥusnā**

The phrase “the Most Beautiful Names” appears at 7:180: *“And to God belong the Most Beautiful Names, so call upon Him by them, and leave those who deviate concerning His Names”*; at 20:8: *“God — there is no god but He; to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names”*; and at 59:24. The tradition enumerates ninety-nine Names, on the basis of the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (2736) and Muslim (2677):

> *“God has ninety-nine Names, one hundred less one; whoever preserves them (aḥṣāhā) will enter the Garden.”*

The science of the Names became a major discipline in Islamic theology and spirituality, with al-Ghazālī’s *al-Maqṣad al-asnā* among its most influential treatments.

**The Instruction on the Voice in Prayer**

*Wa lā tajhar bi-ṣalātika wa lā tukhāfit bihā wa-btaghi bayna dhālika sabīlā* — “and be not loud in thy prayer, nor too quiet in it, but seek a way between.”

Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn Kathīr record the occasion: the Prophet ﷺ was reciting aloud in Makkah, and the idolaters responded by cursing the Qur’an and the One who sent it. The instruction to lower the voice was to prevent the sacred act from provoking blasphemy.

Ibn Kathīr records the other side: not so quiet that those behind the imām cannot hear. And Ibn ʿAbbās’s reading, preserved by Ibn Kathīr, gives the interior reason: not loud out of ostentation, not quiet out of fear of people.

Compare 7:205: *“And remember thy Lord within thyself, humbly and in fear, without loudness of speech, in the mornings and the evenings, and be not of the heedless.”*

**“Seek a Way Between”**

*Wa-btaghi bayna dhālika sabīlā* — *sabīl* is a path; the instruction is to find the middle way. The verse’s closing thus restates the sūrah’s governing principle: *aqwam* — the most upright (v. 9); neither wasteful nor miserly (vv. 26–29); neither loud nor silent. The middle is not a compromise between two errors but the correct position, which happens to lie between them.

Compare 2:143: *“And thus We have made you a middle community (ummatan wasaṭan), that you may be witnesses over mankind.”* The middle is the Qur’an’s characteristic location, and this sūrah — which began by describing the Qur’an as guiding to what is most upright — ends its legislative section by legislating it in the matter of the voice.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:111

> **And say, “Praise be to God, who has no child! He has no partner in sovereignty; nor has He any protector out of lowliness.” And proclaim His Greatness!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Closes the Chapter**

*Wa quli al-ḥamdu li-Allāhi alladhī lam yattakhidh waladan* — “and say: Praise be to God, who has not taken a child.” The sūrah that opened with *subḥān* — glory be to Him — closes with *ḥamd* — praise be to God. The two are the Qur’an’s twin formulae of divine acknowledgment: *tasbīḥ* declaring God free of every deficiency, *ḥamd* attributing every perfection to Him. The sūrah is thus framed by the two acts of speech that constitute worship.

**The Three Negations**

The verse denies three things, and each answers a specific claim current in the sūrah’s context.

*“Who has no child”* — *lam yattakhidh waladan*.

The verb *ittakhadha* means to take, to adopt; the negation is of *taking* a child, which is the specific claim made by the three groups the Qur’an addresses: the Christians concerning Jesus (19:35, *“It is not fitting for the Compassionate to take a son”*); the Jews and Christians claiming to be God’s children (5:18:

> *“And the Jews and the Christians say: ‘We are the children of God and His beloved.’ Say: ‘Then why does He punish you for your sins?’”*

); and the Makkans concerning the angels as daughters (16:57; 37:149–53; 43:16; 52:39) — the belief verse 40 of this sūrah demolished. Sūrah al-Ikhlāṣ states the same in its shortest form: 112:3, *“He begets not, nor is He begotten.”*

*“He has no partner in sovereignty”* — *wa lam yakun lahu sharīkun fī al-mulk*. This is the denial of shirk in its political form: no one shares God’s rule. The sūrah has argued this at verse 42 (the rival gods would seek a way to the Possessor of the Throne) and at verse 56 (those called upon besides Him have no power).

*“Nor has He any protector out of lowliness”* — *wa lam yakun lahu waliyyun mina al-dhull*. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr explain: God neither seeks nor needs anyone’s aid or protection, as a lowly person does. The negation is not of *walī* in general — the Qur’an describes God as the *walī* of the believers (2:257) and describes believers as having God as their *walī* — but of a *walī* arising from *dhull*, weakness. God has friends by His bounty, not protectors by His need.

**The Three Negations as a Summary of Tawḥīd**

The commentators note that the three denials cover the three forms of shirk: shirk in essence (a child, implying a second of the same nature), shirk in sovereignty (a partner in rule), and shirk in need (a protector, implying deficiency). The verse is therefore a complete statement of divine unity in negative form, and it is for this reason that the tradition regards it as one of the most comprehensive verses in the Qur’an.

A ḥadīth recorded by al-Tirmidhī (3401) and others preserves the practice of reciting it, and it has entered Muslim devotion as *āyat al-ʿizz* — the verse of majesty — recited at the conclusion of prayers and gatherings.

**“And Proclaim His Greatness!”**

*Wa kabbirhu takbīrā* — the final command. The verb *kabbir* is the second form of *k-b-r*, to declare great; the cognate adverb *takbīrā* intensifies: proclaim His greatness *repeatedly and fully*.

This is the scriptural anchor of the *takbīr*: *Allāhu akbar* — “God is Great.” The formula’s place in Islamic practice is without parallel: it opens the canonical prayer (*takbīrat al-iḥrām*) and recurs at each transition within it; it opens the call to prayer and is repeated in it; it is recited in the funeral prayer; it is chanted in congregation at both ʿĪd prayers and throughout the days of Dhū al-Ḥijjah; it is uttered at the conclusion of a marriage contract, upon a person’s acceptance of Islam, and at moments of collective joy; and it has historically been the cry of armies and of crowds facing danger.

**Why the Chapter Ends With the Takbīr**

The ending is a deliberate conclusion to a sūrah that has argued at length about God’s transcendence. The arguments have been made — against divine children (v. 40), against rival gods (vv. 42, 56–57), against the objects of worship that themselves supplicate (v. 57). The final verse does not add an argument; it performs the response. Having established that God has no child, no partner, and no need, the only remaining act is to declare Him great.

The *takbīr* is not a description but a declaration, and the distinction matters. “God is great” as a description would imply a comparison — greater than what? — and the comparative form *akbar* is understood in the tradition as absolute: great beyond every comparison, great in a sense that admits no standard. The one who says it is not measuring God against anything; he is removing everything else from consideration.

**The Reader’s Position at the End**

The sūrah began with a journey no one witnessed and ended with a formula every Muslim says daily. Between the two it has passed through the history of Israel, the accountability of the individual soul, the moral code, the arguments against shirk, the demands of the Quraysh, the account of Adam and Iblīs, the establishment of the prayers, and the nature of the Qur’an itself. The final command gathers all of it into a single utterance.

And the utterance is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in the imperative — *say* — which means it is addressed to everyone who recites the sūrah. The reader who has followed the chapter from *Subḥāna* to *takbīrā* has been led, verse by verse, to the point where the only adequate response is the one the verse supplies. The sūrah does not conclude with a conclusion; it concludes with an act — and the act is available to anyone who says the words.

---
